The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to supplement my telegram No. lo7 of
Maroh 25, 1932, reporting the passage of certain financial
measures at the extraordinary session of the Diet, which
olosed yesterday, and the issuance of an Imperial Ordinance
by which the budget for the fiscal year ending March 31,
1932, will be carried over to the fiscal year beginning
April 1, 1932.
Due to the dissolution of the regular session of the
Diet on January 21, l932,* which rendered impossible the
passage
Despatoh No. 471 of January 29, 1932.

were issued for that purpose, namely:
1/.

2/.

3/.

4/.

was authorized, to be raised either by public subscription
or by borrowing from other Government accounts. Yen 7,579,-
5/.
l61 of this amount was passed by the Diet as a supplementary
budget for the fiscal year ending March 31, 1932, and Yen
6/,7/. 59,519,085 as a supplementary budget for the fiscal year be-
ginning April 1, 1932, the former for expenditures arising
from the two expeditions between the time the amounts granted
by Imperial Ordinance were exhausted and the end of the
present fiscal year and the latter for expenses during a
part of the coming fiscal year. The total expenditures,
relating to the two expeditions, approved or passed at the
extraordinary session of the Diet were Yen 137,410,000.
At the Diet session of March 83rd the Minister of Finance
stated that from September 18th to that date the total
amount spent by the Departments for Foreign Affairs and of
the Navy and the Army in connection with the two affairs
had reached approximately Yen 80,000,000, which would in-
dicate that the average monthly expenditure so far has been
about Yen 13,000,000.
Certain other financial measures, not relating to the
Manchurian and Shanghai affairs, were also acted upon at
8/.
the extraordinary session of the Diet.
. The Imperial Ordin-
ance prohibiting the conversion of notes into gold was
given

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3-
9/. given post-facto approval.* A second supplementary budget
for the fiscal year was passed to cover a deficit of Yen
1,422,471 in the pensions account of the Department of Com-
10/. munications. Approval was given to an Imperial Ordinance
limiting to Yen 44,000,000 the amount for redemption of
principal of national loans to be transferred to the sink-
ing fund for the present fiscal year. According to a
statement published recently in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE this
measure was necessary to cover a deficit in the General
Aocounts of the present fiscal year amounting to Yen 64,000,-
000.
The carrying over of the budget for the present fiscal
year to the fiscal year beginning April 1, 1932, is due to
the dissolution of the regular session of the Diet on Janu-
ary 21, 1932, and is in accordance with Article 71 of the
Constitution, which reads: "When the Imperial Diet has not
voted on the Budget, or when the Budget hes not been brought
into actual existence, the Government shall carry out the
Budget of the preceding year."
Aocording to Artiole 14 of the Finance Law:
"The
Ministers of State may not use an amount fixed by the budget
for an objectother than that specified . . ."
As the
budget for the present fiscal year is the smallest in recent
years, having been drawn up by the Minseito in accordance
with its policy of retrenchment, while the preceding Seiyukai
budget** was the largest in Japan's history, it is very doubt-
ful

Maroh 31, 1930, which was also the working
budget for the fiscal year ending Maroh 31,
l9s1, as a result of dissolution of the Diet
in the latter year.

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
ful if the present Government will be satisfied with the
limitations in expenditure imposed by its predecessor.
The present Government may present a supplementary budget
to a special session of the Diet - which is now reported
by the press as probably to be convened in May or June of
this year, - or, without calling a special session, it
may exceed the appropriations of the working budget and
then submit such expenditures to the next regular session
of the Diet (at the end of this year) for approval. In
spite of the law that the amounts of appropriations may
not be altered, the Ministers of State seem to exercise in
practice rather wide discretion in this regard. Under
such ciroumstances a working budget is not a very satis-
factory indication of what the expenditures of the present
Government may be during the approaching fiscal year.
Revenue of the coming fisoal year will undoubtedly
be less than revenue estimated in the enclosed budget.
Figures were published recently in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE
showing the actual revenue of the first nine months of the
fiscal year ending March 31, 1932, and the actual revenue
of the same period of the preceding fiscal year.

orease was as follows:

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
agreed to reduce the expenditures of the budget by Yen
35,000,000, yet, according to the statement in the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE mentioned above, an Imperial Ordinance limiting
the amount to be transferred to the sinking fund was
necessary because of a deficit of Yen 64,O00,000. The
present Seiyukai Government, with its so-called positive
program, can scarcely be expected to make a similar effort.
The press reports that the several Government departments
will probably demand budgetary increases amounting to more
than Yen 200,000,000 to carry out promised positive policies.
Whether or not these reports may be exaggerated, the present
Government shows a distinct tendenoy toward inflation which,
with revenue decreasing, can only be met by resort to tax
increase, which the Seiyukai promised before the recent
general elections not to do, or by loans. A newspaper
report this morning states that acceptance of such demands
would necessitate flotation of loans of over Yen 500,000,000
to meet revenue deficit and that it is doubtful if the
Department of Finance, in view of this, will give in wholly
to the demands of the various Departments.
Further discussion of Government finances will be sent
later.
Respectfully yours,
Sin t. haill

IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 6 (1932)
Official Gazette, January 31, 1932.
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanction the measure for the issue of a loan in order to
cover expenditures of the Manchurian incident under para-
graph 1 of Article 70 of the Constitution, and order the
same to be promulgated.
(IMPERIAL SEAL)
Countersigned by -
Premier Inukai and other Ministers of State.
Imperial Ordinance No. 6
For the purpose of covering expenditures required in
the Manchurian incident, the Government may float a loan
of Yen 20,910,000 or borrow this amount.
If it is necessary to replenish the above amount the
Government may issue another loan or borrow the amount of
this loan.
Supplementary Rule
The present Ordinance shall be put into force from
the day of promulgation.

Deepatoh No. 3
From American Embasay
Tokyo.
IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 14 (1932)
Official Gazette, February 15, 1932.
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanction the measure for the issue of a loan in order
to cover expenditures required in the Manchurian incident
in accordance with paragraph 1 of Article 70 of the Con-
stitution and order the same to be promulgated.
(IMPERIAL SEAL)
Countersigned by -
Premier Inukai and other Ministers of State.
Imperial Ordinance No. 14.
For the purpose of covering expenditures relating to
the Manchurian inoident, the Government may, besides the
amount to be obtained by the flotation of a loan under
Imperial Ordinance No. 6, issued in 1932, float a loan
of Yen 34,000,000 or borrow this amount. If there is a
neoessity of replenishing the above amount, the Government
may issue another loan or borrow the amount of this loan.
Supplementary Rule
The present Ordinance shall be put into foroe from
the day of promulgation.

From Amarican Embamay

DMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 19 (1932)
Official Gazette, March 3, 1932.
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanction the measure relating to the issue of a loan in
order to cover expenditures in the Manohurian incident in
accordance with paragraph 1 of Article 70 of the Constitution
and order the same to be promulgated.
(IMPERIAL SEAL)
Countersigned by -
Premier Inukai and other Ministers of State.
Imperial Ordinance No. 19
For the purpose of covering expenditures required in
the Manchurian incident, the Government may, in addition to
the amount of loans to be issued under Imperial Ordinance
No. 6 and Imperial Ordinance No. 14 in 1932, float a loan
of Yen 15,000,000 or borrow this amount.
If there is a necessity of replenishing the above
loan, the Government may issue another loan or borrow the
amount of this loan.
Supplementary Rule.
The present Ordinance shall be put into force from
the day of promulgation.

LAW PROVIDING FOR THE ISSUE OF A
LOAN IN ORDER TO COVER EXPENDITURES
REQUIRED IN THE MANCHURIAN INCIDENT
(MARCH 25, 1932)
For the purpose of covering expenditures
required in the Manchurian incident, the Government
may, in addition to the amount of loans to be issued
under Imperial Ordinance No. 6, Imperial Ordinance
No. 14 and Imperial Ordinance No. 19 issued in 1932,
float a loan of Yen 67,500,000 or borrow this amount.
In case it is necessary to replenish the amount
of the above loan, the Government may issue another
loan or borrow the amount of this loan.
Supplementary Rule:
The present measure shall be put into force
from the day of promulgation.

IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 4 (1932)
Official Gazette, January 28, 1932.
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanotion the measure for the prohibition of notes of the
Bank of Japan being converted into gold coins under para-
graph 1 of Article 70 of the Constitution and order the
same to be promulgaged.
(IMPERIAL SEAL)
Counter-signed by -
Premier Inukai and other Ministers
of State.
Imperial Ordinance No. 4.
The Bank of Japan shall not allow the conversion of
its convertible notes into gold coins for the time being,
except in cases where permission is given by the Minister
of Finance.
The Bank of Chosen shall not allow the conversion of
its convertible notes into gold for the time being, except
in cases where permission is given by the Minister of
Finance.
The Bank of Taiwan shall not allow the conversion of
its convertible notes into gold coins for the time being,
except in cases where permission is given by the Minister
of Finance.
Supplementary Rule.
The present Ordinance shall be put into force from
the day on which Imperial Ordinance No. 29l, issued in
1931, lost its legal effect, namely, December 17, l931.

IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO. 7 (1932)
Official Gazette, January 31, 1932.
We, with the approval of the Privy Council, hereby
sanction the measure for the prohibition of part of a
certain amount of money from being transferred to the
Amortization Fund in accordance with paragraph 1 of
Article 8 of the Constitution and order the same to be
promulgated.
(IMPERIAL SEAL)
Countersigned by -
Premier Inukai and other Ministers of State.
Imperial Ordinance No. 7.
The amount for the redemption of principal of
national loans to be transferred to the Amortization
Fund for the 193l-1932 fiscal year under Artiole 2 of
the National Loans Adjustment Fund Special Account
Law shall not exceed Yen 44,000,000 altogether.
Supplementary Rule
The present Ordinance shall be put into force from
the day of promulgation.

GOVERNMENT FINANCES:
Nothing to report.
vI.
GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMI CONDITIONS:
A. Excess of Imports.
Japan's visible foreign trade for the first
quarter of l932 resulted in an exoees of imports in the
amount of Yen 159,002,000, showing a gain of Yen 122,080,000
over the corresponding period of last year. Exports for
the period amounted to Yen 251,688,000 against Yen 293,428,000
for the mar before, and importw were Yen 410,690,000

Note issue in excess of 120,000,000 yen of gold
reserve is taxed 5% and, as the margin between issue and
reserve has widened, interest rates have risen to cover the
tax. There has been no liquidation of loans, and there is
agitation to remove the tax on overissue and thereby lower
interest rates and facilitate liquidation. This will
spell inflation and further depreciatien of yen, but is
regarded as a lesser evil than forcing liquidation.
Prices.
In spite of the depreciation of the yen, commodity
prices are lower than they have been in many years.
Shipping.

over 1,000 tons, with total tonnage 3,562,346. 356 vessels,
of tonnage 358,747, are laid up now. The leading companies,
Nippen Yusen Kaisha, Osaka Shesen Kaisha, and KokusaiKisen
Kaisha, deolared no dividends in 1931.

Government Finance.
It 1s estimated that from 300,000,000 to 500,000,000
yen will have to be borrowed by the Government to meet

are in the noighborhoed of 400,000,000 yen. Perhaps

200,000,000 yen.
Conclusions - Page 21.
In the absence of a calamity such as a disastrous
earthquake or war, Japan will probably manage to meet its
obligations, which after all are not heavy and are long
term. Balances abroad are enough to meet immediate re-
quirements, and sufficient foreign sales are expeoted to
discharge interest payments. Due to credit control by
the Bank of Japan and the Yokohama Specie Bank, private
funds abroad can easily be transferred to state account.
Further gold export may be necessary, but this will
only inflate ourrency, which is a domestic problem.
While conditions in Japan are not satisfactory, the same
may be said in most countries today.

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC REPORT
On May 11 and July 5, 1927, the Embassy submitted
reports on the financial situation in Japan precipitated
by the failure of the firm of Suzuki and Company. An
immediate result of this failure was the suspension of
payments by a number of banks, including the branches
of the Bank of Taiwan outside of Formosa.
At that time a moratorium was declared and steps
were taken by the Government to relieve the situation.
The Bank of Japan advanced about 200,000,000 yen to
the Bank of Taiwan and about 500,000,000 to other
banks including 80,000,000 yen to the 15th Bank.
The Commercial Attaché informs me that according to
the figures he has, about 575,000,000 of this amount
is still outstanding. This is over and above other
loans, and shows little prospect of further liquidation
after five years. It will be recalled that the banks,
at the time these loans were made, were carrying paper
which dated back to the post war period. This means
that the ultimate security against this large out-
standing account of the Bank of Japan is a mass of
obligations of long standing and doubtful value .
A statement of loans supplied to the Commercial
Attaché by the Finance Department is as follows:
(Reverse

This would indicate that of the  9,548,000,000 out-
standing long term loans, the Hypothec Bank, the Industrial
Bank and the Hokkaido Colonial Bank held ￥ 1,851,295,000;
practically all of this sum is probably land and industria
loans. It has been impossible to obtain more detailed
figures than those given above. The conclusion to be
drawn from the data is that long term loans, aside from
commodity and security loans, are not being liquidated.
The Commercial Attaché informs me that the best
judgment of persons in a position to know is that land
values

=== Page 32 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
values are now not in excess of 35 per cent of values
at the time many of these loans were made. They are
distributed about the country and if liquidated rapidly
it seems likely that many of the country banks would
fail. It will be noted that the Hypothec Bank alone
has outstanding loans of  1,072,836,000. Against
this it has outstanding debentures of 853,692,000, and
deposits of 130,961,000. These deposits probably re-
present funds of smaller country banks. While the bank
is stated to be very conservatively managed, decreased
land values and land income have undoubtedly affected
the security behind its mortgages. With such a small
margin between its mortgages and the total of its
deposits and debentures, its position cannot be con-
sidered as favorable. At the same time it should be
stated that the Hypothec Bank was organized for the
purpose of making long term loans on land, and that it
is not expected to maintain its assets in the liquid
condition demanded in the case of commercial banks.
The industrial loans, as indicated in the table
given above show a substantial increase over a period
of years. It is difficult to state how these loans
are distributed or on just what security they were
lent. Presumably they represent loans on buildings
and installations. Much of the industrial plant on
which the money was lent has deteriorated. On the
other hand, as will to some extent be seen later, new
investments have been made in cotton spinning, rayon,
fertilizer and rubber which conservative opinion considers
fully

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
fully warranted.
The commodity loans show considerable reduction.
The Commercial Attaché , however, states that there is
some doubt as to whether it really represents a liquidation,
or merely a transfer from one account to another. In l929
the Government undertook to protect the silk industry and
the banks advanced, it is reported, something like 100,000,000
yen against silk in storage. The Government accepted liability
of 29,500,000 of this amount. This silk has never been sold.
It is not known under what heading this loan is carried,
nor at what value the silk has been carried as security.
It is difficult to estimate the value and amount of com-
modities which may be held off the market by loans.
Loans against securities were greater in l93o than
in l927. This indicates that there hasbeen very little
liquidation despite the marked decrease in security values
during the past two years. There is a good deal of what
may be termed juggling of these loans. For example, the
Kawasaki Dockyard has a paid up capital of 90,000,000 yen.
Outstanding obligations are reported as approximately
145,000,000 yen, a condition which dates from the moratorium
of 1927. There appears no prospect of a revival of business
that will enable this company to pay off its debts. Instead
of selling the concern at auction, and thus wiping out the
indebtedness, arrangements are being made to cancel some
62,000,000 yen of the debts by issuing preferred stock.
This preferred stock will probably appear ultimately some-
where as security for bank loans, although dividends cannot
possibly be earned. I am informed that much of the security
against

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
against loans outstanding has about the same value as
this stock. There are, however, many going concerns in
Japan whose securities would be considered valid col-
lateral under almost any circumstances.
The type of loans described as "others" is difficult
of analysis. It consists probably of building loans as
distinet from land and industrial loans and of chattel
and miscellaneous obligations hard to classify. As will
be noted, this item has shown a steady although not large
increase. In the meantime income from buildings and
commercial operations has been decreasing, and it is im-
probable that conditions warrant the continued carrying of
such large sums.

The total public debt of the Japanese Government,
as obtained by the Commercial Attaché from official sources,
was ¥ 4,525,470,725 domestic issue and ¥ 1,477,334,729 foreign
issue or a grand total of Yen 6,002,805,454 on December 31,
1931. There has been an increase during the current year,
and the figure will probably be considerably larger at the
abroad,
end of 1932. There are some bonds falling due this year,
estimated as requiring something over fifty million yen at
par. It is understood that the Government expects to meet
them from balances abroad.
In addition to the Government debts, the Commercial
Attaché informs me that figures from official sources show

It will thus be seen that the estimate indicates an adverse
balance of approximately Yen 75,000,000. These figures are not
official: in the nature of things they cannot be, but the
Commercial Attaché in my judgment is as likely to be right as
any one. This adverse balance, added to an excess import
balance of Yen 139,839,000 for 1931 shown by the Review of
Commerce and Industries of the Consulate General makes a total
of something over Yen 214,000,000 to be made up in other ways.
This figure is of course only approximate. It has been checked
with estimates from other sources, and represents the best
judgment available.

It will be noted that while the note issue has not
varied greatly, there has been a decided drop in the gold
reserve. The Bank of Japan is allowed to issue tax free
notes to the extent of Yen 120,000,000 over the gold re-
serve. Above that amount a tax of 5 per cent is leviable.
As the gold reserve dropped, this had the effect of in-
creasing interest rates. Reference to the state of bank
loans will show that there has been no liquidation. There
is much agitation for reduction of the tax on excess note
issue and for an increase in the limit of tax free issue.
It is realized that this would have the effect of decreasing
the exchange value of the yen, but this is regarded in many
quarters as an evil less likely to cause trouble than an
attempt to force liquidation of outstanding loans. In other
words, the policy advocated is inflation to the point where
increased yen prices will enable debtors to discharge long
standing obligations without too much inconvenience.
There is little indication as yet of rising prices.
The Tokyo wholesale price index based on October 19oo prices
as l00 indicates a price level in February l930 of 199.8,
in February 1931 of 158.00 and in 1932 of 16l.4 . Even with
the decreased gold value of the yen, ( about 33-l/2 cents at
ourrent rates) prices are less than in l930. The fall in
the

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.92)
-10-
oost of living is noticeable, and affects all except
imported articles. A table of prices prepared by the

I have had the benefit of the investigations of the
Commercial Attaché and his staff who have been helpful
in the highest degree. The conclusions drawn from the
facts are not necessarily those of the Commercial Attaché,
although we have been in close contact.
Iron and Steel.
The paid up capital of the iron and steel industry
in Japan Proper is reported as Yen 385,406,000 . Of this
amount about Yen 140,000,000 represents Government investment
in the Yawata Steel Works.
Private companies are stated to
have an indebtedness of a proximately Yen 320,000,000. The
output of steel in 1931 was given as 1,549,000 tons against
1,789,000 in 1930. Pig iron production was given as
1,187,196 tons in 1930. The industry cannot be described
as large. It has not been profitable in the past few years,
and the loss on the operation of the Yawata plant alone is
given as Yen 10,000,000 in 1931.
The Government has made great efforts to assist the
development of the industry. The companies now operating
are exempt from business and income taxes and are permitted
to import machinery at a reduction of 20 per cent in duty.
There is also a subsidy on output. This amounted to
Yen 2,422,000 in 1928- the last year for which exact figures
are at hand, The industry is handicapped by the necessity
for importing most of the raw materials, as Japan has very
little iron ore, and such coal and limestone as are available
are not close to the large industrial centers. It does not
seem probable that the steel industry can ever reach large
proportions.

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
proportions. It seems certain, however that the
Government will continue its efforts to supply as
large a portion as possible of domestic needs from
domestic manufactures.
Cement.
The Japanese cement industry represents a capital
investment of Yen 200,000,000. Production amounts to
about 3,700,000 tons per year and is sufficient to supply
the Japanese market and to provide about 450,000 tons
for export. Imports are only about l0,ooo tons, practically
all of it special type for particular purposes. The quality
of Japanese cement is good and it finds a ready market
all over the Far East.
. The industry is reported as in good
financial condition.
Copper.
Copper mining and production is one of the oldest
industries in
Japan. Production amounted to 74,800 metric
tons in 1931.
The Japanese copper ore has a gold and
silver content which helps to reduce the cost of copper
production. The four leading producers have a paid up
capital of Yen 102,500,000. These concerns are said to be
in as good condition as could be expected under present
adverse conditions.
There is an import duty of Yen 7.00
per 100 kin(approximately 2.6 cents per pound). There
were exports( mostly to Russia in the form of bars) of
26,802 metric tons of copper in 1931 and imports of 2,026;
the amount of imports and exports have been quite irre-
gular over a period of years.
Expectations are that the development of the
electrical industry in Japan will provide a constant
and perhaps increasing market for copper. Produdtion
costs

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
costs are said to be higher than in Chile, the United
States and Africa, which may militate against successful
copper exports.
Shipping.
The register of Japanese shipping on January 1, 1932
listed 914 vessels of over 1,000 tons and a total tonnage
of 3,562,346. On January 15, 1932, there were 356 vessels
totaling 358,747 tons laid up, according to official reports.
The international ccounts of the shipping companies showed a
favorable balance of Yen 138,290,000 in 1928, Yen 159,175,000
in 1929 and Yen 124,335,000 in 1930. Official figures are not
at hand for l931, but rel iable estimates place the decrease
in income from the 193o figures as in the nei hborhood of
Yen 60,000,000.
Jap nese shipping suf ered from a number of adverse
conditions in 1931. A serious loss in income was caused by
the slump in the pound sterling, as rates on about 60 per
cent of the freight received by Japanese lines are quoted in
English money. Drop in the China trade incident to the
boycott movement as well as general trade depression brought
decreased revenue.
The three la rgest shipping companies, the Nippon Yusen
Kaisha, Osaka Shosen Kaisha and the Kokusai Kisen Kaisha
who own more than 40 per cent of the tonnage of vessels of
over 1,ooo tons, declared no dividends during the past calendar
year. The Department of Communications reports for the
fiscal year l931 as follows:

The outlook for the coming ear is not bright,
particularly in view of the competition which Jap nese
companies have to face in foreign competition.
Shipbuilding.
The shipbuilding industry in Japan has not been
prosperous for the past few years. The Department of
Communications gives the following re ort for the fiscal

There is no evidence that conditions have improved any since
that report was made. Repair work will perhaps keep the
shipyards going, but there is li tle likelihood of any im-
mediate new building. However, the larger yards, vith the
exception of the Kawasaki Dockyard which was mentioned in
another connection, seem to be in solvent condition.
Silk.
The silk industry as a whole has not been profitable
for some years, and it is reported that about one fourth
of the silk filatures have been operating without profit
for a long time.
The present acute situation dates from
1929, when silk prices dropped below production costs.
The Government, in an effort to relieve the situation, put
the Silk Price Indemnification Law into effect. Banks
advanced

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
advanced up to Yen 1250 per bale, and the Government
undertook to assume bank losses to a maximum of Yen
29,500,000.
There are said to be 107,830 bales of the 1929
crop still in storage, against which the banks advanced
about Yen 130,000,000.
. These loans have never been
liquidated, and the silk cannot be sold to any advan age
under present conditions. It seems probable that ultimately
the whole transaction will result
in a loss to the banks
and the Government.
. The banks theoretically have recourse
against the filatures on whose silk the loans  ere made,
but as about 40 per cent of the filatures are closed, very
little could be realized.
There are 38o0 silk filatures in Japan, and it is
reported that there are only about loo of these whose
physical and financial condition would permit them to
operate at a profit under present conditions. It is
impossible to estimate the capital invested in these
filatures, or in the raw silk industry, as the filatures
operate on a hand to mouth basis, and buy their cocoons
from the farmers pretty much in the same manner as cotton
factors buy cotton in the United States, or as a farmer buys
cattle to fatten for the market. The total bank loans are
estimated as at least Yen 250,000,000, of which at least
Yen 100,000,000 cannot be redeemed under any conditions
foreseen at present.
The outlook for the present year is not very good.
The prosperity of the Japanese raw silk industry is de-
pendent upon purchases in the United States. From all
accounts increased American purchases cannot be expected,
at least at a price of Yen 8oo per bale, the lowest at
which

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
which profits can be realized.
Cotton.
The cotton spinning and weaving industry in Japan
is reported to be in sound condition. The industry is
well managed and well financed. Since l930, Jap nese
cotton concerns have experienced some difficulties in
their foreign trade, but substantial profits are re orted
by the larger ∞mpanies. The difficulties were especially
marked in China, due to political complications. There
and elsewhere they were faced with sharp competition from
British manufactures, especially after the depreciation
of the pound sterling.
Stocks of raw cotton, arns und t xtiles have
increased steadily since October, 193l. A comparison
of 1930 and 1931 is given in the appended table:

Since the imposition of the gold embargo, costs for
American Cotton have advanced 60 per cent and yarn prices
54 per cent. Prices have increased more than the added
cost of the raw material
 caused by the depreciation of the
yen.
The industry is dominated by nine large companies
whose combined capital represents 69 per cent of the
industry and whose reserves are equivalent to 85 per cent
of the industry. The total paid up capital is Yen 384,827,000
and reserves total yen 239,429,000. The larger companies
made heavy purchases of American cotton before the imposition
of the gold embargo and should be able to show a profit even
during the current year.
The outlook for 1932 is not favorable, as the China
market for Japanese cottons shows no improvement, and
conditions in India show no prospect of increased purchases.
It seems probable, however, that domestic consumption and
even a reduced export will enable the cotton industry to
operate comfortably.
Electric Light and Power.
The only reason for discussion of the Electric Light
and Power industry is because of the foreign loans which
have been contracted. This industry has a paid up capital
of about Yen 3,000,000,000, of which Yen 1,307,000,000
represents strictly light and power companies, as distinguished
from electric railway or other projects which may from time
to time sell light and power. There are five electric
power companies which have foreign loans. The Tokyo Electric
Light Company, the Daido Company, the Toho Company, the
Ujikawa Company and the Nippon Company. Their total paid
up capital is about Yen 900,000,000. The outstanding
foreign

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
foreign loans of those concerns amount to about
Yen 340,000,000, and interest charges call for
Yen 21,347,000 at par. The present depreciated exchange
makes it necessary for them to find an additional Yen
7,1l6,000 for interest abroad. There is also the Taiwan
Power Company loan of Yen 45,700,000 which requires
interest payments of Yen 3,350,700.
There is every reason to believe that these concerns
will be able to meet their foreign interest charges, although
it seems probable that dividend payments may have to suffer
to enable it to be done. Power sales have increased since
December 1, and efforts are being made to eliminate
duplications and competition among the power concerns.
The increase in power sales is apparently due to reduced
rates and high prices for fuel.
Government Revenue and Expenditure.
The foregoing short discussion of financial and
industrial conditions will indicate to some extent the
lessened sources of revenue open to the Government. In
addition there was a decided drop in imports, reflected
in a lessened income to the Government from import duties.
Despatch No. 553 of April 5, 1932 discussed the
budget in some detail. It was pointed out in that despatch
that efforts had been made to reduce Government costs, and
that on the administrative side real progress had been
made. On the other hand, there have been increased military
expenses due to the Manchurian enterprise and the actions
around Shanghai. In consequence the budget failed to
balance

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
balance by something over Yen 68,000,000. This will have to
be made good by additional loans. Despatch No. 533 of
March 26, 1932 reported that the decrease in revenue for
nine months to December 31, 1931, compared with the same
period for the preceding year amounted to 13.8 per cent,
or Yen l09,425,266. Exact figures for the full fiscal year
are not yet available, but there is nothing to indicate any
improvement. Reports of the amount of borrowing necessary
on the part of the Government for the current year vary from
Yen 300,000,000 to Yen 500,000,000. The amount cannot be
accurately estimated now, but it is safe to assume that the
national debt of Japan will show a substantial increase at the
end of the year.
It may not however. be necessary for the Government to
resort to a public issue of bonds. The Commercial Attaché
estimates that banks in Tokyo and Osaka could arrange bond
subscriptions of from Yen 100,000,000 to Yen 200,000,000 in
case of need. It seems probable however that the Government
will approach private banks only after borrowing to the
available limit from funds deposited with the Bank of Japan
and with the Postal Deposits Bureau. The Commercial Attaché's
information is that perhaps Yen 400,000,000 could be relied
upon from these sources. It seems that the deposits of the
Postal Deposits Bureau have increased as the collapse of a
number of small banks has caused people to use the Postal
Savings facilities, and there is some other idle money
available as well. Govarnment bonds could undoubtedly
obtain a market for the time being in these quarters.
The condition would not be difficult until the absorption
of

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
of the liquid funds reached higher proportions.
Conelusions.
There is no doubt that financial and economic
conditions in Japan are not at present satisfactory.
That is a situation, however, that it not peculiar to
Japan. Conditions are far from satisfactory in most
countries. In the absence of a calamity such as the
earthquake of 1923, or the outbreak of a war, it seems
probable that the Japanese will manage to meet their
foreign obligations, which after all are not heavy and
in any case are long term debts. Balances abroad are
sufficient to meet inmediate requirements, and the Japanese
look to sufficient sales in foreign markets to discharge
their interest payments. Due to the credit control
exercised by the Bank of Japan and the preponderant
influence exercised in the exchange market by the Yokohama
Specie Bank, transfers of funds abroad can be readily made
from private to public accounts and vice versa.
It may and probably will be necessary for Japan
to ship gold abroad from time to time. This may have the
effect of lessening still further the gold reserve and
cause an inflation of the currency. That, however, is a
domestic problem. As previously pointed out, there are
many Japanese who look upon inflation as a desirable
method of facilitating the liquidation of the debts which
are now such a handicap to the business interests of the
country.

GONERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
The outstanding features of the general financial
and eoonomic condition of Japan during the month of
August were the continued distress in the rural areas,
the deeline in the value of the yen, and the rise of the
silk market. The last-mentioned offers the only ray of hope
in the situation and, if it continues, ought to help in
improving both the condition of the farmers and the exchange
rate. However, if the rise is artificial and there is a sharp
fall, many Japanese speculators will be hit.
Regarding the distress in the rural districts there is
little to add to what has been said in previous reports from
this Embassy. From every direction come stories not only
of the mounting debts of the farmers which they will never
be able to pay but also of their inability to fight starvation
any longor. It is diffioult to find a standand of measurement
for determining the degree and the extent of the poverty that
exists but it is known that the desperation of the farmers is
one of the principal causes of anxiety for the Government at
the present moment.
The value of the yen fell during the month from 27-1/2
cents to 22 cents with a slight rise during the last few
days to 23 and a fraction. In the judgment of local
bankers there was nothing in the commercial situation to
warrant such a drop; in fact, Japan's situation in foreign
commeree seemed to justify a rise rather than a fall. The
drop was due to fear as to the future of the yen, the
Seiyukai's deelarations in favor es inflation, the wanting
wmeurtata'y vere the iarlmential factors. The export trade

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
was reported to have reacted inmediately to the fall in
the yem. A "rush of exports" was reported from Yokohama and
Kobo.
A rise of over 50 per cent in the price of raw silk
during the month is attributed both to the fall in the
yen and to the rising commodity prices in the United States.
Some observers fear that silk has risen too much on the
New York market, but the Japanese speculators are now
"bulls". Some encouragement was given to the farmer by a
rise in the price of cocoons which reflected the rise in
the raw silk market.
An element of gloom was introduced into Japan's export
trade at the end of the month by the report of India's
decision to raise the duty on non-British cotton cloth
from 31-1/4 per cent to 50 per cent ad valorem, to be
effective immediately. According to local statisties,in
1929-30 Japan suppl1ed 29.3 per cent of the cotton cloth
1mported by India whichoonstituted 28.8 per cent of Japan's
cottom cloth exports. It is believed that the preferenee
now accorded British goods will eventually seriously
ourtail Japen's trade there. According to the local press
1t will mean a slackening in cotton textile manufacturing
in Japan and a consequent curtailment of her demand for
raw cotton from the United States. However, as long as
the yen and the cotton price remain low Japanese will have
some advantage.
According to anouncement made by the Finance Department
on August 1 the total amouat of foreign currency securities,
including Japanese Government bonds issued in dollars.
pouno

一27 -

VI. GENRAL OHOIC AND VINANCIAL ONDITIO
It is believes that the internal eeonomie situatiom
of Japan has been improving during recent months. This
may not comsimue. In fect the mounting expenditures in
Manchuria and the desande of the rmy and Novy for inoreased
eppropriations will necossitate more taxation and the outlook

country a breathing spell.

priees are aleo rising. However, the efforts of the govern-
ment to bring about iaflation of the curreney have been futile.

DIVISION OF
Mr. Moser's letter of January 27, 1933,
encloses a ciroular prepared in the Far
Eastern Section of the Department of Commerce,
according to which the domestic situation
in Japan at the beginning of 1933 is less
favorable than it was a year ago. Prices
have risen without an increase in purchasing
power. Practically all forms of income have
doolined. Actual earnings of labor in Septem-
ber, 1931, were 88.7, in November, 1932,
86.2; retail prices rose from 134 to 142 in
November, 1932. Rice and fresh fish, the
principal articles of diet, advanced 13% and 32%
respectively between November, l93l, and
November, l932. Current reports indicate no
halt in the upward trend of prices since
November, 1932, but on the ∞ntrary it appears
that prices are advancing still more rapidly.
The farmer's paition is more satisfactory,
but fertilizers will have advanced by spring
almost loo% in pri ce, and it will take years
of profitable prices to wipe out the rural
debts. City workers are suffering the most,
wages being slightly lower than in l931.
Interest rates on postal savings, which
represent the savings of the humblest people,
lare now the lowest in history, while interest
rates

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
rates on bank saving deposits have been
lowered, and yields from bonds and stock
securities are lower. Unemployment is
unrelieved for the reason that those
industries which have shown greater
activity have increased their machinery
and used the overtime system rather than
omploying new hands.

SPECIAL CIRCULAR NO. 265 - DIVISION OF REGIONAL INFORMATION
(Price 10 cents to other than regular subsoribers)
DOMESTIC SITUATION IN JAPAN AGGRAVATED BY INCREASED LIVING COSTS AND LOWER INCOME
V. P. Copping, Far Eastern Section.
Inoreased costs of living, as evidenced by the advance in retail prices, without a cor-
responding increase in purchasing power, is a matter of growing concern in Japan. Immediate-
ly after going off the gold standard on December 13, 1931, commodity prices advanced, though
the upward trend was halted for a few months in the early summer of 1932 when exchange settled
around the $0.30 level. In August, however, the yen dropped sharply, and by November had
declined to a low of $0.20 against a par of $0.4985. Prices increased rapidly; not only as
a direct result of the exchange situation, but because of the increase in production costs
of most commodities. Prices of manufactured goods particularly, which for the most part are
produced from imported raw materials, advanced as exchange declined.
But while prices have continued to climb there has been no increase in purchasing power,
practically all forms of income having declined. Wages have fallen to a lower level than
prevailed in l931 when the value of the yen was close to par, while unemployment is still
unrelieved. The following table indicates the movements of exchange in relation to retail
prices, the rates of wages and the percentage of employment:
Exchange Rates and Indices of Retail Prices, Wages and Employment in Japan

All Daily Necessities Cost More.
Of the commodities constituting daily necessities, foodstuffs have shown the greatest
price advance, followed by clothing. Rice and fresh fish, the principal articles of diet,
advanced 13 per cent and 32 per cent, respectively, in November, 1932, as compared with the
provailing prices in November, 1931. Principal articles of clothing averaged 7 per cent high-
or. Fuel prices are now advancing and may be expected to increase further during the re-
mainder of the winter, some estimating a total advance of 20 per cent. The average price
index for miscellaneous items entering into the cost of living advanced 4.5 per cent in last
November over November of l93l. Current reports indicate no halt in the upward trend of re-
tail prices sinoe November, the last month for which actual figures are available, but in-
dicate to the contrary that prices have been advancing still more rapidly.
Earmers Position More Satisfactory, but Production Costs Higher.
Farmers have benefited to some extent by the advance in rice and cocoon prices, and
will receive further benefits if the upward trend continues, although the probab!lity that
production costs will also be higher this spring should be taken into account. Fertilizers,
for example, are selling considerably higher. The actual price of bean cake was more than
20 por cent higher in November, 1932, than in the previous November, and sulphate of ammonia
nearly 30 per cent higher. It is further reported that this spring farmers will have to pay
almost loo per cent more for fertilizers. However, as raw silk prices have also advanced
l00 per cent, the farmers may be expected to be in a better position as a result of increased
roturns from the sale of cocoons and other products, although it will take years of profit-
able prices for farm products to wipe out the enormous debts that have piled up in rural
districts over the past few years:
City Workers the Greatest Sufferers.
Industrial workers, the salaried class, and those living on pensions or small savings'
are the people who at present are suffering most from increased living costs. As pointed
out before, wages on the whole are slightly lower than in l931. There have been some in-
oresses in a few industries, but others have reduced wages, as shown in the following table:
T.ET
7744-2.

Cash Wages of Industrial Workers Augmented by Other Benefits.
Current wages are reported to be practically at the September, 1932, level. The above
figures represent the actual daily cash wage received by workers, but do not represent total
income, as it is customary in Japan for employees to receive regular bonuses and sick allow-
ances, with additional bonuses when discharged. Furthermore, a large number of factory oper-
atives, particularly in the textile industry, which employs about 50 per cent of the factory
workers in Japan, live in factory dormitories. These workers receive, in addition to the
usual bonuses and allowances, housing and the advantage of food sold to them at below market
prices, the costs being borne by the mill owners. One of the largest cotton spinning com-
panies in Japan estimates that such carrying charges in his mills amount to .57 yen per day
for each operative.
Qther Forms of Income Reduced.
Notwithstanding these benefits, the total income of Japan's industrial workers is small,
and with the rising cost of living is insufficient to meet current needs.
Those living on pensions and small savings likewise have had their income reduced.
Interest rates on postal savings, which represent the savings of the humblest people, have
been reduced to 3 per cent, the lowest in history, while interest rates on bank saving de-
posits, also representing largely the small savings of the average public, have been lowered
and further reductions are being considered. Yields from bonds and stock securities also are
lower.
Unemployment Unrelieved.
Despite the apparent industrial boom that has been enjoyed as a result of depreciated
ourrenoy, demand for labor has not grown and unemployment remains a serious problem. In-
dustries whioh have shown greater activity, such as the cotton, silk, rayon, cement, and
iron and steel industries, have increased their machinery and used the overtime system rather
than employing new hands. Other industries have not emerged from the depression sufficiently
to warrant taking on more workers.
7744-4.

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
oial Ciroular No. 265--5.
At the end of June, 1932, there were 481,589 persons unemployed in Japan, based on an
cotimated population of 7,046,925 persons holding regular jobs. This represents an inorease
of 110,l68 over the number unemployed in June, 1931. However, unemployment is more widespread
than these figures would indicate. It is impossible to obtain an accurate figure of unem-
ployed in Japan as the great majority of unemployed are never counted. There is no unem-
ployment insurance system, and for the most part, the unemployed are absorbed by the tradi-
tional family system whereby discharged workers return home and are entitled to share the
family's food and supplies. Industrial workers recruited from the farm return to the farm
in times of enforced idleness. In the cities, many workers, particularly female employees,
live with their parents and continue to so live when thrown out of work. Some estimates
place the number of wage earners in financial distress at close to 2,0o0,000, in addition
to the large number of farm hands made jobless through the distress in rural districts.
Years of depression have so pauperized the farming class and city dwellers of normally
moderate means that they are no longer able to care for their unemployed relatives. Thus.
Japan, for the first time in history, is faced with a serious unemployment problem.
Wage Reductions Growing Cause of Labor Disputes.
Labor disputes in the first nine months of 1932 total 1,540, or nearly as many as in
the corresponding period of l93l, although the number of workers involved decreased from
115,000 to 75,000. This may be due to the smaller number employed, as well as to the fact
that those holding jobs are reluctant to participate in disputes in times when jobs are scarce
In some industries workers have continued their work after the company ceased paying wages.
This is particularly true in the silk industry which is reported to owe some 2,O00,oo0 yen
in back wages.
The principal causes of labor disputes have been the failure of employers to increase
wages, and in many cases, the reduction of wages. Disputes resulting from these causes have
been more numerous since last summer, as the rising cost of living has placed wage earners
in a difficult position.
Thus, the domestic situation in Japan at the beginning of l933 is less favorable than
it was a year ago, when purchasing power was higher. It may be expected, however, that if
yen exchange remains low and prices continue to advance, wages will ultimately start upward.
C. K. MOSER,
Chief, Far Eastern Seotion,
Division of Regional Information.

GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
There is little ehange to report in the general economie
and finaneial condition of Japan during the month of January.
The pri cipal industries of the country continue to enjoy a
spurt of prosperity.
894.50
The Tokyo stock market reglstered  decline during the
conth. This was due pertly to s natursl reaction to the
bull market of previous months an; partly to other eamses,
such as the failure of the international situetion to improve,
the fear of higher tariffs for Japan's goods entering other
countries, the stability of the yen uring recent months,
and the deeision of the Bank of Japan to sell the new govern-
ment bond issues to the publie instead of depositing them
in its vaults. The last two factors are indications to the
Japanese business man that extreme inflation of the currency
may not take place and therefore industrial profits may not
be as great in coming months as they have been during the
recent months of the declining yen.
Raw silk prices dropped during the month frow Yen 1000
& bale to Yen 700, due to the falling off in the demand in
New York. The lowered price does not necessarily mean ruin
for the silk people as prices have recently been extremely
high. However, the drop is responsible for the reported
decision to keep the filatories closed during the month of
February. They are elosed every year during January because
of the cold weather but this year the period will be extend-
ed to two months.

EXCHANGE RATES
Par value of the yen equals $0.4985.
Yen exchange rate was approximately at par throughout 193l until Japan went off the
gold standard on December 13, 1931, when the rate declined, reducing the average for the
year to $0.4885. As the drop in exchange occurred too near the end of the year to affect
trade, conversions of trade statistics from yen to dollars for l93l are made at the weighted
average of $0.4936.
The average rate of exchange was $0.4610 in 1929; 0.4939 in 1930;
and $0.2811 in 1932.

LABOR AND WAGES
At the end of October, 1932, there were 503,958 salaried persons, workers and day
laborers unemployed in Japan, based on an estimated population of 7,221,043 perons holding
regular jobs. This represents an increase of 64,944 over the number of unemployed in October,
193i. Unemployment is more widespread than these figures indicate. It is impossible to
obtain an accurate figure, as most of the unemployed are not counted but are absorbed by
the traditional family system. Some unofficial estimates place the number of wage earners
in financial distress at close to 2,O00,000, in addition to the large number of farm hands
made jobless through distress in rural districts.
In 1931 there were 2,146 cases of labor disputes, involving 141,685 laborers; in the
first 11 months of 1932 there were 1,798 cases, involving 92,858 laborers.
Based on the latter half of 1920 as 100, the average wage inlex for all industries in Tokyo
stood at 96.4 at the end of 1930; 94.6 in 1931; and 93.6 in November, 1932.

1931, and 184.6 in December, 1932. Retail price index (July, 1914, as equal to 100) stood
at 134.6 in December, 1931, and 146.0 in December, 1932.

In 1929 the United States purchased 69,759,000 pounds of raw silk from Japan; 69,526,-
000 pounds in 1931; and 69,137,000 pounds in 1932. (1932 figures preliminary).
Raw silk represents about 80 per cent of the United States total imports from Japan.
The United States takes from 65 to 75 per cent of the raw silk produced in Japan, and
accounts for 95 per cent of Japan's total raw silk exports.
Japan supplies about 82 per cent of our total raw silk imports. Raw silk comes into
the United States duty-free and is used in the manufacture of silk fabrics and hosiery in
such principal manufacturing crnters as Philadelphia, Allentown, and Wilkes Barre, Pa., and
Patterson, N. J. Comparatively small amounts of such manufactured and dutiable articles as
silk fabrics, chinaware, and toys are also imported from Japan.
Japan supplied 86 per cent of our total imports of crabmeat in 1931.

*United States dollar figures of American trade with Japan and Japanese figures con-
verted from yen values into dollars will not agree, owing first, to the fact that United
States figures of trade with Japan include Taiwan and Chosen, while Japanese figures are
for Japan proper; second, to exchange difference, and third, to difference in transit
time. For example, items exported from the United States late in any year will not be re-
corded in Japan as imports until in the following year. These factors combined make a
considerable variation between Japanese and American statistics.

In 1929 the United States exported 1,101,000 bales of raw cotton to Japan; 1,744,000
bales in 1931; and 2,240,000 bales in 1932. (1932 figures preliminary).
Raw cotton accounted for 42 per cent of our total exports to Japan in 1929; 50 per
cent of our total exports to Japan in 1931; and 64 per cent of the total in 1932. Raw
cotton for Japan comes mainly from the States of Oklahoma, Texas, and Arkansas.
Iron and steel products come from the vicinity of Pittsturgh, Pa., and Birmingham,
Ala.; lumber almost exclusively from the Pacific West Coast; automotive products from the
vicinity of Detroit; and machinery from various cities in the northeastern part of the
United States.
In 1932 the United States had a favorakle balance in merchandise trade with Japan for
the first time in history. The favorable balance totaled only $526,000, tut compares with
an unfavorab1e trade balance of $56,634,000 in 1931, and $172,745,000 in 1929.
In recent years Japan has ranked fourth among world markets for United States exports,
but in 1932 it held third place, exceeded only by the United Kingdom and Canada.
Japan ranks second as a source of imports into the United States, exceeded only by
Canada.

VISCOUNT

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
VISCOUNT KIKUJIRO ISHII, Privy Councillor.
Viscount Ishii is a prominent Japanese diplomatist
and statesman. He has had little experience in finance
or commerce. Although not so far as is known antagonis-
tic toward any foreign nation, he is an ardent national-
ist and has been in important positions at moments of
Japanese expansionist activity. He was Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs at the time of the annexation of
Korea and is said to have received the title of Baron in
recognition of his services in that connection. He was
Ambassador to France and Minister for Foreign Affairs
during the period when Japan took possession of German
territory in the Far East and when secret treaties grant-
ing permanent possession of such territory to Japan were
ontered into with the Allies. As Special Envoy to the
United States he concluded the Lansing-Ishii Agreement of
Hovember 2, 1917 (canceled April 14, 1923), which the
Japanese interpreted subsequently more favorable to Japa-
nese desiderata in the Far East than did the United States.
He was Ambassador to Washington in 1918 and 1919 and was
again Ambassador to France from 1920 to 1927. During the
latter period he was a delegate to the League of Nations
and was Chief Delegate to the Naval Disarmament Conference
at Genova in 1927.
Viscount Ishii is 67 years old. He is of samurai
descent and his family is Roman Catholic. His nephew is
Mr. Shiratori (Chief of the Intelligence Bureau of the
Foreign Office, who, in his capacity as "Foreign Office
spokesman", has made many inaccurate and intemperate
statements whieh have done much to injest tension into
relations between the United States and Japan). Viscount
Imh11

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.98)
Imhii is & oultivated gentleman with a good command of
English and French; has broad sympathies, a strong
personality and charm. It is to be expected that he will
do his best and will make full use of his large staff
toward advancing the interests of Japan.
MR. EIGO

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
5一
MR. BIGO FUKAI. Vice Governor of the Bank of Japan.
Mr. Fukai is understood to have officially the same
rank in the delegation as has Viscount Ishii. Educated
at Doshisha College in Kyoto, a Christian institution, he
served for several years as foreign editor of a Tokyo
daily newspaper. With a good command of English, he
ontered the Bank of Japan in 1905 as an interpreter. He
became in l928 Vice Governor of that institution. He was
a member of the Japanese delegations to the Versailles
Conference of l9l9, the Geneva Economic Conference of
1921, and the Washington Conference of 192l-1922.
Mr. Fukai is said to be "sound" in his financial
views and to have considerable influence in financial and
economic circles. As he has a thorough knowledge of the
oconomic situation in Japan, it is to be presumed that he
will supervise technical and financial conversations.
Mr. Fukai is 6l years old. He is of samurai lineage,
has a pleasing personality and is highly regarded in Japan,
ospecially in Christian educational circles.
THE

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.98)
一6-
THE POLITICAL SITUATION IN JAPAN.
The initiation of action in Manchuria by Japanese
military on September 18, l93l, marked the end of an era
in Japan and the beginning of another. Previous to that
date the affairs of Japan were in considerable measure
directed by a group of civilians among whom were a number
of liberal and internationally minded men who were striv-
ing to make Japan a great power in reality as well as in
name through such methods as the development of parlia-
mentary government, industrialization of the country, the
maintenance of the gold standard, and the conclusion of
and adherence to international agreements looking toward
disarmament and world peace.
The "military element", particularly junior officers
of the army, was dissatisfied with the methods and aims
of the civilians in control of the government. This ele-
ment regarded decreasing armaments as threatening to its
prestige. It was out of sympathy with the civilian
government's conciliatory policy toward China. It regarded
with great dissatisfaction indications of corruption in the
political parties and the unequal distribution of wealth
which gave certain financial and industrial interests great
influence in the government. The military, particularly the
junior officers, felt that the destiny of Japan could be
directed more ably by military men than by civilians.
On September 18, 1931, the army took matters into
their own hands and began the action which has since separated
Manchuria and Jehol from China and today threatens the
Peiping-Tientsin area. The liberal civilians of the government
were unable to impose any effective check on the military,
and shortly after the beginning of the inoident it could be
aa1a

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
seid with acouracy that the government of Tokyo was to all
intents and purposes & government of and by the military
element. There was great danger that junior officers of
the army would alter the government in form as well as
in fact. Party cabinets continued until May 15, 1932, when
the Prime Minister, Mr. Inukai, was assassinated. Subsequently
a cabinet in the nature of a "national" cabinet was formed.
This cabinet, which still continues in office (although its
days are said to be numbered), was regarded as the product
of a compromise between those junior officers of the army
who desired a super-party cabinet and who seemed to have been
working toward a dictatorship and those who adhered to the
principle of parliamentary government.
That the military could act without the knowledge or
direction of the civilian branohes of the government and
that the junior officers could act without authorization
from the higher military authorities were possible by
virtue of the right of direct access to the Emperor which
帷
certain military officers enjoy (that is, certain officers
幄
上奏
are permitted to enter the presence of the Emperor and ob-
tain his sanction of military policies and war plans with-
out the approval of or interference by any other organ of
the government). This right, and all that it implies, has
apparently had a profound effect on the psyohology of the
Japanese military and has caused the army to regard itself
as heir to a noble tradition and hereditary proteotor of
the Emperor's person and the Emperor's honor. The course
of action of the military derives from this right.
There is little or no evidence today that control of
the gevernment of Japan by the military element is weakening.
Mereever, there is evidence that the military forees
opereting on the Asistie mainland are not whelly under the
effeetive

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
offeetive control of the military authorities at Tokyo.
The mass of the Japanese people appear to be whole-
heartedly behind the military element. The liberals are
still, except in a very few instances and at rare inter-
vals, inarticulate. And the country is still in a state
of "war-mindedness".
RELATIONS

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
RELATIONS OF THE UNITED STATES WITH JAPAN.
A. Prior to September 18, 1931.
Since Japan's first treaty with the United States
(1854), which was the first treaty entered into by Japan
after her long period of isolation, the United States has
been interested in the progress and modernization of Japan.
For several decades following the signing of the Treaty of
l854 our attitude toward Japan was not unlike what it has
later been toward China. We favored the development of a
strong Japanese state; and the American attitude served as
a deterrent to possible foreign aggrandizement. The
United States led the way among the powers in showing
willingness to revise the unequal treaties which Japan had
entered into and by the revision of which Japan gained
tariff autonomy and the abolition of extraterritoriality.
From the Sino-Japanese War (1894) to the Russo-Japanese
War (1904), both the United States and Japan opposed
Russian aggression in Manchuria; and the United States and
Japan stood together on the "Open Door" policy. President
Theodore Roosevelt helped to bring the Russo-Japanese War
to an end, which was to the best interests of Japan, and
stood aside while Japan absorbed Korea (annexed in l9l0).
Following this period, however, interests of the
United States began to come into conflict with the interests
of Japan and there developed a gulf between the two nations.
Following the Chinese Revolution (19ll-l9l2) conditions in
China became more and more chaotic. This caused Japan to
become correspondingly more concerned about its interests
in Chine. Then eame the World War, during whioh Japan
became one of the leading industrial nations of the world.
Fesed by the need of new markets and sourees of raw materials,
disturbod

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
disturbed by unstable political conditions in China, and
confident that the nations were wholly occupied by the
World War, Japan presented to China the notorious "Twenty-one
Demands" (1915). These "Demands" in their original form
provided for almost complete Japanese military and political
domination of China, and were in defiance of the generally
agreed upon international policy toward China.
The United States undertook to liquidate the Far
Eastern situation in connection with the Washington Con-
ference (1921-1922), which was called to stabilize
political conditions in the Far East and, coincident with
and depending upon this, to effect naval disarmament. As
a result of the conference, the interested nations signed
the Nine Power Treaty and subscribed to the resolutions
with regard to policy in China; the Japanese came to an
understanding with China with regard to Shantung (the
Gorman possession there - Kiaochao - had been taken by
Japan in fulfillment of her obligations under the Anglo-
Japanese Alliance); and the Four Power Treaty, ending
Japan's alliance with Great Britain, was signed. A naval
agreement was entered into which definitely put an end
for a period of years to the naval rivalry growing out of
a disturbed political situation. For a period of years a
feeling of good will and confidence existed among the
Powers and it appeared that the gulf between the United
States and Japan had appreciably narrowed.
This feeling of good will and confidence was based,
however, in so far as Japan was concerned, on the war-
ranted assumption that the situstion in the Far Bast
would be treated as & whole and progress in naval limite-
tion would proceed with the proper consideration of condi-
tiene in the Far Bast.
By the time the London Naval Conference (19s0) wes
oa11od

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
ealled, Russis had emerged again in the Far East as an
important elment in the situation and the internal con-
aitions in Chine had been growing steadily worse. The
London Conference, however, took no account of the
political situation in the Far East. This created a
feeling of uneasiness in Japan where there developed a
belief that the London Treaty had no spiritual value and
did not touch fundamentals, thereby breeding distrust.
(The recent Geneva Disarmament Conference has had a similar
result.)
These problems, however, although fundamentally of
paramount importance in American-Japanese relations, were
international in aspect and could have been met only by
international cooperation. The problems which affected
only Japan and the United States, and which called for
adjustment, were seemingly few in number. Brief comment
with regard to them is given below, as follows:
1. Immigration.
Among these few problems, the outstanding one was
that of our discrimination against the Japanese (and other
Orientals) with regard to immigration. The "Japanese ex-
clusion" provision in the Immigration Act of l924 created
resentment on the part of the Japanese against the United
States and that resentment still exists in Japan and is
still a factor to be reckoned with. The resentment on the
part of the Japanese arises not from the fact that Japanese
immigrants are exeluded but from the fact that they regard
as obnoxious the method of selection, it being based on
distinetions between races and implying that the races
which are debarred are "inferior". The Government of the
United States has not been able to move toward improving
this situation -- for the reason that any move on the part
of

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
of this Government would, under existing conditions, arouse
opposition on the Pacific Coast and among its representatives
in Congress whioh opposition would occasion discussion of a
type which would be sure to cause unfortunate reperoussions
in American-Japanese relations. Until it can be foreseen
that a move for amendment of the "Japanese exclusion"
provision in our Immigration Act would be assured of success
in Congress, it appears advisable to let this "sleeping dog
11e".
2. Crab Industry.
A less important question, namely the possibility of
Japanese fishing interests in Alaskan waters seriously
injuring the American salmon industry, has been for some
years a subject of disoussion between representatives of the
two Governments. This threat to the American salmon industry
arises from the fact that those Japanese interests which are
engaged in the catching of crabs in Alaskan waters are finding
the industry decreasingly profitable, which causes them to
turn their attention toward salmon. Unless this situation
is altered -- and any improvement could come about apparently
only through some conoession on the part of the United States,
such as, for example, a reduction of the duty on crab meat --
there is danger that the Japanese fishing interests may
engage in the catching (and canning) of other kinds of fish.
This might mean the ruin of the American salmon industry in
Alaska and on the West Coast. Although a reduction of the
duty on crab meat would not adversely affect American
industries, the difficulty in the way of negotiating a settle-
ment of this question appears to lie in the inability of the
Amerioan Government to promise definitely to the Japanese
during negotiations a reduction of the tariff rates.
3.Fur

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
3. Fur Seal Industry.
Another problem, at present a minor one, arises from
a difference of opinion with regard to the fur seal industry
in the north Pacific.
The fur seal industry in the north Pacific is governed
by the Convention between the United States, Great Britain,
Russia and Japan for the Preservation and Protection of Fur
Seals (l9ll). This convention provides inter alia for
limiting the number of seals which may be killed annually and
for payment to Japan of 15 per cent of the proceeds derived
from the sale of the skins of American seals killed each
year. The Japanese have expressed a desire for a modifica-
tion of this convention so that the number of seals may be
reduced, it being apparently their belief that the Japanese
herd (on Robben Island) has reached such proportions that it
is becoming a menace to the fishing industry off the Japanese
coast. The Japanese have also contended in the past that
American seals (from the Pribiloff Islands) invade waters
where fishing by Japanese interests is carried on, to the
injury of those interests. (The accuracy of this latter
contention has been denied by experts of this Government.)
The Japanese have also expressed a desire to receive more
than 15 per cent of the annual proceeds from the sale of the
skins of American seals.
At present this question is not under disoussion
between representatives of the two governments but it may
recur at some future time. It is possible that, in negoti-
ations with regard to the Japanese crab industry in Alaskan
waters, the Amerioan Government might offer to consent to a
modification of the Fur Seal Convention in order to dissuade
Japanese interests from activities which would be detrimental
to the American salmon industry.
4. Visits

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
4. Visits of Government Vessels.
Since the acquisition by Japan of a mandate over those
islands in the Pacific which were formally German posses-
sions, the Japanese Government has been unwilling to grant
permission to vessels of this Government (and of other
governments) to visit islands under mandate other than those
where there are Japanese officials. The Japanese insist
that visits of foreign vessels to islands where Japanese
officials are not present would be disturbing to the natives.
Although the Japanese may have a feeling that it would be
impolite on their part not to be able to welcome officially
a vessel of a foreign government visiting any region where
Japanese are charged with administering the government, it
seems probable that a more impelling reason is that the
Japanese, regarding the islands as of strategic value, wish
to prevent foreign governments from obtaining information
about them.
Until very recently vessels of the Japanese Government
have, upon request, always been granted permission to visit
points in the Aleutian and Pribiloff Islands, with the ex-
ception of Kiska which is a closed port. On May 6 of this
year, however, following a request for permission for a
training ship of the Tokyo Fishing Institute to visit the
Aleutian and Pribiloff Islands, as well as certain ports on
the West Coast and in the territory of Hawaii, permission
was withheld with regard to the Aleutian and Pribiloff
Imlands on the grounds that it would be inconvenient to make
adequate arrangements for a visit at this time. This reply
was made at the instance of the Navy Department which urged
that further opportunity should not be given the Japanese to
obtain information of strategic value with regard to the
Aleutian and Pribiloff Islands as long as visits to the
Japanoso

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
Japanese Mandate Islands were restricted.
5.011.
Very recently a situation has developed in Tokyo which
may become a question between the two governments. According
to a report received on May 8, 1933, from the Ambassador at
Tokyo, the Mining Bureau of the Japanese Department of
Commerce and Industry recently gave to the press certain
tentative plans with regard to the oil industry in Japan.
Under these plans it is tentatively proposed to establish
either (a) a system of governmental control, through the
licensing of importation and refining of oil, or (b) an
oil monopoly. Under the former the oil industry and trade
could eventually, if desired, be operated exclusively by
Japanese companies. (This plan also envisages obtaining
crude oil from new sources, that is, from sources other than
American or British.) Under the latter proposal, the Govern-
ment would buy up the existing properties in Japan of the
foreign oil companies.
In case either of these proposals is adopted, the
foreign oil companies in Japan could expect to be driven
oventually from the field (except in the sale of crude oil).
Either would necessitate the abandonment of distributing
plants and organizations in that country. One of the two
principal foreign oil companies in Japan is the Socony-Vacuum
Corporation (the other being the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
belonging to the British Shell in terests) which has very
large investments in Japan. This company has been attempting
for some time to obtain a statement of policy from the
Japanese Government in order to determine whether to make
further investments in Japan and how to shape its future
plans. The American Ambassador (as well as the British
Ambassedor and Duteh Minister) has already informally expressed
to

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.98)
-16-
to the Foreign Office the hope that the Japanese Government
would give some indication with regard to future develop-
monto.
The publication in the press of these plans may pos-
sibly have been made at the present time to afford a point
for bargaining at the Monetary and Economic Conference. It
may possibly have been a trial balloon to gauge the reaction
of foreigners. It is possible, however, that the Japanese
may desire to discourage, until the Japanese Government is
in position to determine its future policy, further expansion
on the part of the foreign oil companies. While not denying
that such plans exist, the Commercial Bureau of the Japanese
Foreign Office has disclaimed knowledge of the plans as
published in the press.
This question, information with regard to which has only
very recently been received, is now receiving the careful
consideration of the Department.
