B. Since September 18, 1931.
Since the initiation of Japanese military action in
Manchuria in September, 193l, the United States has been
primarily concerned in its relations with Japan with the
conflict between American policy and Japanese policy in
relation (a) to China and (b) to the peace movement and the
existing machinery in relation thereto (although the
immigration question has, of course, not been ignored).
Prevailing opinion in the United States, official and unofficial,
regards the principles which were agreed upon in the Nine Power
Treaty in regard to China as sound, both in theory and for
practice, while prevailing opinion in Japan regards those
principles as unsound and is giving full support to a set of
principles which are antithetical to them, both in theory and
in practice; and prevailing opinion in the United States
believes in and is giving support to the principles of the
peace movement and the machinery thereof, while prevailing
opinion in Japan is skeptical and defiant of those principles
and that machinery.
In its relations with the Japanese Government this
Government has been hampered by the fact that the Japanese
Government is controlled by a military clique which has so
far mhown no inclination to compromise in any way with regard
to its policies outlined above. Moreover, that clique has been
decidedly and persistently truculent -- in its attitude toward
and relations with all of the Powers, especially the United
States. At the same time the American Government has been
hampered by the probability that any communication which it
might make to the Japanese Government no matter how confidential
would shortly thereafter be given out by Japanese anthorities
(in Japan) to the press and usually in distorted form. The
position

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
position of this Government has also been rendered difficult
by the fact that hostility to the United States has been
engendered in Japan through propaganda -- sometimes directly
and sometimes indirectly attributable to the Japanese military.
Relations today are therefore strained and there is talk,
especially in Japan, of possible war between the two countries.
This is not the first time, however, in the history of the
relations of the United States and Japan that war talk has
existed. During the past twenty-five years the possibility
of war between Japan and the United States has been widely
envisaged in Japan or in the United States or in both
countries -- especially in 1907, 1913, 1915, 1920, 1924 and
at the present time. In 1907, 1913, 1915 and 1924 the
discussions arose out of the general question of the treat-
ment of Japanese nationals in the United States with regard
either to disqualifications imposed by certain States on
Japanese or to the entry of Japanese into the United States.
The idea of war was largely cultivated by reactionary and
irresponsible elements in Japan as a means of remedying an
alleged affront to Japan, but it was never until now seriously
supported by the controlling elements in Japan; while a large
number, perhaps even a majority, of persons in this country
felt that Japan had in the matter of the treatment accorded
in this country to persons of Japanese race a legitimate
grievance. A climax over questions of this nature was reached
in l924, and despite the chauvinism which developed in certain
quarters the strain passed off without undue tension. The
possibility of war had been more widely envisaged in l920, due
in part to the provisions of the Treaty of Versailles which
sought to regularize the occupation by Japan of Shantung. It
will be recalled that feeling ran high in this country as
well as in Japan following the agitation for the rendition by
Japan

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.98)
-19-
Jepan of this Chinese province to China. A similar situation
exists today. A part of China has been occupied by Japan,
sympathy in this country is largely with the Chinese (and the
League of Nations) in the demand for the evacuation of this
territory by Japan, the Japanese people regard the United
States as the villain of the play and are therefore aroused
against this country; and there are indications that the
American people are gradually becoming resentful in response
to the resentment which is felt -- and which is cultivated --
toward the United States by various Japanese official and
unofficial agencies.

PROBLEMS
INTRODUCTORY STATEMENT.
The Embassy at Tokyo reported, under date April 19,
1933, that prior to the receipt of the President's invita-
tion to the preliminary conversations neither the Japanese
Government nor the Japanese public showed any considerable
interest in the Monetary and Economic Conference. This was
due to the fact that the attention of the Japanese has been
absorbed in Far Eastern political rather than world economic
problems. Japan has not suffered from the present economic
depression to nearly so great a degree as have most other
nations. The War debt problem does not directly concern Japan.
The disarmament question is not of great interest to the
Japanese as they do not intend to disarm to any greater
extent than may be absolutely necessary to prevent a breakdown
of international amity. The fact that the subjects of the
lowering of tariffs and the stabilization of exchange will be
discussed at the conference does not arouse their enthusiasm --
a point which will be disoussed more in detaillater in this
memorandum. The general attitude of the Japanese nation was
that, as Japan is already getting along very well as compared
with other nations for the reason that she is in fact at this
time enjoying a "boom", there was no special reason why they
should be particularly interested in this conference.
Following the receipt of the President's invitation,
however, a different attitude was manifested. The Japanese
were relieved and gratified, for the reason that the invita-
tion indieated that Japan was not to be internationally
iselated ("outlawed"), as they had mere or less feared when
they withdrew from the League of Nations, and they felt that
tho

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
-21-
the preliminary conversations et Washington would afford
them an opportunity to bring into disoussion their views of
the political situation in the Far East.
It is possible that the Japanese delegation may attempt
to bring up political questions, especially those relating to
the Manohuria situation. The facts that Viscount Ishii has
little or no special knowledge of economic or financial questions
and that the Japanese do not appear to be affirmatively
interested in the economic aspects of the approaching conversa-
tions would seem to indicate this. Viscount Ishii, however,
has stated to press representatives that he will not bring up
the Manchuria question unless it be introduced by the
President.

Subjeots Primarily Economic in Character.
1. Foreign Exchange.
Japan imposed in December, 193l, an embargo on the
export of gold. Subsequently the foreign exchange value
of the yen declined, at its lowest level, by about 60
per cent. (Gold par is $0.4985, and the exchange rate at
present is about $0.24, while the lowest level has been
approximately $0.20.)
This decline has not been of great consequence in
the sum total of the direct trade between the United
States and Japan as the higher yen prices which Japan
receives for her exports have been largely set off by the
higher yen cost of the raw cotton which she buys from us.
In certain countries, and in regard to certain kinds of
commodities in the trade with the United States, however,
the low yen has given Japan a marked price advantage. The
yen value of Japan's total exports increased in 1932 by
23 per cent over that of 1931.
Japan has many interests which render stability of
exchange desirable. The extensive foreign trade of Japan,
for example, makes it necessary for her large foreign
oxchange banks to maintain large dollar balances in the
United States and large pound balances in Great Britain.
Japan is & manufacturing nation, obtaining abroad much of
the raw material for her manufacture and disposing of her
manufactured articles in foreign markets. In commenting on
the question of exchange, Mr. Eigo Fukai stated recently to
the Tokyo oorrespondent of the NEW YORK HERALD TRIBUNE that
it was his belief that a low foreign exchange value of &
nation's ourrenoy does not serve its interests in the long
run and that & return to the gold standard should be striven
for.
whilo

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
-23-
While no official or unofficial pronouncement on the
question has been made, it is doubted, however, whether
Japan's financial and mercantile circles are as yet in
sympathy with the idea of stabilizing international
mediums of exchange, as Japanese manufacturers and exporters
for over a year have been enjoying the advantage of the
depreciated yen exchange in the markets of the world; and
they will not willingly forego this advantage.
It is believed that, with regard to other currency
problems, Japan will be favorably disposed toward cooperation
in plans to restore the purchasing power of silver, to adjust
specie holdings and to insure the free circulation of
capital.

A. Subjects Primarily Economio in Character.
2. Tariffs.
Mr. Fukai was reported to have stated in the same
interview that Japan was prepared to discuss tariffs on a
basis of reciprocity. There are not, however, tariff
issues of great importance between the United States and
Japan. This is indicated by the facts that in 1932 in
our imports from Japan 80 per cent was raw silk (which
enters this country free of duty) and that in that same
year in our exports to Japan 63 per cent was cotton (which
enters Japan without duty). Tariff discussions would,
therefore, relate to less important articles of trade,
such as lumber, petroleum products, machinery, iron and
steel, chemicals, and hides and skins, which are exported
from the United States to Japan; and canned comestibles,
potteries, toys, tea, lamps, dried plants, silk and
artificial silk textiles, and hats and caps, which we
buy from Japan. The most recent instance of complaint on
the part of Americans against the Japanese customs tariff
occurred in l931 when the rates on certain kinds of lumber
were raised. The Japanese market for certain American
produots has been narrowed very materially by a tariff
amendment adopted by Japan in 1924 by which an ad valorem
rate of lo0 per cent on goods classified as "luxuries"
was made effeotive.
It is of interest to note that the estimate of revenue
from customs duties in the Japanese budget for the fiscal
year ending March 31, 1933, is ¥129,000,000, which is one-
tenth of the total ordinary revenue of the Japanese Govern-
ment for that year. (The revenue from customs duties in
the budget of 1932 of the United States was $327,500,000,
whioh

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)

whioh is approximately l7 per cent of the total revenues.)
As Japan's exports other than raw silk are principally
manufactured goods, it might be expected that Japan would
be in favor of lowered tariff barriers. It is believed,
however, that Japanese industrialists are opposed to a
lowering of Japan's tariff walls, which are fairly high.
It is anticipated that Japan will approve in principle
the polioy of general reductions of import tariffs but will
claim that, as Japan is still operating under the Import
Tariff Law of 19lo and as Japanese tariff rates are low in
comparison with those of most of the other principal commercial
countries, Japan cannot envisage a reduction of its tariff
schedule on the same basis as other countries but would be
willing to consider reciprocal tariff conventions. Mr. Fukai
has stated that "which countries can lower their tariffs and
in respeot to what commodities are matters for negotiations
between the countries concerned".*
It
The above suggested claim that, as Japan is still using

the Customs Tariff Law promulgated on April 14, 19lo, is

almost always in an upward direction -- that little of the

1930 and 193i. In addition, in 1924 the so-called "Luxury

on some l2o lines of merchandise, including such necessi-

Moreover, in l932, to compensato for the decreased value
of the yen in coliection of duties, all specific duties
which were not raised by specified amounts were inoreased
by 35 per cent. If a comparison should be made between
the original l9lo sohedule of import duties and the one
now in force, it is believed that it would be found that
the rates on the majority of the items have been increased
very considerably since i9lo.

B. Subiects Primarily Political in Character.
1. Recognition of Japan's Position on the
Asiatic Mainland.
As has already been suggested, it is possible that
Viscount Ishii may attempt to enter into discussion with
regard to questions arising from the Manchuria situation.
If he does, it may be presumed that his object will be to
obtain a relaxation of the policy of non-recognition and/or
some acknowledgment of Japan's special position with regard
to the Asiatic continent.
a. "Asiatic Monroe Doctrine".
It will be recalled that in an address on June 21,
1932, at a dinner given by the Japan America Society at
Tokyo in honor of Ambassador Grew, Viscount Ishii made
some statements which received wide publicity and which
gave rise to the report that Viscount Ishii was in favor
of the so-called "Asiatic Monroe Doctrine". Among other
things he said:
"In my opinion an armed conflict between our
countries is possible only in the two extremely
improbable contingencies. In the first place if
Japan were foolish enough to attempt unduly to
interfere with the matter of the Western hemisphere
then a war would become inevitable. .....In the
second place if the U. S. ever attempted to domi-
nate the Asiatic continent and prevented Japan from
her pacific and natural expansion in this part of
the world, then indeed a grave situation would be
created, for it is obvious that a nation with a
population over-crowded to the breaking point could
not be shut up indefinitely in a narrow strip of
land."
"Every nation has its own interests to look
after and its own sphere of activity. The vast
majority of the intelligent Americans and Japa-
nese know perfectly well where their respective
national interests lie and where stops their
respective center of activity beyond which pure
common sense forbids them from exposing their
flesh and bones for futile and vain purposes."
In

above-mentioned dinner was erroneously called a "Monroe
Dootrine for Asia"; that what he really meant was that the
United States has no real interests in the Far East aside
from the maintenance of the Open Door and equal opportunity
in China and American trade in the Pacific, and that the
United States should, therefore, leave other questions of
▲siatic concern to Asiatics to settle among themselves.

other Japanese, especially some of the leading publicists,
have talked for years about a "Japanese Monroe Doctrine
for Asia" or a doctrine of "Asia for Asiatics".

b. The Lansing-Ishii Agreement.
In connection with any speculation with regard to
what Viscount Ishii may say during his approaching visit
to Washington, it is well worth while to have in mind the
history of the conversations which were held between
Viscount Ishii and the then American Secretary of State,
Mr. Lansing, in 1917 and the Agreement concluded in con-
sequence and termination of those conversations known as
the Lansing-Ishii Agreement. Those conversations began
on September 6, 1917, and, with fourteen conferences, re-
sulted in the conclusion on November 2 and the signing of
an exchange of notes. (NOTE: In this connection there
was also a transaction of a strictly secret character --
the nature of which is known to and can be explained by
the undersigned.) Throughout these conversations Viscount
Ishii, under instructions from his Government, endeavored
to maneuver Mr. Lansing into the making of commitments
which, had they been made, would have meant considerable
relaxing of American pressure on behalf of the Open Door
and the integrity of China principles of American Far
Eastern policy and a corresponding unfettering of Japan
from restraints upon her freedom of action with reference
to China established during the preceding twenty years by
and in consequence of that pressure. Japan sought an
affirmation on the part of the United States of a
"paramount influence" on the part of Japan in China. It
had been and was the policy of the Uni ted States to admit
no greater right on Japan's part than that of "special
interests" in China. The result, after great persistence
on Vimcount Ishii's part and some participation by Presi-
dent Wilson, was & compromise in which Japan sacrificed
nothing and the American Government made an affirmation
whish Japan was able immediately to make use of in her
on

own way and for her own purposes: Japan reaffirmed adher-
enee to the principles of the Open Door policy; but the
United States subscribed to a formula which was phrased,
"that territorial propinquity creates special relations
between countries, and, consequently, the Government of
the United States recognizes that Japan has special interests
in China, particularly in the part to which her possessions
are contiguous". Japan immediately saw to it that there
was disseminated in the Far East, especially in China, a
version of the exohange of notes in which the phrase
"special interests" was translated "paramount influence".
For five years, the Lansing-Ishii Agreement hung as
more or less of a cloud over American diplomatic effort in
the contest between American policy and Japanese policy in
reference to China. That agreement was ultimately, after
the Washington Conference, "scrapped", on the theory that,
the policies of all the powers having been agreed upon in
the Nine Power Treaty, there was no longer need for such
a special and bilateral declaration regarding Japanese and
American "China policy". (NOTE: In this connection also,
there is need for reference to the character of the secret
transaction connected -- "on the side" -- with the signing
of the Lansing-Ishii notes.)

o. The Open Door in Manchuria.
In case Viscount Ishii attempts to obtain a relaxing
of the American policy toward "Manohukuo", he will probably
claim that Japanese activities do not and will not inter-
fere with the Open Door.
It is the belief of many interested Americans that
the policy of the Open Door in Manchuria has already been
seriously infringed by Japanese activities both before and
since September 18, 193l. American trade with Manchuria
seriously declined during 1931 and 1932, and the decline
cannot be wholly explained by reference to the world-wide
depression. It is clear that Japanese control in Manchuria
has been an important contributing factor.
Professions of adherence to the Open Door policy in
Manchuria continue to be made periodically by officials of
the Japanese Government. The most recent such statement
was made to Ambassador Grew by the Japanese Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs (Tokyo's telegram No. 89 of May 3,
1933) who authorized the Ambassador to inform this Govern-
ment that 'the principle of the Open Door in "Manchukuo"
would be strictly maintained'. This statement was oc-
casioned by press reports to the effect that a Japanese
who is a Privy Councillor in the "Manchukuo" régime had
asserted that "Manchukuo's" promise of an open door and
oqual opportunity presupposed reciprocity and consequently
was applicable only to countries recognizing "Manchukuo"
and had declared that henceforth this principle would be
onforoed.
Although officials of the Japanese Government continue
to give assurances that the Open Door will be maintained, the
fest remains that during l932 the direct importations from
America into Dairen (the port through which practically all
American

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
American products enter Manchuria) decreased in value by 60
per cent as compared with American exports into Dairen during
the preceding year; and that during l932 the imports from the
Japanese Empire into Dairen increased 30 per cent over those
of 193l and formed 65 per cent of the grand total of imports
into Dairen.* Apparently the trade of other countries has been
similarly affected. Within the past few days there have been
press reports that several British firms -- the only two
named in the reports being the Hong Kong and Shanghai Banking
Corporation and the Jardine-Matheson Company (both very
important firms) -- have instructed their staffs to prepare
to close their branches in Manchuria. According to a
correspondent of the United Press, a director of the above-
mentioned bank stated that the reason for the step was the
rapid closing of the Open Door by the present régime in
"Manchukuo".
In local business, Japanese goods and firms enjoy in
Manchuria a specially advantageous position by reason of
the honey-combing of all departments of administration and
of all large enterprises by all-powerful Japanese "advisers"
and supervisors.
In public contracts and development projects, Japanese
equipment and Japanese contractors are advantageously situated
owing to the directing and execution of all such work by
government purehasing and engineering departments staffed
almost exclusively by Japanese.
American trade and other commercial interests in
Manchuria had depended largely upon the custom of the local
(Chinese) government and of large enterprises for their
business.
Based on returns of the government of the Kwantung Leased
Territory.

business. The taking over of control by Japanese
authorities has seriously injured them and even threatens
their existence in competitive fields.
According to a statement given recently to the

"oommercial relations between the United States and Man-

occupation of Manchuria has adversely affected American
interests there.

APWISABILITY OF CAUTION IN CONVERSATIONS
There have been evidences in the past which indieate
that, following conversations with officials of other
countries, Japanese spokesmen are inclined subsequently
to diselose in terms of their own the substanee of those
conversations. Mention has already been made of the fact
that, following the Lansing-Ishii Agreement of l9l7, this
agreement was interpreted (and even translated) by the
Japanese in a way not in accord with the interpretation
pleced on it by Mr. Lansing himself before, when and
after he had signed it.
According to information received from the Embassy at
London, an instance of similar distortion recently oc-
eurred there. In l928, British and Japanese officials
reached an informal agreement that Great Britain and
Japan should maintain olose contact through constant com-
munications and consultations between their respective
ministers at Peiping with regard to developments in China,
as relations between Great Britain and Japan were based
on the obligations of full and frank communications specified
in the Weshington Conference Nine Power Treaty of 1922, On
Mareh 8, 1933, a member of the British Government informed
the Counselor of the American Eabassy at London that the
Japanese had informally "invoked" this understanding. This
aetion wes epparently taken by the Jepanese because of
Jepem's umeomfortable position vis-à-vis the states members
of the Leegue arising out of the League's consideration

A few months ago Viscount Kaneko, a prominent Japanese,

THE JAPANESE PEREPECTIVE AND OUTLOOK.
The authore of this memorandum realise that they have
given an account in the course of which they emphasize
the negative or defensive side of the attitude which it
seems necessary or advisable that the Administration take
in dealing with Japan's representatives at this time:
they find little to offer of a constructive or positive
character. They have suggested that in conversations with
Tiscount Ishii and his stafr the rule of great caution
be observed. In doing this, it is by no means their desire
to impute to this Japanese spokesman and his staff any
laek of integrity. Viseount Ishii is in their opinion an
able, a high-minded Japanese gentleman. Certain facts,
however, must not be overlooked and must oonstantly be in
mind. No Oriental nation has today the perspeetive and
the outlook of the more advanced occidental nations, and
Japan is no exception. The Japanese state is still essentially
feudal in the fundamentals of its political and social
organization. The temperament and psychology of the Japanese race
are comparable, by way of illustrative approximation, to those
of the Prussians. They believe thoroughly in the right of
might and therefore the use of foree. Although their
diplemats have been induced -- sometims with conviction,
scmotimes under pressure of persuasion -- to place their
sigmetures upon and their Government to ratify the various
moderm treaties which place restrietions upon methods of
direet action in prosecution of national poliey, the
matiema as a whole have little eonception of and little
sympethy toward the principles underlying end the provisions
witton

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.98)
-41-
written into those treaties. In no other country is the
pirit of nationelism more intense and in none are the
rosesses of chauvinism more active than in Japan today.
of that race, and coming from that environment, no Japae
mese statesman can possibly approach problems of general
international relations in the same spirit or with the
same intent with which representatives of a country so
situated as the United States and a people so thinking
as the American people approach them. Moreover, the Japa-
nese have e highly paternelistic government. In Japan
there is no laisses faire. The Government gives aid and
instructions and orders to its nationals; and it asks and
gets from them loyalty, obedience, and, for the most part,
cooperation and concerted action. Yet, just at this moment,
control in this rigidly organized state has been seized
and is completely in the hands of that element in the
mation which believes in positive or forceful action.
That seisure of control by that element not only is as-
quieseed in but has now the whole-hearted support of the
great majority of the people. Notwithstanding all this,
it hes been observed that among those officials of Japan
who heve had a considerable amount of diplomatic service --
and mo thus have gained a substantial knowledge of wrld
polities end an understanding of occidental thought and
ideals so there is a not inconsiderable group who for
some time at least were out of sympathy with the poliey
wmderlying Japan's edventures on the ontinent of Asie
and who believe that Japen's course during the pest two
yeers in intermational relations has been in the wrong
aireetien. This group hes attempted to prevent complete
Bevesemee of ties between Japan and the more modern Oecident.
Ito

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.97)
-42-
Its members have worked assiduously to put their country's
eourse in the best light possible abroed. Partly in con-
sequence of their efforts, considerable portions of the
publie in some occidental nations have with regard to the
real situation in Japan and in China and between the two
countries impressions which are by no means in accurate
perspective. For practical purposes, the ruling element
in Japan today is proceeding according to one rule only,
that of expedieney (according to its own lights). For
prinoiples of international law and provisions of treaties
it has, expept where these may be used to Japan's ad-
vantage, no respect and no use. Such is today the country
which Viseount Ishii will be representing. And by these
factors there must in large measure be determined his ap-
proach to and his efforts in regard to the questions with
which he will be concerned.

PARAPHRASE
Telegram dated May 17, 1933, from the American Ambas-
sedor at Tokyo, reads substantially as follows:
With regard to the Embassy's despatch No. 365 of
April 19, and its telegram No. 79 of April 13, which refer
to the Japanese attitude toward the conversations at
Washington preliminary to the Monetary and Economic Con-
ference, -
No detailed instructions have as yet been approved
and issued to the Japanese delegation, according to
information supplied to the American Embassy by the
Ministry for Foreign Affairs. It is probable, however,
that the Foreign Office will receive this week, for ap-
proval, a detailed compilation of general poliey which has
been prepared by the interested governmental departments.
The mbassy's understanding is that the delegation must
refer each proposal to Tokyo for approval, as it has no
authority to bind Japan with regard to any question. Based
on information obtained from various sources with regard to
the trend of opinion among the departments of the Govern-
ment, the belief of the mbassy is that the general poliey
to be followed at the preliminary conversations at Washing-
tem and at the conference at London is as indicated by the
following:
It appears that the Japanese, as will be seen, are
willing to consider in the light of the best interests of
Jepan any preposal which may be made but that they do not
heve any construetive preposals to offer. A statement re-
perted in the press to have been made by Mr. Pakei, a
celegate, just before leaving for Washington, sums up
their

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.97)
8-
their general attitude:
"there are two aspects to international
eeoperation. There is uneonditional cooperation
to promote the common interests of the world.
There is cooperation to further the interests of
any one nation. I hold to the latter viewpoint.
In erder to promote Japan's interests, Japan
will cooperate with other nations on certain
points."
A. Questions of a Political Character.
1. Implementation of the Kellogg Paet.
The Japanese view with alarm any attempt to implement
the Kellogg Paot because of their experience with the
League of Mations. The Japanese are afraid of the reaction
on Japan of a consultative pact which provides for the
definition of an aggressor. The same ciroumstance which
brought about the Japanese break with the League of
Nations, it is claimed, will cause them to accept a con-
sultative past only with reservations which exelude from
its scope Far Bastern Affairs.
2. The so-called "Monroe Doctrine for Asia".
An effort to obtain recognition of Japan as the
"guardian of the peace of Asia" is advocated by Viscount
Ishii, rather than efforts to establish an "Asiatic Monroe
Doetrine" or to renew the Lansing-Ishii agreement. Ob-
servers believe, although it is not known whether the
Government favers Ishii's views, that some attempt will be
made to establish the doctrine that Asiatic affairs should
not be interfered with by Western powers.
5. Armaments.
Under present eonditions the Japanese have no wish to
disarm (Reference, Bmbassy's despatch No. 565). It may be
their intemtion to consider disarmement in conneetion with
the preeeding peregraph. They will probably insist upon a
meview of Fer Restern pelitieal questiens in conneetion
with

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.97)
-5-
with disarmement, as they are inclined to regard disarmament
as a political problem.
B. Questions of an Bconomic Character.
1. Credit. the Gold Standard, the Exohange Rate, and
Silver.
The Japanese favor, in principle, a return to the gold
standard and the stabilisation of ourrenoy. It is alleged,
however, that the accomplishment of these ends will be
difficult for Japan for the reasons that Japanese ourreney
is weak and the gold reserve small. Assistance from the
United States in the form of exchange operations or credits
would, therefore, be required for stabilisation of exehange,
while redistribution of gold reserves among the nations
would be required for a return to the gold standard. Desir-
ing to avoid bringing the yen back - in terms of the gold
dollar - to its former level, the Japanese apparently would
prefer to stabilise it in the vioinity of twenty cents, al-
though, if nothing better could be effeeted, they might
compromise at twenty-five cents. Rather than fix an
artifisial high rate, the Japanese would prefer to let the
yen find its owm level in international trade, altering the
gold content eventually to conform in that regard. The
Japanese would like an arrangement whereby interest on
Jmpenese Government bonds issued in foreign countries would
be paid mot in gold but in the depreciated currencies of
the countries where issued or in paper yen. The Japanese
do mot regerd the fixing of the relative value of silver to
gold as feasible, although plans for raising the value of
silver nd for using silver for specie reserves are
generally approved.
2. Riees.
The Japanese are in favor of plans to raise, by means
of

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
of mere liberal international and domestic cireulation of
ourreney end credit, the level of commodity priees.
3. International Movement of Capital.
The Bank of Japan will cooperate with other central
banks to obtain free international movement of capital, an
objeetive which the Japanese are very desirous of attaining.
4. Restrictions on Trade.
The removal of existing or threatened restrictions on
the trade of Japan, which the depreciation of the yen has
caused (such as anti-dumping tariffs or import quotas), is
greatly desired by the Japanese. As a consequence, it is
anticipated that the mitigation or abolition of such
restrictions will be approved by them in principle, but
they will ask for consideration of speeial conditions which
exist in each country. They are unwilling, for example, to
abolish those restrictions which they have always maintained
on the importation of rice in order to regulate the price of
domestio rice.
5. Customs Duties.
The Japanese approve, in prinoiple, a reasonable lower-
ing of tariff barriers. However, they are opposed, on the
ground that their tariff level is already comparatively low,
to a general flat reduction of tariffs. The Japanese are in
favor of reciprocal tariff conventions, either bilateral or
multilateral.
6. Organisation of Trade and Production.
The Japanese are aware of the desirability of readjust-
ing the international production and consumption of com-
modities but they do not, because of the peculiar conditions
whish obtain in eaeh country, regard the ascomplishment of
this by international convention as feasible.

The improvement in the export trade of Japan in 1932
was due to the fact that in the summer of that year Japan's
economic situation improved to such a degree that during
the latter half of 1932 she enjoyed an industrial boom.
Commodity prices rose, company share values increased, some
faetories worked on long schedules, and the principal
industries were more prosperous than they had been for some
years. This prosperity was the result of the depreciation
of the yen, which gave the Japanese an important advantage
in expert trade and which, by inereasing the prices of im-
ported gools (in yen) permitted the manufasture in Japan
of preduets which had been formerly imported, of the demand
for military sapplies arising out of the armed confliet

ANNEK III.
SOuPGO: WASHINGTON DAILY NEWS,
April 23, 1955.
PEOPLE AND POLITICS
By Ray Tucker
The selection of Viscount Kikijairo Ishii as Japan's
representative at the Roosevelt economic conferences, and
subsequently at the June world parley in London, is of
extraordinary significance.
The Oriental power has, clearly, cast about carefully
to diseover the ideal man to voice Japan's cause at her
first re-entry into an international assemblage since she
stomped out of the League of Nations in protest against
the Lytton report condemning her invasion of Manchuria.
So she is sending the "grand old man of Japanese
diplomaoy," one of her eldest statesmen and privy councilors,
and a figure of treat prestige in occidental countries.
Sixty-eight years old, Viscount Ishii has held prominent
posts in Japan's Foreign Service since the days when his
country was only & straggling, struggling island. He has
been a key figure in every war, every negotiation, every
opisode that have combined to raise his country from a
fourth-rate to a world power.
Faces Diffioult Task
How, in the evening of his life, he may face his most
arduous and difficult task. Not that Japan will sit around
the White House or London table in an apologetio mood. On
the contrary, whe will resent introduotion of problems
which she considers purely domestic. She knows, of course,
that western mentiment is critical of her recent Manchurian
and shenghai ventures and it will require all the arts of
diplemecy to steer a straight course thru international
waters she has helped to muddy.
Japan needs American good will and trade; she needs
our cotton and mast sell us raw silk. This oountry is her
best oustomer, as she is our third best. So she has a
sreat stake in stabilisation of exehange and the free flow
of trade. In a military sense, she may dominate the land
and waters ef the Rising Sun; in & commercial sense, she is
dependent upon American markets.
Jepan is eemperetively fortunate at the present time.
Only 500,000 ef a population ef 70,000,000 are unempleyed.
Bat trade with the Umited States has fallen off $100,000,000
mmally for several years and this bears most heavily on
egrieultwral predueers ef riee md raw silk. Incidentally,
it is this elass which protests most bitterly against
Tekio's recent costly military expeditions.

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
Las Clean Record
Tho always identified with the class insistent upon
Japan's persistent, wnremitting pursuit of world power and
prestige, Viseount Ishii's career is marked with no un-
pleasant episodes. He is quiet, affable, courtly. He
speaks English and French fluently and the Capital recalls
him as an extremely popular figure with diplomats and
dowagers. He has friends among the big-wigs here and in
Europe as a result of long ambassadorial service at this
capital and Paris. As former Foreign Minister, he will
not be caught unaware.
Taller than the average Japanese, he is of distinguished
mien. His white mustache and hair give a eurious glint to
his swarthy complexion. Like so many of his countrymen, age
rests softly on him, even tho he indulges in very little
physical exercise. His principal recreations are poker and
billiards. In fact, while here he won an enviable reputa-
tion as the smartest stud player in the Capital. He plays
his diplomatio cards the same way, rarely raising unless he
has "the papers," but then going the limit. In President
Roosevelt he meets a man who plays the game -- both games -
pretty well himsolf.

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
ANNEX IV.
SOUrGe: WASHINGTON DAILY NEWS,
May 12, 1933.
'ASIA FOR ASIATICS,' IS
THE JAPANESE POLICY
HONOLULU, T. H. -- The Japanese "Monroe Doctrine"
presoribes an "Asia for Asiatics," Viscount Kikujiro Ishii
said upon his arrival here, en route to Washington for the
economic conferences.
The head of the Japanese delegation foresaw little
possibility of a war with the United States.
"There are only two ciroumstanoes under which confliet
would be imaginable," he said. "The first would be in case
Japan was foolish enough to interfere in affairs of the
western hemisphere. The second would be in case the United
States should interfere with Japan's peaceful expansion and
develepment in Asia.
"That can be interpreted as a Japanese Monroe Doctrine,
or as a polioy of Asia for Asiatics if you wish."

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.98)
SOUPGO: NEW YORK JOURNAL OF
COLMERCE, May 17, 1933.
JAPAN TO ASK CREDIT TO KEEP YEN STABLE
Delegates at Washington Will Seek Funds
to Restore Gold Base
By Shumnasuke Oishi
(Staff Correspondent)
Tokyo, May 2.- In their fortheoming conversations
with President Roosevelt, the Japenese delegates to
Washington and the economic conference in London, Viscount
Kikujiro Ishi and Eigo Fukai, will seek American assistance
and co-operation for providing credits for yen-dollar
exchange stabilization, it is now indicated.
The various Government departments after a series
of conferences have about orystalized pivotal principles
for many subjeets of discussion likely to be raised in
the parleys in America and England and these will be
ombodied in the instructions to the delegates who are
now leaving Yokohoma for Washington via San Francisco.
The rough outline of these instructions believed to
be as follows:
Parity Question
The Japanese Government is of the conviction that
curreney problems are the chief issue of the economio con-
ferences. It attaches great importance to the determina-
tion of international currency parities and is prepared
to cooperate with other nations to the fullest extent in
order to arrive at an equitable agreement.
However, Japan at present is deprived of practical
means to resume gold payments or stabilze yen exchange
quotations at a new low parity to be agreed upon in
the world economic conference. It therefore desires that
a reasonable period of grace be given in which to fermulate
measures for exehange stabilization. The most important
of these measures is the provision of stabilization funds
for which American assistanee is invaluable.
The Government is in perfect accord with the poliey
of the restoration of gold standard as a matter of principle
but it is not in a position to agree to radical marking
up of yen eurreney parity from the current depreciated
basis when fixing new parity.
Trede Barriers
Jepen is for assuring freedom of international trade
by the abolition of various barriers or restrictions as
a gemeral principle, but she is at the same time of the
opimien that peeuliar sonditioms ebtaining in different
countries should be taken into aeceunt when applying this
prineiple. For instanee, Japen will want rice made an
exeeption

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
exeeption for its unrestrieted importation into Japan will
seriously undermine the living conditions of the farming
population to result in social and pelitical unrest.
Japen is ready to co-operate with foreign nations to
her best ability for tariff reductions. It is, however,
apparent that Japan is not in a position to inaugurate a
uniform downward tariff revision with other countries and
the Japanese level of tariff rates away lower than that
for most other nations. Japan therefore intends to co-
operate in this direction by concluding multilateral
reciproeal tariff agreements.
Japan is in sympathy with the policy which aims at
boosting commodity prices by means of unrestricted cirou-
lation of currency internally as well as internationally.
She is for an international mobility of capital and, as a
means of attaining such an object sponsors inauguration
of liberal credit policy by the co-operation of all
sentral banks and organized actions of the Bank for Inter-
national Settlements.

MONETARY AND ECONOMIC CONFERENCE --
CONVERSATIONS.

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
COPY
May 25, 1953.
Conversation:
Present: The Secretary of State,
Viscount Ishii,
Mr. Fukai,
The Japanese Ambassador,
Mr. Tsushima,
Mr. Taketomi,
Mr. Iida,
Mr. Warburg,
Mr. Bullitt,
Mr. Tugwell,
Mr. Wylie,
Mr. Livesey,
Mr. Sussdorf,
Mr. Hornbeok.
The conversation was opened by the Seoretary of State,
who made a statement with regard to the purposes of the
conference and certain views of the American Government in
connection with procedure related thereto.
Visoount Ishii said that his delegation would be con-
tent with any order of procedure in regard to the conversa-
tion.
Mr. Warburg gave an account of the suggestions which
the American Government has laid before other delegations
and of the views whioh we entertain and of some points in
which other delegations, by name, have been in accord or

MISCELLANEOUS MEMORANDA

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
OOPY
May 23, 1933.
Vimoount Ishii's Attitude Toward the Integrity of China
In conneotion with conversations which will be held
with Viscount Ishii, --
Despatches from Japan have in several instances
indicated that Japan's interest in the preliminary con-
versations and the Economic Conference arises in great
measure from the hope of the Japanese that in the course
of the conversations and the Conference opportunity may
arise or occasion be made to introduce political ques-
tions that are of maximum current interest to Japan.
Some of these reports have carried the suggestion that
the Japanese delegation may attempt to trade adherence
on Japan's part to the proposals of the American Govern-
ment for some kind of assent by the American Government
to the policy which Japan is following in relation to
China. There has even been the suggestion that the
Japanese may bring up expressly the question of Manchuria
and of the American Government's "non-recognition" policy.
In case Viscount Ishii brings up any such question,
it may be useful to the President and the Secretary of
State to know that Viscount Ishii is on record in his
own Memoirs (published in 1930) to the effect that
Manchuria is a part of China. He wrote: "It is an
arbitrary assertion, without basis of fact, that
Manchuria is not a part of China." He said that a
contention of the German Government to the contrary was
clearly a misinterpretation which must be regarded as
perverted. Furthermore, with regard to the integrity
of China, he wrote: "The maintenance of China's
territorial integrity is vital to Japan - as
indispensable

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
indispensable as lips to teeth and as wheels to an axle --
and to preserve this integrity Japan did not hesitate to
stake her national existence".
In this connection, it is also interesting to turn
to the position taken by the Japanese Government itself
in 1904. In that year, in February, the Japanese Govern-
ment in correspondence with the Russian and other
Governments made it very clear that it regarded
Manchuria as an integral part of China, that it was
definitely concerned in support of the principle of
respect for China's territorial integrity and that it
based its grievance against Russia in part upon "the
successive refusals of the Imperial Russian Government
to enter into engagements to respect China's territorial
integrity in Manchuria which is seriously menaced by
their (Russia'e) continued occupation of the (that)
province, nothwithstanding their treaty engagements with
China and their repeated assurances to other powers
possessing interests in those regions . . . . ." (See
Department of State, Japan, 78)
FE:SKH/ZMK

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
May 25, 1933.
Viscount Ishii's Visit: Need of Caution.
In the CONGRESSIONAL RECORD of April 10, 1933
(page 1406) Senator J. Hamilton Lewis is quoted as
having made a statement reading in part as follows:
"England in the preservation of what she
feels to be her rights, long established in
some form in the Orient, has within the last
10 days consummated the revival of her treaty
with Japan such as was had by her with Japan
at the time of the Japanese-Russian War.
England covertly finds this action justified
for the preservation of what she assumes are
her property interests, as well as the
interests of her subjects living in the Orient."
Subsequently Senator King inquired orally of the
Department whether this statement had any basis in fact.
The Department telegraphed on April 12 to the
Embassy at London, as follows:
"It has been stated here that QUOTE
England has within the last ten days consum-
mated the revival of her treaty with Japan
such as was had by her with Japan at the time
of the Japanese Russian War UNQUOTE.
"Please report whether there is any basis
in fact for suoh statement." (Telegram No. 73,
April 12, 1933, 5 p.m.)
The Embassy reported on April 18 as follows:
"Have been assured by the Foreign Office
that the statement in your 73, April 12, 5 p.m.,
is without foundation." (Telegram No. 82,
April 18, 6 p.m.)
and on April 19 that, according to the Toreign Office,
"following the abrogation of the Anglo-
Japanese Alliance of 1922, no formal document
relating to any working agreement in the Far
East has been signed, nor has there been any
exhhange of communications between the
British and Japanese Governments which might
be construed as a revival of the Allianoe."
The

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The foregoing assurances would seem definitely to
show that the statement attributed to Senator Lewis is
without basis in fact.
Although the Department is without knowledge of the
source of Senator Lewis's statement, it would seem that
he may have received some garbled intimation of a recent
approach to the British Government by the Japanese in
reference to an understanding reached in 1928 by the
Japanese and British Governments in regard to develop-
ments in China. This understanding, which, it is believed,
has no relation to the defunct Anglo-Japanese Alliance,
came into existence as a result of the visit to London
in September, 1928, of Count Uchida, the present Japanese
Minister for Foreign Affairs. While at London, which he
visited following the signing at Paris, as Japanese
representative, of the Kellogg-Briand Pact, he discussed
with various officials of the British Foreign Office a
memorandum which he had prepared with regard to the
situation in China. (Subsequently, when in Washington
in the same month, he handed to the Secretary of State a
memorandum, under date September 29, 1928, on the same
subject, which it would seem probable was similar to
the memorandum discussed at London.) As a result of
Count Uchida's conversations at London, an informal
agreement was reached to the effect that, relations
between Great Britain and Japan with regard to China
being based on the obligations of full and frank
communications specified in Artiole vII of the Washington
Conference Nine Power Treaty of 1922, which reads:
"The

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
"The Contracting Powers agree that, whenever
a situation arises which in the opinion of any
one of them involves the application of the
stipulations of the present Treaty, and renders
desirable discussion of such application, there
shall be full and frank communication between the
Contracting Powers concerned",
Great Britain and Japan should maintain close contact
through constant communications and consultations
between their respective ministers at Peiping. (This
explanation of the agreement was given on November 28,
1928, by Sir Austen Chamberlain, the Foreign Secretary,
in the House of Commons. Sir Austen also s tated that
the United States Government was aware of the existence
of this agreement which was a natural consequence of the
Washington Conference Nine Power Treaty. It seems doubtful,
however, that this Government was aware of the existence
of this agreement until the receipt of despatch 3223 of
December 3, 1928, from the Embassy at London, which con-
tained a report of the agreement and Sir Austen's
explanation.)
According to information received from the Embassy
at London (letter of April 19, 1933, from the Counselor
of Embassy to the Chief of the Division of Far Eastern
Affairs), it appears that the Japanese Government recently
"invoked" this understanding, apparently in connection with
Japan's undesirable position vis-a-vis the states members
of the League arising out of the League's consideration
of the Sino-Japanese controversy. In view of the informal
nature and limited scope of the 1928 agreement, its
provisions and purport appear to be scarcely pertinent
to recent developments in the Far East and at Geneva.
It would seem that the Japanese have attempted to read
into and from the understanding of 1928 more than had been
intended

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
intended by the officials (on the British side, at least)
who entered in that agreement.
This seeming effort at misinterpretation is not the
first indication that at times the Japanese give an
impression of trying to distort to their own advantage
the meaning of statements made by officials of foreign
governments. A few months ago Viscount Kaneko, a
prominent Japanese, attributed to President Theodore
Roosevelt in the course of a conversation held thirty
years ago statements applicable in support of the present
Japanese thesis with regard to the Sino-Japanese dispute.
As no contemporary written record of the conversations
between President Roosevelt and Viscount Kaneko exists and
as almost thirty years have elapsed, it is difficult to
avoid a surmise that the statements alleged by Viscount
Kaneko to have been made by the President Roosevelt
have been given out in a form modified to fit Japan's
needs of the moment. It will also be recalled that the
Lansing-Ishii Agreement of November, 1917, (subsequently
canceled) was interpreted (and even translated) by the
Japanese in a way not in accord with the interpretation
placed on it by Mr. Lansing himself before, when and
after he had signed it.
In view of these evidences that the Japanese on
occasion at later dates distort the original intent and
significance of statements made at earlier dates, it is
suggested that, in discussions with Viscount Ishii and
other Japanese representatives who will participate in
the conversations at Washington preliminary to the Monetary
and Eoonomic Conference, care be exercised to avoid, so far
as may be possible, statements which might at a later date
lend themselves to abuses of interpretation.

Proposed Sale of Cotton to China
In the course of the conference held at this Depart-
ment this morning between the Japanese representatives
and representatives of the Amerioan Government, when the
question of international assistanoe for internal recon-
struotion in countries where assistance is needed came
up, the Japanese made it clear that they were opposed to
efforts in that direotion. They took the position that
eaoh country should look after and should supply funds
for its own internal reconstruotion. A hearer familiar
with current problems in the Far East and the views of
various governments and of the League of Nations with re-
gard thereto, and familiar with the position whioh the
Japanese have taken sinoe the submission by the Lytton
Commission of its report on the Sino-Japanese Manohuria
dispute, in which report there is an express recommendation
of assistanoe to China in conneotion with that country's
internal and eoonomio problems, could not fail to surmise
that, in the position whioh they took this morning, the
Japanese were reflecting an angle of their China polioy.
For some time it has been known that the League of Nations,
and among the members of the League the British Government
in partioular, have conoeived that the soundest line of
approaoh to a lasting solution of the Far Eastern problem
would lie along the way of international assistance to
China, that assistance to take the form of advice and
administrative and finanoial aid in conneotion with a
program of general eoonomio development.
Sometime

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
Sometime ago Sir Charles Addis outlined to Mr. Thomas
Lamont a British view of the desirability of an effort on
the part of the powers to move in that direetion. Mr.
Lamont submitted the matter to the Department of State,
and in reply the Seoretary of State informed Mr. Lamont
that a move in that direotion would have our blessing.
The China Consortium Agreement of l920, oonoluded in
consequence of an initiative taken by the American Govern-
ment in 19l8, was intended primarily for the purpose of
putting an end to and preventing a variety of competitive
finanoial diplomaoy whioh had been engaged in by certain
nations, espeoially the Japanese, to the detriment of the
real interests of China on the one hand and the interests
and rights of various other powers whioh looked with dis-
approval upon that type of aotivity and did not choose to
meet the competition by themselves resorting to suoh means.
The most notorious of the anteoedent abuses which have been
indulged in have been the so-called Nishihara Loans which
had been made by a Japanese financial agent aoting under
the aegis of the Japanese Government, to China, on the
basis of a variety of seourities. That group of transao-
tions had involved politioal aims under cloak of mere economio
and financial bargaining. After prolonged negotiations and
an immense amount of effort on the part of the Amerioan,
the British and the French Governments together wi th the
American, the British and the Frenoh banking groups con-
cerned, the Japanese Government and a Japanese banking group
were induced to oommit themselves to the prinoiples of the
proposed banking agreement; and the banking groups of the
four powers conoluded the agreement. It must be kept in
mind

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
mind that the great objeotive, so far at least as the Ameri-
oan Government and banking group were conoerned, was to
put an end to a dangerous and disoredited course and method
of international competition in relations with China and to
bring about a régime of regulated international cooperation,
in so far as that could be promoted by sound finanoing,
in that field.
A breaoh by the American Government of either the
letter or the spirit of the Consortium Agreement would be
all that the Japanese would ask as affording them a war-
rant for turning their backs entirely on both the letter
and the spirit of that Agreement and reverting to their
earlier practice of a purely national financial polioy in
reference to China. If the Government of the United
States makes a loan to China, no matter what the terms
and the ciroumstanoes attaohing to that loan, it will put
the Japanese in a position to offer loans on their part,
on suoh terms as they may be able to induce the Chinese to
aocept and in suoh ciroumstanoes as they may regard as
opportune, to the same borrower. Whatever may be the
reasons for or warrant of a loan which we may make to
China, we should, in oonoluding such a loan, if and when,
have it olearly in mind that in so doing we are probably
saying goodbye to the China Consortium Agreement, are
removing the obstaole whioh it constituted to unregulated
international competition in China, and are not only open-
ing the door to a new soramble for advantages in China but
are putting ourselves in the position of being the first
of the powers to pass through the door thus opened.
It

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.95)
- 4 -
It is believed that these points, among others,
should be carefully weighed by the Administration in
conneotion with any project for a loan either by the
Amerioan Government or by any finanoial agenoy of or in
the United States to China.

JAPAN'S OIL PROBLEM:
POSSIBLE MONOPOLY OR GOVERNMENTAL
CONTROL.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.98)
PARAPHRASE
Telegram dated May 8, 1933, from the Amerioan
Ambassador at Tokyo, reads substantially as follows:
It is presumed that the Department has seen des-
patohes from Japan in the WAIL STREET JOURNAL and other
newspapers with regard to a tentative proposal to es-
tablish either an oil monopoly, under which the existing
properties in Japan of the foreign oil companies would be
bought up by the Government, or a system of governmental
control through lioensing of importation and refining of
oil, under which Japanese companies could eventually, if
desired, operate exolusively the oil industry and trade.
The securing of sources of crude oil other than Amerioan
and British is also contemplated under the latter plan.
Aocording to the press these tentative plans were
compiled in response to a resolution passed by the
House of Peers in March of this year, calling upon the
Government to develop the domestic oil industry for
reasons of national defense, and were recently given to
the press by the Mining Bureau of the Department of Com-
meroe and Industry. Announoement of these plans may
conceivably have been made at the present time to afford
a trading point at the approaching Monetary and Economio
Conference at London, or even as a ballon d'essai to
guage what the reaotion abroad might be. It may, however,
be the desire of the Japanese to disoourage, until the
Government is in a position to determine its future
polioy, further expansion on the part of foreign oil
oompanies. That (or when) they will be put into effeot
1s

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
is not as yet indioated. Knowledge of those specifio
plans is disolaimed by the Commercial Bureau of the
Ministry for Foreign Affairs, but the existenoe of such
plans is not denied by it. The Commercial Bureau states
further that it disapproves entirely of any monopoly
projeot, but with regard to the licensing plan it ex-
presses no opinion.
Except for the sale of crude oil, the foreign oil
companies could expect to be driven eventually from
the field under either of these plans. The abandonment
of their distribution plants and organizations in Japan
would be necessitated. The Socony-Vaouum Corporation
and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company (British Shell
interests) - the two prinoipal foreign companies -
have been endeavoring for some time past to obtain
from the Japanese Government some statement of policy in
order to determine whether to make further investments
in Japan and in order to shape their own future plans.
As no satisfactory results followed the inquiries by these
companies, the Dutoh Minister, the British Ambassador,
and I have taken ocoasion separately to express informally
to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs the hope that some
indication might be given to our oil companies with re-
gard to the outlook for the future in Japan of their
business so that the determination of their own plans
would be made possible for them.
It was also pointed out to the Minister for Foreign
Affairs and the Vice Minister by the Dutoh Minister that,
partioularly at the present time when Japan is working
for

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
for international freedom of trade, an unfortunate
impression would be oreated abroad by the Japanese oil
plans as published. It is the belief of the Dutoh Minister
that the terms of the Dutoh-Japanese Treaty of Commerce
would be conflicted with by any plan to monopolize the
oil refining industry.
In my despatch No. 366 of April 21, 1933, there is
contained a disoussion of this general subjeot. It may be
the desire of the Department, although the precise text
of the plans is not yet available, to consider whether
along general lines the provisions of the American Japanese
Treaty of Commeroe oould be held to be contravened by a
monopoly or a licensing system. The future of the Amerioan
and other foreign oil interests in Japan may possibly in
due course be coming to a head, but the sanotion of the
Diet would presumably be required for any definite polioy.

A number of interestin comments on conditions in Japan h ve
been received by the Bureau in the weekly reports from Commercial attache
Frank S. Williams at Tokyo. As this material cennot be published and is
F/E

have made the following digest for your information and to be used wi th

SP

894.5
Political Situation.
894.50/94
The 65th session of the Diet closed on A arch 26, and while the
session was generally considered one of the most stormy in the history of
the nation, the Diet reelly accom lished a good deal. Sixty-three bills
were introduced by the Government, of which 54 were approved as sunmitted,
and 7 passed after being revised. Two failed to pass. Of the 63 bills
submitted, 13 were budget meesures.
During the session, the Ministers of war, Commerce nd Industry, and
Education were changed, the latter two as a consequence of severe cri-
ticism from members of the Diet based on t eir reported connection with
rather shady financial transactions.
The fact that the Saito Cabinet weathered the storm is indeed
surprising, as there were several occasions when it was on the verge of
collapse from outside pressure. ven now, after the height of the storm
has passed, there is considerable press agitation for the resignation of
the Cabinet and the call of a general election. More conservative writers
believe, however, that Premier Saito will be able to carry on for some time
to come.
Debate on the 1934-35 budget, calling for expenditures of more
than 2,140,000,000 yen, at times waxed exceedingly turbulent. Howeyer,
while many Diet members expressed their disapproval of the unbalanced pro-
visions for defense and relier, the continued inorease in the nationel
visions for defense and rel ief, the continued increase in the national
debt caused by huge deficit loans in the f'orm of bonu issues, and the ab-
sence of a definite future financial policy, nevertheless, the budget was
passed as it stood because "it includes appropriations for the national
defense."

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
This phrase sums up in a nut-shell the strong appeal the budget
ffered not only to members of the Diet but to the public at large. It
was characterized "an Emergency Budget", based on the present unsettled
international situation, and the enormous appropriations for the Army
and Navy were undoubtedly responsible for the absence of discussions in
the Diet of the estimates submitted from those two Departments. The
wave of patriotism that has for some time been sweeping over the country
is being carefully encouraged and fostered by the military authorities,
and it is very probible that if any member of the Diet had really and
honestly attacked the defense items, he would have found himself the sub-
ject of at least verbal onslaughts, and perhaps have endangered his life.
Financial Situation
Approval of the Budget for thefiscal year 1934-35 insures large
expenditures for munitions and allied supplies, which means continued
high levels of production for manufacturers of those items. A statement
of Government accounts indicates substantial increases in revenues fron
taxes. Total expenditures of the Budget as passed by the Diet amount to
2,142,528,000 yen, while receipts from taxes and Governnent undertakings
are estimated at 1,248,543,000 yen and extraordinary revenue at 893,894,000
yen. Of the latter amount, 81l,189,000 yen is to be obtained from new
bond issues, which is a reduction of 107,895,000 yen from the 1933-34
deficit bond issue. This reduction is the result of incrased taxes.
It is reported that the Treasury estimates the tax increase during 1934-35
over 1933-34 will reach 83,280,000 yen, while Government undertakings will
show a gain of approximately 37,000,000 yen.
Commenting on the financial situation, r. Williams states that on
the basis of Government revenues for the past two years, it is reasonable
to predict that fairly large deficits will occur each year for the next
several years. So far the Government has no known definite policy formu-
lated to meet the inflationary conditions that must follow the heavy bond
issues. To date, it has been able to fairly well control the situation
by means of open market operations by the Bank of Japan. It is a question,
however, just how long this practice can be resorted to with effect. Not
only Government bonds are being offered on the market, but each month eees
qn increase in provincial, banking and industrial bonds. These public
loan bonds now in the possession of banks amount to an enormous sum, the
total, including stocks at the end of February, was 3,655,802,000 yen,
and there must be a saturation point somewhere. Lower interest rates
and higher bond prices may inject further interest in the bond market for
a time, but currency inflation and speculatioh may be expected to develop
if the present upward trend of bond issues is not checked.
Moreover, agricultural relief expenditures offer another problem in
the adjustment of the national finances of the country. The Government is
becoming very heavily involved in its rice purchase scheme, to the extent
in fact that more funds will have to be allocated if this relief program
is to be continued. To date the Government has purchased over 10,000,000
koku of rice, costing over 400,000,000 yen. The Minister of Agriculture
is requesting an additional budget of 150,000,000 yen to purchase Chosen
and Taiwan stocks to the extent of 7,O00,000 koku. It is estimated that

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
the total deficit caused by the operation of the Rice Control Act to
date is more than 200,000,000 yen.
Anglo-Japanese Trade Parley.
Japanese reaction to the breakdown of the trade parley at London
is summarized by Mr. Fusajiro Abe, President of the Japan Spinners'
Association, and a recognized authority on Japanese cotton uestions.
Mr. Abe said in part:
"There is no need for Japanese traders to be unduly affect-
ed over a possible anti-Japanese policy to be adopted by Britain.
"Come what may, Lancashire men cannot effectively compete
with Japanese spinners, unless they carry out drastic internal
reforms, which are more important for them than to contend against
Japanese spinners. How to improve their internal affairs depends
upon improvement of their financial status. Should the British
Government advance sufficient funds to improve their business
conditions, it would indeed be a serious problem for spinners here,
but under existing conditions Lancashire spinners are not rivals
to our spinners.
"A decade ago, the Japanese spinning industry would have
suffered severely through tariff measures, but during several
years, the spinning industry here has undergone a signal improve-
ment in operation, production costs, as well as many other points,
and is to-day working upon a sound basis. It is a hasty conclusion
to say that the Japanese soinnin inaustry will be completely
shut out from markets abroad. he have already entered upon a
trade convention with India, Australia, Canada, the Union of
South Africa, and other British Lominions ure not expected to
fall into line with the home Government."
Commenting on these remarks, mr. illiams states that ne seriously
doubts any other Government's willin mess or readines: to go as far
towards assisting any home inuustry as tht Japanese Government is to
assist Japanese spinners. From this angle, it appears that the Japanese
have far less to worry about than the British. The Japanese, says Mr.
Williams, certainly have mervelous plants, cheap and efficient labor,
a well planned snc economic system of distribution, and strong financial
support.
Reports are current that the Japanese Cotton Spinners' Association
will shortly invite British manufacturers to visit Japan and personally
inspect the conditions of the Japanesc cotton spinning industry.

-4 -
Trade Parley Between Japan anu the Dutch.
Japanese spinning circles, however, seem to be evincing more interest
in the approaching trade parley with the Dutch (to be held some time
in May) than in negotiations with the British. The market in Netherland
India is considered of particular importence to Japan and the quota system
and other restrictions the Dutch have taken and are contemplating,
not only on imports, but also on the activities of Japanese merchants in
Netherland India, are viewed with no little alarm. It would be indeed a
serious blow to Japan to be shut out of the Netherland rast Indies.
A spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office, in discussing the Dutch
situation with Mr. Williems, intimatcd that the coming trade conference
with the Dutch will offer difficult problems. The Dutch are insisting
that steps be taken to more evenly balance the trace between the Nether-
land Last Indies and Japan, which last year was favorable to Japan by
100,000,000 yen. The Dutch want Japan to purchase larger quantities of
Java sugar, but that industry is now being decidedly encouraged and de-
veloped in Formosa, and any curtailment in Japan's takings of sugar from
Formosa would result in greater rice production in that territory, - just
what Japan is now trying to discourage.
Importation of Dutch Oil in Preference to American is Suggested.
At a meeting of the Japanese Economic Federation early in March it
was decided, according to reports, to recomm end that the Japanese Govern-
ment import crude oil from the Netherland East Inuies in accordance
with the barter system proposeo by the Netherland Government. It was
pointed out that in line with the Oil Control Bill (since passed by the
Diet) crude oil could be imported from the Netherland rast Indies in
larger quantities under Government control, thereby increasing Japan's
imports and decreasing her favorable prevailing annual trade belance
with that territory. It was also pointeu out tiat the price of Dutch
oil is higher than the Amcrican product and that a reduction of freight
rates on the former or a Government subsidy to the Japanese importers
would be necessary to encourage such imports.
Barter Agreements.
The Japanese press of March 14 carrieu an account of an agrecment
purported to have been made between importers anu exporters of Japan and
the South and Central American countries for the aaoption of a voluntary
barter system whereby the present one-sided commerce may be reaajusted.
A similar scheme is now in force with South Africa.

Maroh 7, 1938.
Reference Tokyo's 2750, January 29,
1938, "Eoonomic Japan after six months
of the China War".
Mr. Coville's principal conclusions
are as follows:
The economic condition prevailing
in Japan after six months of war can, in
itself, scarcely be a cause of desperate
concern on the part of the nation's
leaders:"
". . . the external appearance of
Japanese life has changed little.
The rise of prices does not yet much modify
people's living habits. . . ."
There can be little doubt that Japan
is able to bear a greater war load than
that imposed at the present time. Six
months of the China war has been borne
with ease. . . . The Japanese nation has
yet to show its war powers."
These and other conclusions in the re-
port are interesting matters of fact in
regard to which there will be little dis-
agreement. The reasons given by the report
for the ease of Japan's war efforts thus
far must be gleaned from an exceedingly
abstruse

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
abstruse presentation replete with misused
terms, but they appear to be as follows:
(1) For many years a very large pro-
portion of Japan's industriel output has
consisted of capital goods for further
industrial expansion. The industries pro-
ducing these capital goods are able in time
of war to produce for war needs with rela-
tively little difficulty, and with practi-
cally no effects upon consumers (a govern-
ment requires wide war powers in order to
direct production according to its needs,
but in Japan it possesses these powers).
(2) Large military budgets in recent
years have enabled the accumulation of
large reserves of arms, ammunition and
implements of war.
(3) At the beginning of the conflict
there had been accumulated in Japan large
stocks of raw materials, financed by the
exportation of gold stocks.
The one prediction of the report -
"There can be little doubt that Japan is
able to bear a greater war load than that
imposed at the present time" - is probably
true in its intended meaning but is badly
expressed. The actual war load will
probably continue as at present; but as
reserves

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
resorves of commodities and foreign ex-
change are exhausted, the "load" will be
shifted directly onto the people. At that
time the test of the popularity of the
conflict will come. To what extent will
the people suffer privation in order to
support the conquest of China?
In this conneotion it should be pointed
out that stooks of raw materials have been
reduced to very low levels, that stores of
arms and ammunition are exhausted with
great rapidity in time of war, that Japan's
exports are declining rapidly, and that the
cost of living is inoreasing. According to
a recent report from the Commercial Attaché
in Tokyo, the retail price index inoreased
3.1% in January 1938 over December 1937,
and 11.5% over January 1937. Clothing,
lighting, and heating costs have all ad-
vanced sharply. These trends are expeoted
to continue.
