IV.

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
IV.
Explanation of the comparative ease with which the
war is being waged offers roon for an attempt at analysis.
The first and obvious explanation is that the well-integrated
system of emergency control measures has performed its task.
The prompt enforcement of those measures was one indication
of how complete has been the advance preparation for war;
and the fact of promptness in their enforcement has been
an important factor in a ceneral confidence thnt the country
is ready for the larger demands of a larger war if one is
brought on. Control of imports, control of foreign exchange,
control of investment, control of holdiigs abroad -- all
these steps have shown a sound antici ation of what are
Japan's emergency requirements in war, and they ha ve
worked with fair smoothness.
But there are other and perhaps fully as determinative
reasons for the adequacy of the country's economic prepared-
ness for war. In an economic sense, Japan has been prac-
tising for war for several years. She has accustomed her-
self to a rate of production far in excess of her accus-
tomed popular consumption, and a large part of the dif-
ference has been applied to such uses that there is made
possible, on very short notice, the direction of a large
share of the nation's economic energies to war; and this
without change in the people's habits of consumption.
Without substantially altering the rate of consumption
by the Japanese population, the country can stand a large
war drain by simply focusing upon the war the large portion
of its production which during the last years has been
diverted.
The

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
The period since l931 has been a period of very rapid
increase in the production of Japan. At the same time
there has been in the average no rise in real wages. The
increase went into the above-mentioned henomenally rapid
rate of capital goods investment in Japan and Manchuria,
and into larger and larger military budgets. By 1936 invest-
ment and the military were taking four billion yen of the
national income. It is from this reservoir that the two
and a half billion of war costs thus far is chiefly coming.
Construction and investment is being checked in accordance
with a careful plan, and the manufacturing industries which
serve construction are being in part directed to the purposes
of the war.
This explanation at the same time explains the logic
of the movement for attracting American capital to Manchuria.
Manchuria has been a field of huge Japanese investment; be-
cause of the war Japanese investment there can not continue
in the same volume; therefore American money advanced to
enterprise in Manchuria would exactly fit in with the
economic adjustment in Japan demanded by the war.
There is a separate and temporary reason for the war
readiness of Japan in July 1937 and the relative ease of
carrying the load in these last months: supplies useful in
war were purchased by cold export, in the sum of half a
billion yen over and above the imports which were balanced
by commodity exports. Late in l936 a wave of speculative
buying brought about regulations in January 1937 to limit
imports to current needs; but in spite of exchange diffi-
culties

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
culties it was as a matter of policy decided to make
purchases, chiefly in war materials in anticipation of war.
The buaget for the fiscal year beginning April 1, l937 exceeded
the previous year's by half a billion yen, and the increases
were in military expenditures. Imports in pre aration for
possible war were thought so important as to warrant the
shipment of gold abroad in large quantities, and the con-
sequent importine gave Ja an a reservoir or ooas on which
the country has drawn to reat advanta e ror the pur ose
of the war in China. It is impossible to itemize the bulge
of imports from official custons returns because the former
listing has given way to one which lumps together in a mis-
cellaneous classification a very large quantity of goods,
doubtless for war pur oses, to which the Government does not
desire to attract attention. But their general nature is
a matter of common knowledge. As the Mitsubishi Economic
Research Bureau has recently stated, in commenting on the
year 1937: "The most prominent feature in the import trade
was the abnormally heavy shipments from the United States,
the increase totalling 439 million yen. The largest part
of this increase probably consisted of unlisted war materials,
as arrivals of raw cotton remained on the level of former
years." Germany also profited by similar purchases in
smaller amount.
V.
There can be little doubt that Japan is able to bear
a greater war load than that imposed at the present time.
Six months of the China war has been borne with ease. By
drawing on an already existing large military machine, con-
siderable

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.97)
-10-
siderable accumulations of war materials, and manufacturing
resources devoted in peace times to the country's accelerated
pace of capital investment, the war has thus far been met with
very little efrect on the social pattern of consumption. What
would be the ultimate economic capacity of the country in a
war making larger demands, after the excess is exhausted,
the slack taken up, and the load placed directly on the
people, can not be deduced fron the experiences of the past
six months. The Japanese nation has yet to show its war
powers. The war in China has not yet brought it to the
test.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

have the honor to submit the followin study. This review
is based on ststistics directly available in this Con-
sulate and fron interviews with at least fifty or more

The opinion of ship in interests, leadin banks, and the

F/FC
I wish to acknowledge with thanks the valuable
assistance and cooperation of Consul General Makinson in
supplying up to date invoice export figures, as well as
most useful comments concerning commercial conditions in
Osaka.
Before

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Before attempting to draw any conclusions a
factual presentation of the various elements present in
the situation has been attempted under separate headings.
Foreign Commerce
As will be recalled, toward the end of 1936,
anticipating government control of foreign trade,
exporters rushed to place their goods in foreign markets
while there was a similar pressure on the part of
importers to build up stocks. Exporters wished to assure
themselves that they would be able to fill orders and the
movement to ship abroad was accelerated by the equal desire
of purchasers to stock up on Japanese goods which they
quite rightly felt would be higher. In America heavy
orders were placed on the assumption also that domestic
business would continue to improve, an assumption unfor-
tunately not justified. An oversold condition of Japanese
merchandise abroad, particularly in the United States,
resulted.
Japanese importers wished to stock up, both to insure
themselves of sufficient raw materials for future needs
and because they wished to show a large volume of busi-
FLLED
ness, since they had an intimation that the governnent
would grant import quotas on the basis of the previous
volume of business.
The government put exchane restrictions into effect
in January 1937. The early part of July witnessed the
beginning of the China hostilities and in October exchange
restrictions were supplemented by the import licensing
system. Although these measures of control had caused
consider:

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
considerable anxiety and some dislocation of business,
particularly with respect to the handling of future
commitments, the actual level of foreign commerce did
not show an appreciable decline until January 1938,
December 1937 being a more or less normal month. This
may be largely attributed to the fact that the completion
of existing orders did not take place until the beginning
of the new year.
Investigating carefully the trend of foreign commerce,
interpreting such reliable figures as are available and
obtaining a cross-section of business opinion, which is
virtually unanimous on this point, we find that there
has been a marked decline in both exports and imports
Telegram
during 1938. Official figures indicate that the shrinkage
t Puping
in imports has been appreciably greater than in exports.
puil20, 1434
This may be the case but it must be borne in mind that
official returns do not indicate importations for the
account of the military services. The purchase of war
materials has been relatively large, hence informed
observers believe that imports have declined less than
exports. A rough corroboration to this assumption is
given by the fact that shipping companies inform me that
they are still fairly heavily loaded on inward voya es
but leave Japan lightly laden.
It is apparent that exports have fallen off greatly
to the United States and Canada, somewhat less tc England,
and appreciably less to the other European countries. On
the other hand, the export trade to Africa, British and
Dutch Colonial possessions in Asia and other parts of
the world does not seem to be suffering in nearly the
same proportion. The information obtained from the va?
of

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -

In considering Japan's ability to carry on in spite
of difficulties in its foreign trade it should not be
overlooked that bi industries Lave made tremendous pro?its
with which lerge reserves  ave been lt ill uy. The bead
of one of the leacine cotten compenies s owed me the most
recent report of the Spinnin Association which indicated

even greater than 1936 - during the last half of 1937. In
some cases these profits ran as high as 40, 50 and in one
case 71%.
Outlook

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Outlook for Foreign Trade
Certainly the present recession in foreign trade
will continue for some little time. With the exception
of war materials, both exporters and importers have very
little on their books. It should be added that there is
a wide range of opinion on the future. Some exporters
feel that by the end of 1938, or even before, the export
trade ill show improvement.
They think that the decline
is due fundamentally to an oversold condition which will
be corrected later as speculative stocks of Japanese goods
held abroad are moved. This group discounts the boycott
and other non-economic factors. It takes the position
that a large volume of merchandise normelly exported is
now being sold in Japan because of the high domestic
prices and that when this condition is righted exports
will again rise. It looks to the government to correct
this situation. However this may be, even if the govern-
ment is successful in stopping the present inflation,
there is little prospect of an upturn for the next few
months. Aside from other factors, prices of Japanese
goods are now too high competitively.
Internal

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Internal Trade
With the exception of firms whose business is
directly curtailed by import restrictions and a few
industries which for special reasons are in unfavorable
positions, business is at a high level. It is probable
that when the government at Tokyo laid out a blue print
of a controlled external economy it had in mind that
certain curtailments in inports would entall certain
export losses, in other words that a saving of foreign
exchange availabilities could be made at a definitely
determinable cost in terms of exports.
What has happened here is probably not quite in
accordance with schedule. The curtailment of imported
materials entering into domestic manufacture has had the
effect of enormously increasing domestic prices by
affording a wholly unnatural protection to Japanese sub-
stitutes for imported articles and by increasing the
demand for foreign materials on hand in Japan which may
not be replenished. This situation has resulted in a
typical "boom" psychology, consumers rushing to buy
before prices are raised, retailers hastening to stock
up for the same reason, manufacturers bidding for raw
materials and so on.
The home market has proved so attractive that even
such standard export articles as cotton sheeting and other
piece goods are being cut into small units to supply the
domestic retail demand at high prices. Foreign trade is
suffering but merchants in general are making huge profits.
Consider a few examples: hemp, which today is going
down

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
down in Manila, has been steadily going up in Japan
and is bringing four or five times the Manila price;
gum arabic, prior to import restrictions, was purchased
for Yen 40 a unit - the price is now Yen 140. Al1
chemicals which can be produced in Japan have gone up
enormously and have increased substantially the cost of
textiles and various synthetic products such as soap,
rayon, glass, paper, etc. Consider rayon for example.
I am informed that for each ton of sulphite pulp one
ton of caustic soda is used. The imported soda being
shut off or restricted, the domestic caustic soda has
risen from Yen 160 per ton to Yen 240. Other examples
could be cited.
In addition to the effect of government control in
foreign trade, the activities of war industries are
playing an important part in creating the present "boom"
or inflationary condition. Ultimately such a situation
must find its own corrective but just when it will come
and what form it will take is doubtful. The purchasing
power of the mass of the people has been declining but,
while the average man is pinched, his situation is still
tolerable and he is still willing and able to buy. Except
for special lines depending on tourists, retail selling
is at a high level and movie houses and other amusement
places are crowded. There is no lack of spending.
Unemployment

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.95)
-9 -
Unemployment
We have seen thal lomestic trade is lourishing.
In spite of the sonevat ra  r eu eio. in orein
business this Cons late an tb u t y
casesofloyeTa
off. Certain firms, it is true, are coute laling
disnissing en loyees in the year fnlure, but how irastic
these cuts wiil be can   u
to be a general policy to carry tieir  ill stafes on tue
payroll for the present, eit er i in into reserves or
makin up in other ways. Furt ernore, nost com anies
are still paying full wa es to :ormer ew loyees now at
the front. The question of t lon ti shall be eon-
tinued is now bein liscusse. In the cot ton inlustry
not only is there no dischar e of workers, but according
to information supplied by t e spinners Ansocial lon, tiere
are actually more on the payroll than durin last year.
For example, 71 yarn mil1s in 1937 had 156, 626 employees
as against 1b7,030 em loyees at the present time. Forty-
four weavin nills in 1u37 em loyed only 41,663 as against
44,428 for 43 weavin aills. (For more detailed figures
see Enclosure l.o. 6.)
The sales forces of the large department stores
have not been cut. There may be some retrenchment in the
small family factory units but this remains unnoticed.
It is true, however, that generally speakin commercial
firms are not replacing personnel as vacancies arise. The
Mayor of Kobe informed me that there is no unemployment
problem in Kobe at the present time, but that one may
arise when the troops are demobilized. The government is
studying

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
studying this, he added.
As to the future trend concerning unemployment,
there are two schools of thought sharply divided. One
maintains that previous crises indicate that released
workers will never be "on relief or on the street" as
in western countries, but will be absorbed on the farms
or be taken care of through the family system; that in
the cotton industry, for example, the mills are located
in rural communities and employ country girls who will
simply go straight back to the farms if released without
causing serious readjustments. The other school main-
tains that the farns cannot absorb an indefinite number
of people, that the process of passing on to the farmer
the increased costs whether indirectly in the form of
rising prices or directly in the forn of taxation will
create a saturation point where additional mouths cannot
be fed from the surplus product of the soil. It would
seem to be a question whose answer only actual experience
will demons trate. Certain it is that unemployment is no
problem in this section of Japan at present, nor does it
seem likely to be in the very near future. It would
appear that a real unemployment problen may arise with
the termination of the China hostilities when demobilized
troops will have to be absorbed, when the activity of war
industry will cease and the urge to give employment for
patriotic reasons will be at a minimum.
Wages

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
- 11 -
Wages
This Consulate knows of only one case where wages
have been reduced. A certain large exporting firm handling
a varied line has reduced wages fifteen per cent. Most
firms, however, not only have not reduced wages but have
made some effort to compensate for rap idly increasing
living costs by making small increases.
To summarize, it may be stated that wages are being
maintained, although real wa es are falling due to the
rapid rise in prices.
Exchange

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.96)
-12-
Exchange and import Restrictions
As might be anticipatei, there is a eneral complaint
in this regioe, botn on the  art t orei ners ane da enese,
concernine the exchar e udport e't s his
criticisa, often very bitter, is levelled at te pelicies
involved, as well as at the mechanies of operation. After
weIghing: the mass of seattered eommsonts and complaints,
the sitiation mi ht ue sunneo  as tollows.
In the first place, bueiness men dislike in prineiple
import ano exchange restric ions, an those w' ich afreet
their own business they are apt to criticise an being
unwarran ted ant unsound ecomtcall, overloel in tae fact
that some sacrifice must be e or t oon urpose.
Bearing this in mine, one is unet le to eteraine how mich
weight should beiven to general attackn on policy.
The main criticism on t is score, ano it is probaoly
a very valid one, is that, as we a e pointed out pre-
viously, the curtailment or stonpa e of numerous foreign
imports enterin into donestic manufacture has raised
internal prices unduly ani thus has both unsettled
internal economic conditions ane jeoparuized the e port
trede in certain lines. Some merchants even believe that
certain foreign markets have beon permanently lost.
An important banker in this region stated to me that
he considered this factor the greatest single menace in
the situation. He sald that he was pessimistic as to
the government's ability to remedy the situation through
measures such as requiring the mixture of inferior
materials for home consumption, for example, staple
fiber

=== Page 311 === (confidence 1.00)
-13-
fiber to be mixed with cotton. He said that it would
be impossible to resist the temptation of high prices
and that the export product would continue to be sold
in the home market.
The other most general complaint with respect to
policy is that importers have not been granted sufficient
exchange ahead to anticipate normal and legitimate
requirements.
With respect to the mechanics of the exchange and
import control, the following points stand out as being
almost unanimously commented upon.
One
The slowness in acting upon requests. Local mer-
chants inform me that at first exchange permits could be
put through in ten days but that it now takes thirty at
least.
Two
The lack of technical ability in the Ministries of
Finance and Commerce and Industry, due apparently to
inexperienced clerks and a too rapid turnover of personnel.
Three
The fact that import permits and corresponding
exchange permits are not coincidental. It is under-
stood, however, that this question is being given study
in Tokyo and probably will be remedied.
Four
Discrimination. - There is undoubtedly discrimination.
This

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
This is discussed in more detall as a separate subject.
In connection with the regulations or policies
governing the granting of exchange permits, this Con-
sulate has learned from an unimpeachable source of a
statement made by the Chairman of the Advisory Council
of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry that the fol-
lowing criteria are used in considering the granting of
exchange permits:
The volume of past business.
Price in relation to qua ity.
Moral standing, regularity of imports and
cooperative attitude with the government of both seller
and buyer.
Adaptability of the product to Japanese technology.
Relations (including both economic and political)
between Japan and the country involved.
Although the pressure of exchange restrictions has
not developed to an acute stage, the be inning of various
subterfuges to circumvent the exchange law are already
apparent. For example, since the Japanese government
permits purchase of Yen 100 per month of foreign currency
without securing a formal permit, certain foreign
suppliers have prevailed upon firms to whom they supply
their goods to import direct merchandise to the value of
the amount of exchan e they are freely allowed. The
exchange thus created is then pooled.
In other cases this Consulate knows of a premium
being given for the purchase of dollar or sterling
exchange.
Exchange

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
Exchange Operations
In spite of the inadequacy of foreign exchange for
various needs there does not appear to have developed
any very important extra legal market or "bolsa negra"
of the type we are familiar with in South America. There
is, however, some trend in that direction and such a
market would probably develop should the pressure for
foreign funds become too great. For example, this Con-
sulate understands from a source which cannot be questioned
that Mitsui a few days ago purchased dollars in New York,
paying as high as 28 3/4 at a time when the London-New
York cross rate was at 5.Ol, in other words, Mitsui paid
a premium of O.25% over the one shilling and two pence
official value of the yen.
Another firm recently paid a premium of 5% to obtain
sterling exchange. There are other cases of this sort,
but so far it is not believed that there is any extensive
or widespread evasion of the exchange law.
Discrimination

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
Discrimination
In general American firms have not complained
particularly of discrimination. British firms have.
After investigating this matter carefully this Con-
sulate believes there is discrimination, both direct
and indirect, against foreign firms by the government.
Furthermore, small Japanese firms also suffer discrimi-
nation to some extent.
Various examples might be cited.
For instance, a
foreign importer of hemp has not been able to obtain
exchange permits for this article while his Japanese
competitors have. The uild system lends itself to
discrimination both toward foreigners and Japanese.
One flagrant case of real discrimination has
occurred, for example, in the case of the shellac guild.
In this instance, it was intimated by the government to
the shellac importers guild that import permits would not
be furnished to foreign members of that guild. Upon the
foreign members objecting to this discrimination the
guild was dissolved and it is understood the matter is
not yet settled.
Discrimination against small Japanese firms occurs
because of their dependence on the great Jananese indus-
trial families. These firms, such as Mitsui, Mitsubishi
and others, force the weaker uilds whose manufactured
articles they market abroad to allot import permits to
them rather than to guild members as intended. They
coerce them through an implied threat not to market their
products if they do not cooperate.
There

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.98)
- 17 -
There is considerable open complaint and opposition
on the part of foreign firms to discrimination. This
was given voice in the meeting of the Kobe and Osaka
Foreign Chamber of Commerce on March 15th when a resolution
was adopted protesting the discrimination against foreign
firms being carried on. The full text of this resolution
is given as Enclosure No. 7.
American

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
American Business
Most American business men, unless they are
interested in industries connected with the war, are
pessimistic over the economic outlook not only for the
near future but from the longer range viewpoint as well.
It may be stated that America firms are more pessimistic
than their British colleagues and certainly have an
entirely different point of view from the Germans. It
is interesting to note that in spite of their attitude
regarding business conditions American firms frankly
state that the British firms are having more difficulty
getting permits than they are. Since the beginning of
the China hostilities four or five American firms have
liquidated their affairs. Whether because of the British
tradition of "muddling through" or whether because the
present situation does not hit the British as hard they
appear to be sticking. No case of the closing out of a
British firm has come to this Consulate's attention.
German firms, on the other hand, have been extending
long term credits and apparently feel a confidence in
the economic outlook in Japan not shared by their
Anglo-Saxon colleagues.
Frozen

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
Frozen Assets
It is not believed that a frozen asset or blocked
yen problem on a large scale is apt to develop as
concerns American interests. This Consulate has been
able to find only a few cases where American firms are
holding appreciable quantities of yen awaiting transfer
to the United States. It appears that even where local
representatives are willing to extend credits or take
certain chances for the sake of doing business they are
restrained by their principals in the United States who
remember disastrous experiences in Russia, South America
and other parts of the world.
American firms are losing or rejecting current
business for the sake of mainteining a stronger financial
position. This practice is at variance with the British,
who are less conservative in this regard, and entirely
different from the Germens who are willing to run large
risks.
The

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.93)
-20-
The Boycott

As against the view that boycotts are ineffective,
there are some indiviouals who think that they are having
some influence at the present time, and will have a
cumulative effect later on. Generally speakin, however,
business men do not feel that boycotts are an important
factor in the present recession. They hold the view that
business will ebb or flow for economic reasons of which
the master reason is the correct "price" factor.
Conclusion

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.94)
- 21 -
Conclusion
In my opinion the proper sppraisal of the situation
in this re ion shml: e ns fol lows:
Forein tr
Crastically sime te be n, we are unable
to evaluate the percents e sut aini.. of zo woulc
appear a conservative estimate.
The situatin ormt eee
of direct losses in trade ane because of discrimiratLon.
Since American firms did not et caught wit. lon accounts
in yen and have a tendene to ci to in  liquic Ionition
or withdraw, a lar e scale protec ior problem is not
likely to erise.
The decline in forein trade  ae beon islecatine
to trade, particularly to fore: n interests.  om the
Japanese standpoint it  as hat two very ioportent e -
sequences. First, the restriction of imports Ias acted
as an abnormal protective tariff an' pas forced internal
prices up enormously. This has created en inflationary
or "boom" situation. Second, since internal prices for
export commodities are way out of line with prices outside
of Japan, Japanese oods have been placed in an unfavorable
condition abroad. Furthermore, it is more attractive to
sell at home. The net result is to accclerate the decline
in exports which had already taken place as a cirect
repercussion from the restriction of imports.
The slump in forein trade will continue for some
little time, since, one, we know from an investigation
of the leading business firms that there is little for-
ward export business on their books and, two, the need
for

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.98)
-22-
for the government to restrict imports still continues
and small imports inevitably mean small exports, parti-
cularly in the case of Japan whose export trade is so
largely dependent on processing imported materials.
The inflationary process to which the shutting down
of imports has contributed is further intensified by
increased production and expenditures releted to the war
●
in China, and by the present psychology which is still,
in spite of a lowering of "real wages" a spending
psychology. There is no clue at present as to when the
process will complete its course.
There is no unemployment. Firms which are slack
are maintaining employees on the payrolls for the present.
There appears no likelihood of unemployment in the near
future.
Wages are being maintained though not in proportion
to the increasing living costs.
With wages and employment kept up it follows that
no special social problems have developed as a result of
the somewhat abnormal trade situation. There is no
visible political problem in this region. Possibly the
seeds for future trouble have been laid, but if such is
the case it can not be detected at the present time.
The
war is not popular but it is accepted. Some liberal
thought is articulate, but it is doubtful how much weight
it has.
The mass of the population are quiescent and most
merchants quite satisfied since they are making money.
Big business men, however, are deeply apprehensive about
the immediate business outlook, which they consider unsound
since

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.98)
- 23 -
since it jeopardizes the export trade, about the Fascist
trend of current legislation, and finally about the
situation of industry after the end of the China war.
What influence they may exert can better be judged in
Tokyo than here.
It would be useful, interesting, and even fascinating,
to endeavor to draw some inferences as to the possible
effect of the unusual economic conditions which have
developed upon the course of the China hostilities.
However, affirmative conclusions are not warranted at
this time. The present economic situation does not seem
sound, but so far no serious suffering has arisen. On
the contrary many of the effects have been stimulating to
trade. A leading Japanese banker, a gentleman well
known to the Embassy, who returned a few days ago from
a conference in Tokyo, stated privately that Japan could
see its way clear to finance the war until the end of
this year, but until then only.
In any event we can not conclude that financial or
social conditions in industrial Japan now, or in the
immediate future, will influence greatly the course of
the war in China or related policies.
Respectfully yours,
Winthrop R. Scott
American Consul

Enclosure No. 7
RESOLUTION
WHEREAS reports of their exclusion from membership
in Associations formed or about to be formed for the
control of the importation of certain commodities have
been received from Members of this Chamber of Commerce;
AND WHEREAS this Chamber of Commerce has fully
satisfied itself that the chief reason for such
exclusion is the non-Japanese nationality of the
Members concerned;
AND WHEREAS this Chamber of Commerce in the interests
of international trade most strongly deprecates any
discrimination against the nationals of any countries
having treaty relations with Japan.
NOW THEREFORE IT IS HEREBY RESOLVED that this
Annual General Meeting of the Kobe & Osaka Foreign
Chamber of Commerce expresses unqualified disapproval
of any and all discriminatory practices and policies
directed against the legitimate business interests of
Members of this Chamber of Comaerce and which may be
carried on, concurred in, or condoned by such Associations
AND that the Committee of this Chamber of Commerce is
hereby authorized and empowered to take such measures
as in its discretion will most effectively cause
offending Associations to desist and refrain from such
discriminatory practices and policies.

BRIEF SURVEY OF JAPANESE ECONOMIC POSITION
Foreign Trade
Imports into Japan proper exceeded exports for the
first seven months of this year by the all-time reoord
of 730 million yen (209 million dollars) as compared
with a negative balance of 399 million yen (87 million
dollars) for the same months of 1936, and with 71
million yen (20 million dollars) for all of 1936. For
the entire Japanese Empire the negative balance in 1936
was 135 million yen (39 million dollars).
D
04
Average monthly exports in 1936 were 63.5 million
dollars and imports were 65.3 million dollars. In the
first six months of l937, average exports were 77
894.51
million dollars, with imports 108 million dollars. In
July imports were 84 peroent higher than last year,
while exports were only 33 peroent higher.
The unusually large negative balance was in part
accounted for by higher unit values of artioles of
F/FG
import, the inorease in whioh has been especially
marked

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.98)
-a
marked and greater than the inorease in unit prioes of
exports. In terms of quantity, both imports and exports
inoreased, but again the rise in imports was much in
exoess of that for exports. Iron, ores, and textile
fibers showed the greatest inorease in terms of value
in Japanese imports. Some of these imports apparently
resulted in inoreased stocks in Japan and it is antioi-
pated that the rate of import will not be oontinued
throughout the remainder of the year. Acoording to an
artiole in the Qriental Soonomiet for July l937, this
is partioularly true in the case of cotton, the imports
of whioh in the first five months inoreased in value by
47 peroent over the same months of 1936 and by 25 peroent
in terms of quantity. After surveying stocks and the
market position in Japan, the artiole conoludes that
"the inward movement of raw cotton seems destined to
taper off during the balance of this year." Imports
of wool are also expeoted to be less in the second
half than in the first half of the year, although
greater than imports in the seoond half of 1936. On
the other hand, imports of iron and steel materials,
including sorap, and of machinery and equipment, are
expected to continue at the same high rate whioh pre-
vailod

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)

The seoond half of the calendar year is usually

that even though war imports continue, the total nega-
tive balanoe will be less at the close of the year than
at the end of July. On the basis of trade figures for
the firat five months and attempts to estimate imports
and exports for the remainder of the year, the Qriental

oolonies of about 350 million yen (over 100 million

The foregoing estimate, however, mist have been
prepared before Japanese activities in China had

Government Debt
At the end of June 1937 the funded debt of the Japan-
ese Government in domestic obl1gations was 9,264,662,000
yen, a new record high for this debt. In addition there
were rioe notes outstanding in the amount of 438 million
yen, which 1s somewhat less than the amount at the end of
June 1936.
The axternal debt of the Japanese Government as of
June 30, 1937 1s reported at 1,315,639,000 yen, at the
old par value of the yen (approximately 50). Outstand-
ing foreign ourrency bonds of local governments amounted
to another 200 million yen, and foreign currency corpora-
tion bonds to about 320 million yen. The total outstanding
external obligations of Japanese governments and corpora-
tions were equivalent to about 916 million dollars, of
which about 656 million dollars were obligations of the
Japanese Government, l00 million dollars of local govern-
ments, and l60 milllon of Japanese corporations, largely
public utilities. Some of the municipal and corporate
issues are guaranteed by the National Government. Due to
retirement operations, outstanding external obligations in
all categories have shown a steady decline in recent years
and are approximately 10 percent lower than they were two
years ago. Total Japanese obligations payable in foreign
ourrenoies

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.98)

strueture. So far as the Governmental debt is concerned,
external obligations account for only 13 1/2 percent of
the aggregate, and as pointed out below, a considerable
amount of these have been repatriated.
Moody's Bond Survey reports an estimate that repa-
triated and Japanese-owned bonds are from 50 to 60 percent
of the total outstanding external debt. Another authority
estimates that of the total dollar issues outstanding, 60
percent are held outside the United States (1.e. have
been repatriated or are owned by other foreign investors).

of dollar issues out-

1933 and are about 58 million dollars at present,
according to Moody's estimates, and when allowance is

country do not anticipate defaults in service on Japan-
ese foreign issues, although there may be substantial

the psyohologlcal effeots of a prolonged war upon the
seourity markets.
Returning to the internal debt,the Commercial
Attach reports that the Government's defioit finanoing

Budgot
Japanese budgets have for a considerable number of
years been run on a deficit basis, the balance being
achieved through def1oit loans. Prior to 1932 these
defioits seldom exceeded 200 m1llion yen. In 1933 the
defioit jumped to 6l9 milllon and has consistently
remained above that figure in every subsequent year.
The deficit estimate in the budget for the ourrent
fiscal year is 77l million but this figure will almost
certainly be exceeded as a result of Japanese military
operations. The cable of August 3 from the Commercial
Attaché reports an appropriation of 10 million yen from
reserve funds and a supplementary budget of 96,800,000
yen, practically all of which is to be raised through
bond issues, and another bill providing expenditures of
400 million yen was, according to the press, expeoted
to be introduced in the near future.
g014
In order to meet the drains on its balanoe of pay-
ments, Japan has recently been exporting gold. By
August 85, 142 million dollars of Japanese gold had
been

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.98)
a
been shipped to this oountry since March 1937. The
total gold reserves of the Bank of Japan and the Japan-
ese Government inereased steadily throughout 1936 to a
high of 469 million dollars at the end of February 1937.
By the end of May (the latest available) these reserves
had fallen to 452 million dollars, and gold shipments
in June and July will have further reduced this total.
Japan has greatly stressed the development of its
gold-mining industry, and the yield for the ourrent year
1s estimated at some 60 million dollars as compared with
24 million dollars in 1936 and 15 million dollars in
1933. If gold production does reach the estimated 60
million dollars this year, it will be twice the total
of the annual net service requirements for the Japanese
external debt.
Other Invisibles in the Balance of Payments
Shipping receipts, and to a smaller extent invest-
ment returns, have in the past been important items in
offsetting Japan's usual adverse balance of trade. The
most recent figures available indicate a net income
from current invisibles in 1936 of 120 million yen
(about 35 million dollars). No estimates are available
for these items in 1937, although Moody's Bond Survey
for

for August 23, 1937 comments that "with conditions
improved throughout the world and the volume of world
trade generally expanding, higher yields from the
sources mentioned, particularly from the Japanese mer-
chant marine, seem indicated."

Reference Kobe's report no. 245 of
April 16, 1938, entitled "Additional Informa-
tion on Recent Economic Trends in Western
Japan!
The most interesting of the miscellaneous
information reported by Consul McConaughy in
the despatch under reference is as follows:
Import control is being enforcod with
great rigidity in Kobe. A number of Japanese
buyers of essential materials, having no con-
fidence in their ability to obtain import and
exchange permits, have declined even to attempt
to obtain such permits and have voluntarily
paid prices from 30 to 40 percent higher than
the prices quoted for the competing imported
products. It is the Consul's conclusion that
the Japanese attach more importance to the con-
servation of their exchange resources than they
do to the curbing of the tendency toward an
exhorbitant price level. (Note: There seems
to have been some change in this attitude since
the appointment of the new Finance Minister
Mr. Ikeda.)
Exports from Kobe to the United States
continue to decline. Shipments in March 1938
from Kobe and Osaka to the United States were
75 percent below those of March 1937.
There

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
There is an acute shortage of export bills
in most of the banks in Kobe except in the
Yokohama Specie Bank. Recent rulings and
regulations have had the tendency of centraliz-
ing foreign exchange dealings in the Yokohama
Specie Bank.
The need for gold is so great in Japan
that, according to the Consul's understanding,
gold bearing ore of very low grade is being
worked in Japan even in cases where the cost.
of mining ore is greater in yen than the assay
value of the gold obtained.
There are no signs of overt labor unrest.
According to the Consul's information, "it ap-
pears to be the intention of the Jananese to
maintain at least the present low level of
exports at all cost, even if it requires the
ruthless sweating of home and small factory
labor in order to neutralize the rising cost
of raw materials."
Several exporters feel that a slight re-
vival of exports to the United States is
imminent. Exporters are inclined to
minimize the importance of boycott sentiment
in the United States.

developments since the date of that despatch.
RECORDING DESK
into evidence in recent weeks is the greater rigidity
of import control. Several instences have come to the
attention of this Consulate where Japanese buyers of
essential materials, havin no confidence in their
ability to obtain import and exchange permits, have
declined even to attempt to obtain such permits and have
voluntarily paid prices 30 to 40 per cent higher than
the price quoted on the imported product for competing
Japanese materials. Japanese manufacturers in this dis-
trict seem to be fully reconciled to the payment of these
higher

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
higher prices. Foreign merchants find their inability
to sell even when they can substantially underquote
Japanese competitors very disturbing. This development
is of special interest inasmuch as it indicates that the
Japanese attach more importance to the conservation of
their exchange resources than they do to the curbing of
the tendency toward an exorbitant price level.
With further reference to the subject of prices
it should be noted that retail prices on most consumer
goods increased substantially on April 1st due to the
enforcement on that date of the new emergency merchandise
taxes on a number of articles such as furniture, liquors,
toilet goods, clothing, jewelry, and recreational articles.
These taxes are contained in the China Emergency Special
Tax Law promulgated by Law No. 51 of March 30, 1938.
As a result of an investigation of prices in various
retail stores in Kobe, I am satisfied that there has
been little if any effort in the larger stores to
capitalize on this tax by increasing retail prices more
than the tax would warrant. In the larger stores the
price increase seems to be substantially proportional to
the amount of the new tax. However, there is some
evidence to indicate that smaller shops are not as con-
scientious in the matter of prices. The imposition of
the tax is in itself a contribution to the inflationist
psychology which appears to be a factor in the steady but
fairly gradual rise in prices.
Despite the effort of the government to discontinue
gold shipments to the United States, three large shipments
of

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
of gold valued at about $17,500,000 have gone forward
from the Yokohama Specie Bank, Kobe, consigned to the
Yokohama Specie Bank in San Francisco within the past
three weeks. It is believed that these shipments were
made with great reluctance and were despatched through
sheer necessity to pay the adverse balance occasioned
by large essential imports over and above the amount of
the greatly shrunken Japanese export shipments to the
United States.
Exports to the United States from this district are
diminishing at an accelerated rate. March 1938 shipments
from Kobe and Osaka were more than 75 per cent below
exports to the United States for March 1937. Exports to
other areas are also substantially off compared with
March shipments of recent years, although the decline
elsewhere is less precipitate than that cited above.
There is an acute shortage of export bills in most of the
local banks other than the Yokohama Specie Bank. Many
banks are unable to accomodate their customers with foreign
currency even when the customers are able to present
exchange permits.
The effect of recent financial and banking regulations
imposed by the Ministry of Finance has been to centralize
foreign exchange dealings in the Yokohama Specie Bank.
The
tendency of foreign exchange bills to gravitate to the
Yokohama Specie Bank is causing great concern to the
smaller Japanese banks in this city as well as to the
American, British, and Dutch banks. Even on the smaller
margin of business left to them the rigid control of
rates

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
rates makes it impossible for the banks to increase
their profit margin on exchange by engaging in any sort
of manipulation. The foreign exchange brokers in Kobe
and Osaka are in a serious plight. They are doing only a
small fraction of the business which formerly came to
them. Under the present system there appears to be little
or no need for brokers and what business they obtain is
chiefly on a personal friendship basis.
With further reference to the gold situation, reports
are current here that the continued necessity of exporting
gold bullion to offset adverse international trade balances
is seriously depleting gold reserves. It is understood
that the need for gold is so great that gold bearing ore
of a very low grade is being worked in Chosen even in
cases where the cost of mining the ore is greater in yen
that the assay value of the gold obtained.
Employment appears to be maintained on a fairly steady
basis. There is some evidence to indicate that there has
been a slight diversion of workers from the light consumer
goods occupations to the chemical and heavy industries.
There has been some decline in employment in the cotton
spinning industry as compared with 1937. This decline was
not shown in the employment figures cited in despatch No.
235 above referred to which gave only the figures for
January 1938 and January 1937. Enclosed is a table giving
employment for each month from January 1937 through February
1938. It will be seen from these figures that there was
a decline in employment of 7.5 per cent in February 1938
as compared with the high month which was June 1937.
It

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
It is reasonably certain that in general wages are
not being raised in this district; it is quite certain
that wages are not keeping pace with the rise in the
price level. I can perceive no manifest signs of overt
labor unrest but it is inevitable that many manual
laborers are already feeling the pinch of decreasing pur-
chasing power.
Several foreign buyers and exporters of Japanese
merchandise, who are in daily contact with small Japanese
manufacturers, have told me that it appears to be the
intention of the Japanese to maintain at least the present
low level of exports at all costs, even if it requires
the ruthless sweating of home and small factory labor in
order to neutralize the rising cost of raw materials.
Several exporters have stated that small orders from
the United States are beginning to filter in after an
almost complete lull of several months. They feel that
a small increase in Japanese exports to the United States,
in certain lines at least, may therefore occur in the
next few months. They ascribe these recent orders to a
depletion of stocks of Japanese goods in the United States
which has rectified the overbought situation which pre-
vailed for many months. Most local exporters are still
inclined to minimize the importance of any boycott senti-
ment in the United States. It is their belief that boy-
cott sentiment is negligible in its ultimate effect on
Japanese exports, and that the chief deterrent influences
militating against a return of Japanese exports to normal
levels

=== Page 351 === (confidence 1.00)
-6-
levels are the rise in price of export commodities
simultaneously with depressed business conditions abroad
and the diversion of many raw materials needed in the
manufacture of export goods to the uses of domestic
industry which has many of the earmarks of a boom status.
Respectfully yours,
Walter P. McConaughy
American Consul

Reference Kobe's no. 257, May 18,
1938, "Economic Trends in Western
Japan".
Consul Scott in Kobe appears to
have adopted the policy of writing the
Department each month in regard to
economic trends in western Japan--a
mixture of fact and impression which
is useful and to be commended. (This
1s the third of these reports.)
Exports from Kobe and Osaka to the
United States continue to decline dras-
tically, both absolutely and comoared
with last year. In January, 1938, ex-
ports were 62 percent below those of
January, 1937; in Apr1l, 1938, thev
were 85 percent below April, 19?7. In
April, 1937, exports from Kobe and
Osaka to the United States amounted to
$7,160,814; 1n Apri1, 1938, they amounted
to $1,156,651. Exporte to other coun-
tries have not declined nearly as much.
Most of the traders in Kobe and Osaka
attribute the decline to the business
recession

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
recession in the United States and high
prices in Japan and minimize the inoor-
tance of boycott sentiment.
Real wages are not keeping pace
with the cost of livingalthough laborers
have received substantfal pay increases.
White collar workers are suffering most.
Textiles of mixed cotton and staple
fiber sell in Japan for 37.34 per piece
as against $2.19 for its pure cotton
equivalent (which may be exnorted only).
The foreign exchange situation is
serious. There may be discrimination
against foreign firms in the granting
of foreign exchange--but there is so
little exchange to be had, specific
discriminations are lost in the general
picture.
The lack of exchange is impairing
the credit standing of substantial
firms. It 1s not infrequent that the
authorities promise exchange, firms
order goods, and the exchange is not
forthcoming. Companies of the highest
credit rating are being required to
post irrevocable letters of credit.
The discipline of the people ap-
pearexcellent.

Certain well derined tendencies have teen pointed out,
namely, the murked decrease in forei  trade, the rise in
prices, the c ntinuance of internal business activities at
a hi h level, purticularly those stinul ted by wer needs
with the consequent maintenance of both em loyment and wa e
scales, and, finally, the difficulties due to the govern-
ment's policy of limiting imports. In general these tonden-
cies have been intensified luring the last few weeks. This
F/A
is particularly true with regard to the diminishing exports
to the United States from this re ion, which show for the
month of April a decline of approximately 85 per cent for
the

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the Osaka and Kobe districts as compared to exports for
April of last year. So far in May the decline is even
more marked. For detailed statement see enclosure No. 1.
As far as this Consulate can check, exports from this
rogion to England, Canada, and other countries while
lower do not show nearly as great a decline as those to
:the United States. The extremely drastic fallin. off in
purchases from America lends color to the theory that the
boycott of Japarese goods in the United States is being
felt. On the other hand, this drop coincides with a time
of high prices for Japanese goods coupled with the busi-
ness recession in the United States so that it is dif-
ficult to determine whether the boycott has an appreciable
effect or whether the falling off is due to natural
economic causes. Most traders in this region still appear
to minimize the importance of the  oycott as a factor in
the trade decline.
As before stated, there is no unemployment problem
and even firms which are losing money are keeping their
employees on their payrolls. While there is no problem
of unemployment, this should not be taken necessarily to
mean that there is no unemployment since, due to the
peculiar family system prevailing in Japan, many people
who have been thrown out of work are cared for in some way
by the family, and in any case are ept off the streets.
For example, with the great decrease in foreign trade
stevedoring work has declined greatly. This means that
about 50% of the ordinary day labor stevedores are out of
work or have been forced to seek employment in the heavy
industries

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
industries. Another example of labor adjustment which is
becoming necessary due to the present emergency is in the
case of chauffeurs. Many owners of private cars, who are
now unable to use their cars to very great advantage
because of the gasoline restrictions, are either dismissing
or .considering dismissing their chauffeurs. Other examples
of this sort could be given but so far at least no actual
problem has been created.
Although real wages are not keeping pace with the
cost of living, the laboring classes have received sub-
stantial increases and some companies have given some forn
of wage increases to their clerical staff to partially
offset higher livins costs. As is eneral, however, in
such situations the white collar class are being pinched
the hardest.
In general the rising tendency of prices has continued
although there ere numerous articles which are actually
lower in price now than seven or eight months ago. As
might be expected, the articles which have risen in price
are first imported oods, second, oods whose chief
ingredients are imported or competitive with a corresponding
imported material, and, third, highly processed articles
whose price reflects greater production costs. Prices of
artioles where government control plays a part have showm
the most striking increase. Textiles of mixed cotton and
staple fiber, for instance, now sell in Japan for $3.34
per piece (a piece is 44 inches by 46 yards) as against a
price of $2.19 per piece for the pure cotton equivalent.
Local

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
Local business men do not feel that the government
will be able to curb prices successfully.
In previous reports the question of discrimination
was touched upon. As will be recalled, last March quite
a vigorous protest was made by foreign firms on this score.
While inequalities continue to occur there appear to be
less complaints of direct discrimination than at first.
No doubt, other things being equal, there is a tendency
to favor Japanese firms at the expense of forein interests
engaged in similar business but actually the exchange
control is so crastic, and it is so difficult for either
foreign or Japanese firms to acquire funds for financing
imports that the question of specific discrimination has
been somewhat lost in the eneral picture.
The most interesting tendency is the increasing
evidence of the extreme shortago of foreign exchane
availabilities as shown by the lengths to which the
Japanese Government is prepared to go to avoid granting
foreign exchange permits. Numerous interesting examples
of this have come to light recently.
In many cases business men complain that although
exchange is promised or at least not refused no action
is taken on the pending requests. It is understood that
even permits for war materials have been held up. In
several cases it is understood that even vessels already
loaded with petroleum products have been held in American
ports pending the receipt of the necessary exchange permits.
▲ story going the rounds in Kobe for which I cannot vouch,
but which is presumably based on fact, is that Mitsui and
Company

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Company recently offered a large donation in yen to the
Army through patriotic motives but that the military
authorities refused the donation in money requesting Mitsui
to donate the equivalent value in imported war materials.
More humble examples, however, whose details are entirely
known to the Consulate will serve to indicate the great
difficulty in securing even a small amount of exchange.
Recently the Governor of Hyogo Ken, at the behest of the
president of one of the leading clubs in Kobe, requested,
during the occasion of the Governors' meeting in Tokyo from
May 1st to May 7th, the Ministry of Finance to allow a
mall amount of exchane for the purpose of liquor and other
necessary supplies. This request on the part of the Governor
was curtly refused, however, and he was told that if it
were too difficult to obtain liquor the members could o
without. Other examples of this sort could be cited.
nother confirmation of the continued shortage of
foreign exchange, coupled with the government's desire to
maintain the position of the yen, is the fact that in
spite of the statement appearing in the press some time
ago that old shipments would be discontinuod they still
continue. On May 2nd the one billion yen mark was passed.
A detailed table of gold shipments to date is shown as
enclosure No. 2.
One bad result of the tightening of exchange is the
effect it has had in impairing the credit standing of
really substantial firms. Racently, for example, one of
the best Kobe firms of hitherto impeccable standing
failed to remit funds to cover the purchase of raw cotton
purchased
心

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
purchased from America. It seems that the firm in
question somewhat ill-advisedly had relied on an
assurance from Tokyo that necessary covering exchange
for this transaction could be obtained and had so
informed its American client. However, the exchange
permit was not forthcoming al though the goods hed been
shipped on the strength of the promise. Undoubtedly this
house will make good in time but the net result has been
most prejudicial to its good name and standing. This
case is typical of similar cases in Kobe anu Osaka. In
view of this situation even companies of the highest
rating are now being required to post irrevocable letters
of credit to obtain merchandise.
Although it is understood that the government has
made an attempt to have the necessary exchange permits
coincide with the correspondin import licenses, com-
plaints on this score have again begun to be noticed
since April lst. One of the leadin importers recently
vigorously criticized this lack of coordination, pointing
out that to grant import licenses wi thout the necessary
exchange was highly improper, since in effect it deceived
foreign firms who sold goods on the basis of an import
permit and afterwards were unable to obtain effective pay-
ment.
In spite of the pressure to obtain foreign funds this
Consulate has not found any very tangible evidence of an
unofficial bootleg market having developed on a large
scale. Here and there certain individuals, who for one
reason or another have large yen balances frozen in Japan,
have

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
have offered substantial premiums to obtain dollar exchange
but there is no widespread practice as far as can be seen.
From the psychological side there is little change.
It is apparent that even the lower classes realize that
difficulties have been encountered in liquidating the
"China emergency", quickly, and that it will be a long
drawn out and unpleasant process. The discipline, however,
appears excellent and the mass of the people seem pre-
pared for whatever may come. Perhaps this stoical attitude
should be somewhat discounted by the fact that the
omorgency has actually stimulated business and employ-
ment so that while the basic situation may be unhealthy the
average individual is still fairly well off. In any case
there is little to indicate in this region at present
that economic pressure from below will have an appreciable
influence on major Japanese policies.
Respectfully yours,
Uruttuop Rxhott
Winthrop R. Scott

In an interview with the press Mr. E. W. James,
Chairman of the Kobe and Osaka Foreign Chamber of
Commerce and head of one of the largest Kobe British
import and export firms, recently expressed his
viewpoints of the general economic situation in Japan.
These are briefly given below.
Japan's Export Trade Chiefly Affected by Conditions
Abroad.
The chief causes of the decline in the export
trade of Japan are not to be sought in Japan itself,
but abroad. The greatest single factor is the general
world depression. But world trade is improving and
Japan is facing the best months, the export half of
the year.
Japan a Spender.
Exports may be so far improved as to create a
balance in trade. This will not be a favorable balance,
because Japan is a great spender and will not hesitate
to spend what it makes.
Trade Doors Not Closed.
The creation of "Tianchukuo" did not close trade
doors in the Far East. On the contrary, there was a
very big increase in imports of all Far Eastern
countries, particularly Japan. In 193l Japan's inports
totalled Yen 1,235 million and in 1936, Yen 2,753 million.
Maintenanoe of Yen.
No ohange in the value of the yen is expected
either through Government action or the pressure of
events.
Lowering

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Lowering the yen would not be likely to aid
exports but would probably be the excuse for fresh
tariffs or new quota systems.
The yen is also not likely to be forced dovm in
spite of efforts to pes it. Japan's credit is good.
It is one of the very few nations which have not
defaulted on any debt, either foreign or domestic.
Japan's foreisn debt is very small.
The Japanese Government still buys bonds on
foreign markets whenever the price is favorable.
This is an indication that the country is not as
poorly off as sone suppose. It is also evidence that
the Government has not the slightest intention of not
servicing bonds or not redeeming them when due.
War with Little Effect on Ordinary Life.
"Those who believe Japan is lheading towards col-
lapse have their money on the wrong horse." This is
obvious to any resident in Japan who can see just what
the extent of the strain on the nation really is.
The economic regulations give an entirely mis-
leading impression abroad. The regulations are all
very strict, but Japan is not a European country and
it is misleading to judge the effects from a European
viewpoint. It would be a hardsllip for a European to
have to use wooden shoes; it is not a hardship at all
for a Japanese. Many of them, for one reason or
another, have never worn leather.
No Labor Shortage.
Many foreign observers believed that Japan would
experience a labor shortage in time of war. This has
not

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
not been the oase.
Shifts in industry have produced a number of un-
employed, but these could be, and are being, drafted
into munition works and other industries.

livieio: 40
FAR EASTERN 
March 8, 1939.
The question which the Embassy puts before
itself: "Is there statistical proof that
economic sanctions would cause the collapse of
Japan?"; and its answer: "Our opinion is that
there is not", are an astonishing manifesta-
tion.
It should be assuned that the Embassy
knows what it means when it uses the word
"proof". but there can be no "proof" in
advance that any specified group of measures
applied by a large number of human beings to
some other large number of hunan beings will
have a specified and inevitably resulting
effect. Even in the physical sciences, the
most that the experts will undertake to do
is to preuict that certain given causes will
have certain given effects.
The Embassy does state that a complete
embargo on Japan's goods put into effect by
certain countries, including the United States,
would "prove disastrous for Japan's economy".
Query: What does the Embassy mean by
"disastrous"?
Reduced to simple terms, the statistics
and the analysis which the Embassy has pre-
pared lead the Embassy to the conclusion that
there is not warrant for a assumption that
economic sanctions by themselves would "stop
Japan" and that imposition of economic sanc-
tions would tend to cripple Japan and might
cause Japan to resort to the retaliation of
armed

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
- 2 -
armed force. This amounts only to testimony;
it is an affirmation of an opinion. As an
opinion it happens to coincide with an opinion
hitherto held in many quarters and which
therefore tends to confirm an opinion which
has often already been expressed.
The analysis does not "prove" that
x plus y woulu produce z. Equally, it does
not prove that x plus y would not equal z.
It "proves" nothing -- nothing either affirma-
tive or negative.
2111
Stanley K. Hornbeck
PA/H:SKH:ZMK

MR. HORNBECK

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERNAF
March 8, 1939.
PL:
Reference Tokyo's despatch no. 3679 of
February 14, l939, entitled "Japan's Economic
Vulnerability".
The report under reference is 35 pages
long and contains 6 enclosures. The first 15
pages of the report convey certain information
in regard to Japan's trade balance and gold
movements. The report has the advantage of
working with statistics for the full year of
1938 but it achieves approximately the same
result as previous estimates of Japan's debit
trade balance for 1938 based on statistics
for only part of the year. For example, the
report estimates that the debit trede balance
of the yen bloc during l938 was 575 million
yen; Reuter's "X-Ray Economio Service" had
previously estimated it at approximately the
same figure. As regards gold movements there
appears to be no new information presented by
the report.
On page 14 there are certain conclusions
which the Embassy at Tokyo has already tele-
grephed to the Department, conclusions such
as (1) "conclusive evidence is lacking to show
that the methods of meeting the past difficulty
(of financing the foreign trade) are them-
selves incapable of intensification in degree,
conformably

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
oonformably to the need of suoh partial
economio reprisals as might be attempted" and
(2) "whether the collapse of Japan would
intervene in the process is a question the
answer to which must be found in data of the
greatest complexity, and the Embassy can not
point to proof that collapse would follow
sanctions".
In the last half of the report there are
discussed, paragraph by peragraph, two reports
previously received by the Embassy and by the
Department on the question of Japan's vulnera-
bility to sanctions, namely, the British Am-
bassador's memorandun of December 31, 1938,
and a report on Japan's financial position by
Reuter's "X-Ray Economic Service". (The Em-
bassy did not have available a copy of
Reuter's report itself, but criticizes a tele-
graphic summary of the report sent by the Em-
bassy at Chungking.) Some of the criticisms
may be accepted as sound and others appear to
be of doubtful pertinence, but as far as
factual material is concerned the Embassy's
observations present little, if any, new
material of importance. These observations
do, however, discredit the data presented in
the above-mentioned reports as being of a
nature too indefinite to warrant the basing of
a policy thereon.
(As far as factual data are concerned,
I believe that the Department has more
accurate information, particularly as regards
gola

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
gold supplies than does the Embassy in Tokyo,
the British Ambassador or Reuter's. A
memorandum on this subject is being prepared
in EA and I believe that it shows that Japa-
nese gold potentialities are greater than are
indicated by any of the three reports under
discussion.)
In conclusion, the Embassy gives its
opinion in reply to the question which it
puts Is there statistical proof that economic
sanctions would cause the collapse of Japan?"
Answer: "Our opinion is that there is not.
. . . We can with assurance venture the
opinion that sanctions would be met by Japan
with a reaction comparable to the reaction
which war would provoke. . . . Japan can not
be deflected from the objectives which are
fundamental to her policy by miscellaneous
economic reprisals thrown in as incidentals
to British, American, and French trade pro-
tection. Sanctions against Japan might well
require a thoroughness and sacrifice which
American interests might not adequately
support, in any stte of mind short of war."
The Embassy's opinion (pages 34 and 35)
appears to take cognizance of the question of
the extent of economic sanctions and to
realize that the effects in Japan of sanctions
might be commensurate with the degree by which
they are applied  foreign countries.When
one considers the matter of degree, small
differences of opinion in regard to Japan's
trade

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 4 -
trade balance, gold supplies, et cetera, be-
come relatively unimportant.' If Great Britain,
France, and the United States, for example,
decided upon and put into effect a complete
embargo of Japan's goods, it could not fail,
and the Embassy so states, to "prove disastrous
for Japan's economy".
The Embassy has taken the position in
this report that no air-tight proof has been
presented to it that sanctions would cause
Japan's economic collapse. This is true, but
it would seem that foreign policy, as ali
other aspects of life, must of necessity be
based upon probabilities.
The Embassy was requested to ccmment
upon previous reports dealing with Japan's
financial position and the report under
reference is of interest in connection with
the general discussion of the matter which
is going on at the present time. It requires,
however, critical consideration and should
be viewed with persnective.

of possible economic sanctions against Japan. Telegrams

of American Companies in Japan at the Beginning of 1939,

It is assumed that the materials cited have been
already studied, and for the sake of brevity no attempt
will be made in the present despatch to take out from
those materials all pertinent data.
I.
Japan has by force imposed a control over Manchuria,
north China, and (to a lesser extent) other parts of China,
thereby establishing an area within which trade is possible
without the intervention of any foreign exchange transaction.
Not only is no claim to a currency other than the yen or its
subsidiaries created or required in this trade, but also
control

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
control over the area is such that there are means for the
postponement of effective payment. The area may well be
termed the yen area.
Economic sanotions against Japan, it is surmised, would
not contemplate the interposing of any direct obstacles to
the continuance, nor to the augmenting, of trade within the
yen area. It is presumed that economic sanctions would de-
pend for their effectiveness solely upon the consequences
of interference in the trade relations of the yen area with
the outside world in co modities, gold, services, and credit.
A first step in anticipating the consequences of such inter-
ference if attempted is to appraise the current trade of
Japan within and without the yen area.
The most authoritative references with regard to the
current trade of Japan remain the official returns of the
Japanese Government. It is not impossible that these are
falsified for military reasons, although the Embassy has
not been able to find support for the assertion of falsifi-
cation in any of several instances in which business men
have suggested that they believe imports are coming into
the country which are ignored in the statistics. It seems
likely that at least some direct purchases by the military
do not enter into the published returns. The subject is of
such importance that the Embassy suggests, as one cheok,
a comparison of American and other foreign records of exports
to Japan in 1938 as against the Japanese published figures.
(This will be reverted to below.) For the time being the
Japanese statistics must be approached with reservation.
1.
Enolosed with this despatch is copy of the Monthly
Return of the Foreign Trade of Japan, December 1938, which
includes

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
inoludes official figures for the entire year l938. It
is published by the Japanese Department of Finance. The
upper table on page 3 of the Return summarizes the com-
modity trade of Japan with foreign countries in 1936,
1937, and 1938; and in this sense "Japan" means the
entire Japanese Empire except Chosen and Taiwan, and
not including the Japanese Mandated Islands. The lower
table on page 3 includes Chosen, Taiwan, and the Mandated
Islands. From the footed totals of the table on page
1l9 of the Return can be obtained the value of 1938
commodity trade between Japan and respectively Manchuria,
Kwantung Leased Territory, north China, central China,
and south China. It is explained in the Return that
north China comprises Hopeh, Shantung, Shansi, Shensi,
and Kansu; that central China comprises Kiangsu, Chekiang,
Anhwei, Honan, Hupeh, Hunan, Kiangsi, and Szechwan; and
that south China comprises Fukien, Kwangtung, Kwangsi,
Kweichow, and Yunnan. Pages 8 through 1l furthermore
contain a table breaking down Japan's foreign trade for
1936, 1937, and 1938 by countries, but in this table
there is no division of China into northern, central,
and southern. Both the page 8 and page 1l9 tables, how-
ever, are based on the foreign trade of the Japanese
Empire with the exception of Chosen and Taiwan and not
including the Mandated Islands; in other words both the
page 8 and page 119 tables are itemizations growing out
of the upper, and not the lower, table on page 3. It
would indeed be more satisfactory if this limitation
were not the faot, but the complete figures are not
obtainable

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
obtainable except on application to the respective Chosen,
Taiwan, and Mandated Islands administrations, and commu-
nication wi th them would require some time. Furthermore,
it is believed that the foreign trade of Chosen, Taiwan,
and the Mandated Islands, in itself not large, was in all
probability proportionately more concentrated upon the yen
area than was the general foreign trade of Japan (see tables
111,123, and 124, pages 248 and 274, 37th Financial and Eco-
nomic Annual of Japan, 1937, for 1936 trade distribution;
enclosed with despatch 2859, "arch 31, 1938), end there-
fore that deductions deriving from the high ratio of Japan's
trade which is with the yen area would not be weakened were
the complete figures used. In the instance of Taiwan,
for example, 74.8 percent of her 1938 exports went to Man-
churia, Kwantung Leased Territory, and other parts of
China, end 70.8 percent of her 1938 imports came from
those laces (figures obtained by the Embassy from the

foreign exports of Chosen, Taiwan, and the Mandated Is-
lands were valued at Yen 207,092,786 (difference between
upper and lower tables, page 3 of the Return ), but im-
ports were also of such dimensions as to cause little net
increase to the export balance (from Yen 26,340,125 to
Yen 60,542,299); and there is no reason whatever to suppose
that the foreign trade of Chosen, Taiwan, and the Mandated
Islands was in such directions as to have given Japan a
net favorable balance on foreign exchange. For Taiwan
the reverse was true in l938, by slightly more than two
million yen.

There seems no probability that Japan's control

IV.
That gold exports from Japan have not been a matter
of policy for the building up of credits for future use,
but have been required to cover commodity imports, is a
conclusion which, thou h not indisputably proved by the
evidence, is at least convincin ly supported. That the
commodity imports thereby rinanced were minimum current
recuirements would, however, be a dangerous deduction, in
the Embassy's opinion.
Evidence is not leckin
that conmercial needs have been
very speringly fed, ut the stete of stocks of strateic
materials is a subject about which little is known. Japan
has for several years faced an extremely threatening inter-
national situation, end there seems every likelihood that
careful effort should i ve been mede to prepare as well
as possible for the needs of the future, whether it be war
against the Soviet Union or the imposition of econonic
sanctions or some other major catastrophe. hile the world
is yet willing to purchase gold (a willingness the continuation
of which mi ht be under doubt) and while the world is yet
willing to sell goods to Japan, the financing of essential
commodity imports by the sale of cold miht well present
itself as the policy of wisdom, end have so presented itself
throughout 1937 and 1938. Indeed, the immediate expenditure
of the currency reserve for the purchase of necessary primary
materials is understood to be under serious consideration.
Cil is one commodity in which imports in 1938 have very
probably exceeded consumption. The large imports into
Manchuria

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.98)
-15 -
Manchuria (chiefly by Dairen) must be taken into account;
and there are reports now and then of tankers discharging
in Japan at points which are not ports of entry. Whether
such cargoes enter into the declered returns of the nearest
port of entry is not mown. Anerican export figures in
this important co modity must be evailable for purposes of
checkin.
In addition to the itemized caterories of the Japanese
import returns there is one large and important lumped
figure w ich gives rise to considerable doubt: group xIv,
1/
ores and metals (page 59, December 1938 eturn). absolutely
no information other than the total yen valuation of this
category is disclosed. For 1936, it states, ores and metels
were imported to the velue of Yen 374,891,804; 1937,
Yen 901,130,824; 1938, Yen 661,894,693. The ubassy submits
that it is impossible to arrive at a convincing estimate of
the present state of stocks of products deriving from such
materials, or of the neterials thenselves.
