V.
Although internal adjustments have been necessary
for the prosecution of the hostilities in China, observers
are in ceneral accord that Japen does not show signs of
economic strain yet comperable to the stresses which were
successfully it stood by severl countries durin the
European wer of 1914-19le. It a me rs not impossible thet
furtier internel djustments could be resorted to in event
of need; in any event, any pertiel cceno ic reprisals which
might be attem ted shouid oe luunched only in full readiness
to carry them to the most intensive nd conplete execution
if recuired. The impossibility of borroving abroad which
Japan has feced in recent yecrs h:s orked e necessary
limiting of i ports. This obstucle e an has met with
relative success. The imposin of purticl economic reprisals
would further li..it the v lwne of J an's importing, whether
directly or by making payment more difficult; but the con-
sequent problems to c nfrent Japan would differ fron her
past difficulties (derivin fron the impossibility of foreign
borrowing) not in kind but in degree only. And conclusive
evidence is lackin to show that the methods of meeting the
past difficulty are themselves incapable of intensification
in degree, conformably to the need of such partial economic
reprisals as micht be attenpted. The effectiveness of
reprisals must depend upon their scope and intensity.
In recent years Japan has been able, though barred
from borrowing abroad, to finance her imports by one means
or another. She has also been able to carry through a
considerable expansion of her productive equipment. At the
present juncture, reconstruction and developmental projects
for China
are envisaged by Japan, and in these Japan's
program

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.97)
- 17 -
program would unquestionably be facilitated if she could
borrow abroad. Partial economic reprisals must work a
partial postponement of the reconstruction and developmental
projects for Chine envisaged by Japan, and they would slow
down the pace of the recent expansion of Japan's productive
mechanism, before the country vould be faced with the necessity
of an cbsolute retro ression.
Economic sanctions guinst Japan must inevitably throw
Japan even more intently upon the yen urea. Japun's effort
would certainly be to develop the ureu as an exclusive
economic unit. Japan would suifer from serious dislocations
in accustomed sources of goods, time woulu assuredly be
required to work into a new trade e uilibrium solely within
the area, and in the process the mode and standard of livine
in Japan (in itself not more than a eneration old, and
therefore not as serious a loss as in some ccuntries) would
undoubtedly suffer & set-back of some years; but whether
the collapse of Japan would intervene in the process is a
question the answer to which must be found in data of the
greatest compl xity, and the Embassy can not point to proof
that collapse would follow sanctions.

VI.
On December 30, 1938, the British Ambassador at
Tokyo handed to me a document embodying the British Embassy's
estimate of Japanese vulnerability to economic counter
measures acainst Japan. Copy of this document was transmitted
to the Department as enclosure to my strictly confidential
despatch 3591 of Januery 7, 1939. For brevity it will not
be reneated in full here, but the following are our conments
upon it, para raph by par raph. The  ritish mbassy
memorandum is in twenty unnumbered p regraphs end seven
numbered conelusions. The numbers below refer to the
respective paragraphs of the memorandum as if numbered:
1. The ritish Embassy assertion that Japan's progress
might in the pust have been checked by foreign countries
is probebly correct. hether or not such checking would
heve been warranted is not touched upon. hether or not
Japanese purchases of named essential materials must here-
after increase is conjectural.
2. The ritish Embassy's statement that between 1918
and 1932 Japan raised loans in the United States and in
the United Kingdom amounting to 468 million dollars and
52,300,000 pounas accords closely with data given on
pages 74, 88, and 122 of the 37th Financial and Economic
Annual of Japan, 1937, published by the Department of
Finance of the Japanese Government (enclosed with our
despatch 2859, March 31, 1938). However, when these sums
are distributed over the period of time during which the
loans were issued, the annual figure is not large. An
average annual increment during those years of approximately
fifty million dollars to the external funded debt of the
Japanese Empire was no great burden. During the period
1918 to 1932 Japanese industry and trade no doubt had the
assistange

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.98)
- 19 -
assistance of foreign credit additional to the funded sums
(as for example through the operations of American firms
with investments in Japan); but it is still not evident
that, even in total, financing from abroad was, under the
conditions of those years, so reat as to indicate a need
by Japan, under present c mditions, for continuing financial
assistance fron phro d.
3. It anpetrs that the ritish Enbassy is correct in
finding no likelilhood in the near future for Janen to borrow
abroad (even from her allies).
4. Profits from shipping have undoubtedly decreased
although the extent of shrinkage has not been made public.
Page 171 of the 37th  inancial and Economic Annual of Japan,
1937, ives a sonewhat higher fi ure for receipts in
connection with shi in in the year 1935 and in the year
1936 than does the _ritish Embassy give for profits from
shipping in a normal year.
The ritish Embassy figure of
from 140 million yen to 180 million yen is perhaps arrived at
by subtracting payments in connection with shipping (Yen
140,750,000 in 1936) from receipts in connection with shipping
(Yen 334,612,000 in 1936). In any event, close study of all
the items contained in the table of invisible trade as

VII.
In its telegram 47, January 23, 1939, 10 a.m.,
the Imbassy at Chungking conveyed to the Department
an analysis with regard to Japan's finances. Our
comment upon the respective numbered paragraphs
of that analysis is as follows:
1. It is a fact that quotations abroad on Japanese
bonds are low. Such quotations reflect foreign opinion
of Japan and uncertainty of Japan's future (taking into
account also possibilities of war), and are not simply
reflective of the foreign business estimate of Japan's
present economic condition. Political considerations
are a factor in such estimate, whereas the question of
the effectiveness of possible econonic sanctions is an
economic question.
2. In calculating Japan's balance of international
payments at the close of 1938 relative to the beginning
of l938 it is of course necessary to take account of
gold exports in addition to the commodity balance. When
this is done, no regression over the year l938 in Japan's
position in balance of international payments is seen.
Commodity imports and exports of Japan in 1938 were offi-
cially declared (upper table on page 3, Monthly Return
of the Foreign Trade of Japan, December 1938, enclosure 1
herewith) to be almost in equilibrium, a small export
surplus in fact (Yen 26,340,125). From the same return
(page 119) it oan be calculated that Japan, according
to Japanese official statement, exported to Manchuria,
Kwantung Leased Territory, and China (excluding Hongkong)
oommodities

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
- 29 -
commodities to the value of Yen 60l,489, 481 in excess
of the value of imports from those same areas. This
excess, which forms a part of the page 3 calculations,
cen be presumed not to have produced foreign exchange.
Making allowance for this circumstance, the total export
surplus of Yen 26,340,125 is reduced to an import surplus,
in commodities requiring payment in foreign exchange, of
Yen 575,149,356. But such foreign exchange requirement
was more than met by sale in the United States of gold
exported from Japan in 1938. This export of gold raises
problems for Japan, discussed elsewhere, which are indeed
great; but apparently it can not be shown that Japan has
lost ground in l938 in her effort to maintain the balance
of international payments.
3. According to the official Japanese statistics,
the proportion of Japan's exports which went to Manchuria,
Kwantung Leased Territory, and other parts of China in
1936 was 24.4 percent, in 1937 was 24.9 percent, and in
1938 was 43.3 percent. (This calculation is made from
page 8 of the December 1938 Return.) The table (page
119) in which the trade with China is broken down by
regions is a new table, and does not go back to l937
and 1936.
4. of total 1938 declared imports into Japan valued
1/ at Yen 2,663,337,186 (upper table on page 3, Monthly
Return of the Foreign Trade of Japan), imports from
Manchuria, Kwantung Leased Territory, and China (ex-
oluding Hongkong) took up Yen 564,050,829; of total
exports

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
- 30-
exports valued at Yen 2,689,677,311, exports to those
areas took up Yen 1,165,540,310. In 1938, 1t is clear,
imports into Japan played a smaller part in her trade
than did exports to those areas. Whether or not the
future will follow the same pattern is of course a
separate problem, not answered by the l938 statistics.
5. Although figures given are not the figures of
the Japanese Government, the principal assertion of this
paragraph is correct. The official return gives (upper
table, page 3, Return), in the commodity balance, an
import excess for Japan of Yen 70,705,913 in 1936, an
import excess of Yen 607,759,056 in 1937, and an export
excess of Yen 26,340,125 in 1938 (against which must be
set the export excess to Manchuria, Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory, and China, calculable from the official return as
Yen 601,489,481 for 1938).
6. Chosen and Taiwan probably produced no favorable
balance of foreign exchange in 1938 (see page 5 above).
In l938 Manchurian foreign trade was apparently a
liability rather than an asset in the matter of pro-
ducing foreign exchange. The Monthly Ciroular of the
Mitsubishi Economic Research Bureau for February 1939
(transmitted February 3, 1939) states at page 13, upper
left, that the 1938 trade balance of anchuria with
countries outside the yen bloc was adverse to the sum
of thirty million Manchurian yuan (which is Yen 30,000,O00).
It is also stated that in l937 Manchurian trade resulted
1n

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
-31 -
in a balance favorable to the extent of twenty-nine
million yuan. As for the foreign exchange yield of
exports from other areas of China occupied by Japa-
nese forces, this Embassy has no information.
7. That Japanese shipping yields less foreign
exchange than formerly is indubitable. Information
on which the extent of the loss might be estimated
has, however, been withheld from the public, and this
Embassy is in no position to confirm the estimates
contained in the seventh paragraph of the analysis
reported by Chungkin. There have ben public state-
ments in Japan that 1938 was a bad year for Japenese
shipping on ocean routes, that coastal shipping prospered,
and that shipping between Jepan and the continent was
active. The shipping companies paid dividends.
8. Balance from tourists in 1938 was certainly
very low.
9. The Embassy has no information which would seem
to be more accurate than the Department's estimates with
regard to Japanese interest and dividend payments abroad.
10. That Japan's 1936 (nineteen thirty-six) import
excess was financed by virtual liquidation of all foreign
exchange holdings is a dictum for which this Embassy
finds nothing approaching proof. On the other hand,
there is a striking correlation, in 1937 and 1938, be-
tween gold exports and the need for foreign exchange;
and

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
- 32 -
and it is true that Japanese governmental control
over foreign exohange remitting works a lag in Japan's
favor by postponing payment until the latest possible date.
1. Commercial stocks of imported raw materials are
low (whether or not in the degree asserted is unknown).
At the same time, the country has in part adjusted it-
self to a scale of exports and of consumption which will
in the future require smaller standing stocks of such
raw materials than in the past. is regards stocks of
materials used in the munitions industries, evidence
of inadequacy is lacking. In the matter of minerals
and metals, the final sentence of the eleventh para-
graph of the analysis reported by Chungking is fanciful.
1!
Japanese returns (pages 58 and 59, enclosure 1), while
still itemizing the group termed minerals and manu-
factures thereof and the group termed metal manufactures,
simply lumps under a single valuation total (without
indication even of tonnage) an obviously related group
termed ores and metals. It is of course for military
reasons that detailed information in this category is
withheld. Adding this lumped sum to the value of the
two itemized groups (but not including machines and
machinery, which would accentuate the divergence even
further) gives the following values for imports into
Japan: 1936, Yen 485,107,564; 1937, Yen 1,039,169,794;
1938,Yen 791,461,408. These value figures, while ad-
mittedly covering large unknowns, are of sufficient import
to discredit the attempt of the analysis reported by
Chungking

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
-33 -
Chungking to arrive at a tonnage comparison, at least
so far as concerns the drawing of any important de-
ductions.
12. Yen 200,000,000 is a plausible estimate for
newly mined gold to be produced in the Japanese Empire
in 1939. The analysis reported by Chungking estimates
present Jepanese gold stocks at a maximum value of
Yen 500,000,000. Even admitting these estimates as
hypotheses, Japan might have durin 1939 as much gold
as she sold in 1938, and might therefore carry in 1939,
with no further strain, the import excess which was
successfully carried in 1938. The sweeping deduction
with regard to Japen's vulnerability to economic re-
taliation consequently does not precisely follow from
the suppositions, unless the analysis be descriptive
of the condition to be expected at the end of 1939,
not at the beginning of l939; or unless effective
blocking of sale abroad of Japan's gold be contemplated.
The analysis furthermore overlooks the necessity of
estimating what adjustments Japan might make toward
meeting such economic retaliation by further contraction
of domestic consumption and by intensifying Japanes•
reliance both on those parts of the Asiatic continent
under Japanese military occupation and on the countries
not participating in the retaliatory measures.

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
- 34 -
VIII.
It has been the effort of this despatoh to indicate
the sources of the factual material presented, in order
that the Department may directly consider sucl material
along with other data on which the Department's judgments
are based. It may perhaps not be out of place to present
also a brief statement of the Embassy's opinion.
Is there statistical proof that economic sanctions
would cause the collapse of Japan? Our opinion is that
there is not. Whether collapse might be highly probable,
or whether Japan would probably suffer loss of her position
as a power, or whether Japan's power would thereby be
curbed to a degree estopping her from becoming an inter-
national militery threat, are se arate questions not
answered by our fundamental opinion that there is no
statistical proof that economic sanctions would cause
the collapse of Japan.
We can with assurance venture the opinion that
sanctions would be met by Japan with a reaction comparable
to the reaction which war would provoke. We do not mean
to insist that a declaration of war would be Japan's
response, but it is our belief that the Japanese state
of mind would become a war state of mind. And there
hangs over all estimates one psychological faotor which
must remain an unknown quantity until tested, and that
is the capacity of the Japanese people to respond to the
emergenoy.
Japan,

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.96)
- 35 -
Japan, in the Embassy's view, can not be defleoted
from the objectives whioh are fundamental to her policy
by misoellaneous economic reprisals thrown in as in-
cidentals to British, American, and French trade pro-
tection. Sanctions against Japan might well require a
thoroughness and sacrifice which American interests
might not adequately support, in any state of mind short
of war.
The question of attempting sanctions remains one of
the broadest complexity and can not easily and automatically
be answered by assurance of quick success. The decision,
in our opinion, should be regarded as of importance equal
to a decision of war, and equally with a decision of war
should be arrived at only after full veight has been given
to considerations arising from relations in other spheres.
Meantime I agree with my British colleague that there is
an obvious value in keeping Japen in doubt.
Respectfully yours,
porifrm
Joseph C. Grew.

It is an undeniable fact that in close concert with each
other, the Governments of Great ritain, the United States,
and France have of late come to take positive actions against
Japan regardin her policy for di posal of the China Inci-
dent, even though opinions may differ as to the limit of
such actions and the effect to be produced by them. This
has been openly admitted even by the British and the United
States Governments themselves. For instance, the Jashington
Government ranted some time ago a credit for 25, 000,000
to the Chiang Kai-shek regime, against the provisions of the
Neutrality Act which prohibit the American citizens to
extend credits to any belligerent nations unless with special
permission of the President. It stands to reason, therefore,
that in giving the presidential permission to the American
loan to the Chiang regime on the basis of the recognition
of absence of belligerent relations between Japan and China,
the United States Government executed in part its systema-
tic policy against Japan, which should not be viewed in the
li ht of business transactions.
It is easy to see that pressure enforced by these Powers on
Japan will assume the two forms - direct pressure and indirect
pressure through assistance extended to the Chiang Kai-shek
regime. But these two forms of pressure may be further
classified into the two kinds of pressure, armed and economic.

in Australia. These suggest that within the present fiscal

Although some two-th irds of the materitls necessary for this
country is sunplied by these two countries, it is practically

As for the Japanese export trade, it is frecuently reported

past six years. Compared with Japan, China does not count
very much for the United States, whose investments in China
amounts only to 5o0 million doliars and whose Chinese trade
represents only 3 per cent of her total trade.
To conclude this brief survey, it may be pointed out that
pressure so far brought by Britain and the United States

though its existence cannot be denied. How, then, it will
develop is a question yet to be answered, but among many
factors which will determine its future development, the
following must be first taken into account:
(1) In proportion to the consolidation of the Rome-Berlin-

friendly relationship;

Tm Britain and America Exercise
uccessful Economic Pressurs
on Japan?
By Ichiro Zoshi
(From the Hanashi, March, 1939)
Britain end America Beginnin to Excrcisc
Economic Pressure on Jaoun
Keeping pace with the development of the Chin incident
and the conmencement of Japan's activities for the ccononic
construction of the Asiatic Continent in a regular Wey, Englend
and Amerioa are beginning to assume a more ond more blunt manner
in their economic pressure on our country.
As the aren under the occupation of the Japanese troops
is steadily extended to all regions where Britain and Aneric.
have their rights and intcrests, Jap n's policy for the econo-
mic co fuction of China and the position of those westcrn ooun-
tries in China have becomo inconsistent. When Hankow nd Canton
fell into the hands of the Japenese army, they thought that Japen's
military operatians in Chinc were brought to an end, and Americ&,
Britain, end France formally recuested tho Tokyo Governmert to
reopen the Yangtzekiang to forcign vessels, obviously with a view
to asoortaining Japan's attitude towards the rights and intercsts
established by other nations in China, and they seem to hove
arrived at the conclusion that Japan is determined to, shut all
other nations out or China, just es she had done in Manchoukuo.

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
(2)
The statement 1ssued by the ex-Premier Konoe on November,
1938, and various mensures taken by the Japanese Government soon
after the issue of Konoe's statement made it clear to all other
nations that Japan had decided to follow a policy towards Chine,
whioh, they understood, would destroy the trade nd cther econo-
mio interests the western nations were enioying in China. There
upon, thoy abandoned the prudent policy they had been following,
and bogan to deal with our country in c blunt and positive manner.
Among the others, England and Amerioe commenced to give pr ctical
support to Chiang Kai-shek's regime on the one hand, and take a
retariative policy sgainst our country on the other hand.
It seems to be their belief that they can give a most effec-
tive brow on Japan through eeomomic pressure. For instance, the
Finanoial News of London (January 6) said in its editorinl that
Britain should give nore economic help to Chiang Kai-shek in co-
operation with America, stating in conolusion "At present, Japan
is muffering not so muoh from want of cepital, as from want of
markets. England is now suffering from the excess of inports,
Amerioa boing in a similar condition. There are no rensons why
England and Amerioa should suffer from the suspension of the im-
port trade from Japan." The paper is apparently suggesting an
anti-Japanese boyoott.
From the beginning of the present China incident, England
 been standing in our way from a selfish and caloulating point

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.98)
(3)
of viow, while Americc has been sympathising with Chine from
sentimontal feelings. Conseouently, the cnti-Jeapanese policy of
Amorica has always been very milder then th t of England. The
London Government, however, hove finally succeeded in inducing
America to act agains t our country in cco eration vith England.
"Blood is always thicker thn w.ter, "soys en' Occidental proverb.
America has begun to play an actunl role in the econouic pressure
planned by England, probably beh ving herself from a stond
point of a demoratio nation against a totalitarian state, as
Japan appears to be in the eyes of the American citizens. The
finanoial help they are giving to Chiang Kai-shek may be regard-
ed as a passive anti-Japanese measure, while the boycott against
the Japanese imports may be interpreted for their  ositive
anti-Japanese policy.
How Are They Helping China?
Extension of economio help to China is at once a passive
anti-Japanese mecsure. We'shall investigate into the way by
whioh England and America are helping Chiang's Government at pre-
sont.
England believes that Japan shall never succeed in conquer-
ing China by arms, that sooner or later Japan will reveal her
financial weakness. From belief like this, England i: now doing
her best to help China construot a route of traffic nd communi-
sation oonneoting the interior of China with Occiden tal count-

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.98)
(4)
rio via Burma end Yunnan, which lies beyond the reach of the
Japanese troops even after the complete c pture of the existing
Chinese railways. Indeed, the greater  ortion of the British and
merioan loans to China is to be exnended for the construction
of a route of traffic, that will keep Chin? in direct touch with
those western countries. Englond is  ctually l nning the ex-
tension of the Yunnan-Burma route as far as Sing  ore, thereby
connecting Chinn with foreign countries. And it is for the con-
struotion of the Yunnan-Burma railwey (500 kilometers), that
the London Government has decided on a flo,0o0,c0o loan to China.
Almost as the establishment of the pro-Chinese credit stated
above, the American Government decided (on December 15, 1938)
on a 25,000,000 loen to Chiang Kaishek's regime to be expended
for the import of auto-trucks from America. As securities for
the credit, the Kuomintang regime offered Chinese silvers broad
and oil-papers produced in Chinc. Chen Kuang-fu, who was directly
responsible for the conclusion of the agreement regardine the
25,000,000 loan from America, said in explanation of the commer-
oial nature of the loan that the auto-trucks Chine would import
from Amerioa by means of the credit just ostablished were intend-
ed for the maintenance of traffic on the new commercial route
now under oonstruotion, and the gasoline to be purchased from
America was also intended for those commeroial auto-trucks. Be
that as it may, there is no question about the fact that those

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.99)
15)
loans by England and Amerioc will strengthen China's fighting
power. It is interesting to note that, while thus helping China,
those two countries are never losin sight of their own interest;
they are selling their own goods to China.
The Kuomingtang Government has already placed big orders
with some railway factories of England, and  is expected to
shortly order larger quantities of rolling stock and other rail-
way materials. It is said that a company in Leeds City has re-
oieved an order for 85o freight cars of steel and 25 miles of
rails with all accessories. Besides, there is no doubt that
Amerioa and England are shipping large quantities of arms and
ammunition to Chine. We have resons to believe that America and
England will extend larger and more active help to Chiang Kai-
shek's Government.
Various Retaliative Measures
Under Contemplation
We hear that America and England are now contemplating vari-
ous retaliative measure against Japan. The anti-Japanese feel-
ings entertained by the British people in this connection are
represented by the Manchester Guardian in one the recent issues.
It said in its editorial:
"England and Amerioa can teke an effective retaliative
measure against Japan by meting out a disoriminative treatment
to the Japanese imports. Or course, we have to expect strong
opposition from Japan as soon as we take such discriminative

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.98)
(6)
trade policy against Japan. But, the Japenese papers cre al-
roady regarding Englend as the recl enemy of Japan, or as a
nation that is supporting Chinc from behind her back. Thus,
England is already attacked as a real enemy of Japin, and it is
of no consequence whatsoever whether we reelly deserve such
attack or not."
The Menchester Guardian is suggesting the ado tion of a dis-
oriminative trade policy cgeinst our country. The British nation
believes that Japan will experience greater difficulty thro ugh
the loss of markets than through the loss of money. Not only the
British nation, but also the American people are of the some opi-
nion, beoause mcst of the retaliative mecsures suggested in veri-
ous quarters of the two countries partake of the nnture of
anti-Japanese boyootts. For instance, Lord Eriebank of the
House of Lords, stated recently that Britain shoul chrogate the
Anglo-Japanese Comnercial Trecty and close all the British ports
against Japanese steamers. In the meantime, Mr. Pitmon of
Amerioa, speaking in the House of Senaters, urged the necessity
of immediate suspension of all trede relations between Jopn and
Amerioa.
Thus, England and Amerion are actually considering the with-
drawal of the most-favoured-netion-treatment from our country,
ereotion of a prohibitive tariff against Japanese ilports, en-
couragement of anti-Japanese boyootts, and, in the case of Americe.

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.98)
(7)
a rovision of the Amerioan Law of Neutrality in favour of China.
Concerning the British memorandum filled with the Japenese
Govornment on January 14 this year, the representatives of the

Very Confidential
Your telegram o. 35 to ashington.
1. In your instructions to Sir R. Lindsay Your
Lordship appears to refer to the protection of specific
trade interests in China as the principal object of counter-
measures. As pointed out in recent tele rans, however,
I feel that the main issue is now fer wider and far more
important, since whet is at stalte is not only our commercial
investment in China but the vhole political and economic
future of countries with interests in the racific, to say
nothing of the urgency of ensurin the observance of treaty
obligations as & matter of principle. If what we are striving
for is to uphold a principle and avert a future denger we
are justified in takin reater risks in terms of short-run
disadvantage than if our aim were merely to preserve existing
interests inteot.
The question at issue seems to me to be the prevention
of the complete establishment of a totalitarian power in
East Asia, with aims similar to those of ermany and Italy,
which is already on the way to becoming a menace to all
countries with interests in East Asia and the Fecific Ooean.
By supplying Japan with all thet she has needed in the past,
the British Empire and the United States have already gone
far

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
far towards buildin up this menace, and if they
continue from now on to allow Japan to obtain what
she wants they will soon have completed the process.
2. If any chane in the British and American
attitude is to be effective, it should be made cuickly,
and the most effective change would be to begin at once
to reduce purchases of Japanese merchandise and cold.
I and my advisers regard present time as opportune for
more resolute action. Jith steady increase in Japan's
imports from China and Manchukuo and possible temporary
easing of tension with Russia, we cannot feel sure that
in a few months situation will not have become less
favourable from our point of view. I heve  ood reason to
believe that recent dampin down here of fishery dispute
with Soviet Russia is not unconnected with firmer attitude
of Great Britain and United States.
3. As to the conclusions reached by is Mejesty's
Government in ovember 1937, I do not think that all of
them now hold good. As regards (b) of paragraph 3 in your
telerem under reference, I em of opinion thet even without
a state of war and even if entrepôt trade were permitted
(which would be most unfortunate), Jepan could not acquire
from alternative sources sufficient supplies of some
essential materials such as oil, cotton, aluminium, tin,
zinc, niokel, rubber. Dut I do not understand why entrepôt
trade, even if not stopped entirely, could not be so
diminished as to have a paralysing effect in respect of
such materials, especially where they can be controlled at
the souroe of supply.
In

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
In (c) it is stated with regerd to an embargo
upon Japan's export trade that no decisive effect
could be expected for a considerable period; but this
neglects the strong probability thet the anticipation
by Japan of increasin pressure upon her would cause
her to moderate her policy long before pressure reached
its maximum effect.
This consideration applies to any
simgle form of pressure and a fortiori to various forms
of pressure in combination. I have never supposed that
any single counter-measure would, before becoming fully
operative, have an imnediate disastrous effect upon
Japan's economy, but I do consider that the application
of a wide-spread enbargo upon Jepan's exports would set
in motion a process that would rapidly prove disastrous.
Point (a). The effect on Japanese shipping would be
far greater than the effect of Japanese retaliation on
British and United States shipping. We submit that while
refusal of facilities to Japanese shippinc in all ritish
and United States ports would seriously interfere with
Japanese shippiny operations in most perts of the world and
consequently with Japanese vworld trede, retaliation in kind
by Jepan would affect the operation of Sritish shipping
only in Japanese controlled waters.
Point (e). Participation of the countries indicated
would prevent Japan from accuiring the necessary amounts
of raw materials (see also paragraph 3 above).
As regards (f), Canada is also concerned because of
her metal exports to Japan. Without endangering her own
economy and her continental policy, Japan is not now in
a position to retaliate by making any further considerable
reduction in her imports from British territories. In any
case our object in stopping Jepanese exports would be to
prevent

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
prevent Japan from financing her essential imports.
Paragraph 9. An element of risk is of course
inseparable from any action of this kind but my
considered opinion is that risk of war is slight if matter
is properly handled. Such risk as there is arises from
possible irresponsible action by younger officers and
reactionary elements rather than from any deliberate aot
by Japanese Government and then only if matter is mishandled
(e.g. by ill-timed publicity). Present Japanese policy is
based on the assumption, to vhich they still hold, that in
no circumstances will Great ritain and the United States
be able to take joint or parallel action in this matter.
If such action should finally be decided upon problem will
be to convince the Japanese Governnent of this without
making any overt or public threat. Everything in my mind
would depend on whether such conviction could be carried
in time to the Jepenese official - and particularly the
military - mind. Vith the situation such as it is the
saving of "face" is an all-important factor. Subject to
above comments I entirely agree with Your Lordship's view
that a policy of counter-mensures should not be embarked
upon unless we ere prepared, in the last resort, to pursue
it to the end.
CRAIGIE

AMERICAN COTTON IN JAPAN
Cotton manufecture constitutes Japan's premier
industry, but having no domestic raw material supply
the indnstry, centered et Osaka, must inport ell of
its raw cotton from abroad.
Aided by low wages and modernenufecturing and
distributing facilitles Japanese mills made rapid progress,
until a few years ago they wrested world supremacy from
Britains Lancushire ootton industry. In the cotton year
ended ugust 31, 1936 the Japunese cotton industry import-

1936 to August 31, 1937) a record tote1 of 4,442,194 bales
wore imported, 35% of this total beine of american origin.
In the first ten months of the present ootton year, i.e.,
September 1, 1937 to June 30, 1936, imports amounted to
only 1,877,845 beles, the .merican proportion of the total
being 34%; in other words, raw cotton imports this year
will be less than halr as large as last year. The reason
for this is the alarming slump in exports.
For each of the years 1936 and 1937 Japan had to
find foreign exchange to the tune of roughly $200,000,000
to finanee raw ootton imports, but all of this, and
more, was recouped from the flourishing export trade

and in addition, a sizeable amount of cotton remaimed

CONCLUSION
Regardless of now or when the sino-Japanese con-
fliet ends, it seems certain thet Japan will be
burdened with enornous debts. laewise, t seems
certain that the standard of living will be even
lower than at present.
There will be lsss purchasing of foreign gooas
(except possibly from the Yen bloo areas of Asia)
because there will be less money to pay for these
goods and also because home industries have been
markedly expanded and will be able to produce a
larger share of home market requirements.
Already the public is being prepared ror lower
living standards. They are being oonstantly admonishod
to economize in all directions; some goods they must
do without entirely, and in other cases they must
aocept inferior substitutes. They have already been
told by responsible officials that such messures as
fuel rationing and the restriotions on the consump-
tion of cotton are to be retained even after the
termination of the China venture.
Domestio industries which can be tied-in in any
way with the military program have been feverishly
expanded, often with subsidies or other forms of
r1s001

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
- 37 -
fiscal encouregenent. In addition, many of them
have greetly benerited by the virtuel elimination of
foreign competition resulting from the operetion of
the import controls, ie., the banning of meny products
and the drastic curteilment of others. Key industries
suoh as mining, steel, mechine toole, vetroleum,
staple fiber pulp end chemicals have & larger produo-
tive oapacity than at any previous time and some of
these industries have letely begun to develop synthetio
products, inoluding rubber end licuid fuel. Not all
of the industrial expension seen here appeers to have
e sound raison d'etre; nevertheless, es much cecitel,
including considerable government money, hes gone
into suoh developnent it seems reasonable to entici-
pate that the investment will receive adeguate proteo-
tion and encouragement even after the li uidation of
the China venture.
From the present indioations it seems certain that
Japan will continue to dominate "Manchoukuo" and may
ovem ond up with a large part of China under its
control. Already "kanohoukuo" has begun to oontribute
to Japan's rew material needs and ambitious plans
have been worked out for developing the agrioultural,
mimeral and industrial resouroes of North China. This
means heavy eapital investment, and because funds for
this purpose promise to be very searoe, headway may

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.95)
- 38 -
be painfully slow. Neverthelese, whatever eventually
comes out of that aree will further reduce Japan's
dependenee upon other sources of supply--end will
be paid for in Yen.
The oombinetion of factors here discussed does
not augur well for American oommercial interests here.
In fect, one is very much tempted to believe that the
years 1938 and 1937 represented a peak in our trade
to this oountry whioh may not be resched again--at any
rate, not for & long time.

The difrioulties of the Hiranuma Cabinet were
greatly inereased by the failure of the British nego-
tiations, and it nad real y fallen before the Germen
blow eame. The kmperor had been much displeased with
the failure to reach an understanding with the British,
and gave a strong rebuke when the army ofricers returned
to Tientsin. This roquired a ohange n the coutacts be-
tween the Amperor and the War Office. So far as can
be seen on the outside, the army did as it pleased; but
it lost prestige in the inner ciroles. The change of
cabinet wms delayed because the Navy for some time re-
fused to accept a general as premier. The new cabinet
is weaker thaln the old, and is widely expected to last
only a short time until a new o urse can reelly be set.
Howover, the Army representetion is really unchenged,
since Hata 1s e junior member of the same old group of
Sugiyame, Itagaki (no Cniei of taff) and Koiso. There
is also the possibility of adaptation by partial change
rather than by oollective resignation.
There is general : reement to co centr te on "olear-
ing up the China Inoident", althougln one army element
still looks loigingly to the possibility of combining
with Germany against either Russie or Britain. But
"conoentration" cover. many lines of policy. some en-
vi sage suooess through ang Chin -wei; others through guick
and powerful militery moves not disoussed in detail;
others through maintaining the present lin s nd waiting
for the exhaustion of Chungkin. ne important el ement
holds to the posi tion that Ch ina's resistanoe is main-
tained only by Russien enu Britiar sup ort, and th re-
fore the elimin tion or reduction or the foreign aia is
the real way to settle China.  specific sugestion is
that advantage be taken of ngl nd's difii cul tie s to
olose the Hongkong and Indo-China entrances, a proeram
In which army and navy might cooperete m re heartily then
in mome. Althoug the prevailing attitude toward America
1s one of careful concer. lest sup lies be interrupted
and the possibility be oponed of seriots pressure for
another Washington Conferenoe in the future, hostility
and resen tment are increasing in spots.
An offioial expert admitted th t there was and would
be no serious attempt to improve the opium situation
while hostilities are prooeeding, because the militery
informed him that there wes no other real source of
revonue for he pup et governments (an unexpected con-
firmetion of what has w en inferred in Peking and Nanking.
but has been difficult to prove).
Very few persons indeed have any thought that there
might be ultimate failure for Japan in China. The
farthest they go is to some anxiety about costs, methods,
time and international risks. ven critioisms are tan-
gont to the assumption of assured success, and the tiny
minority who see the great evil think only of palliation.

GENERAL ECCNOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS *
The outbreak of European hostilities, following upon
o the series of disturbing incidents to Japan that occurred
in July and August greatly augmented the already numerous
094.5
and perplexing problems facing the Government, business and
finance. An immediate reaction to the war was the staging
of a very enthusiastic demonstration in the Tokyo Stock
Exchange, where prices soared on September 4 by from Yen 1.00
to Yen 26.00 with shipping shares in the lead followed by
fibrous groups, other export industries and shipbuilding
issues. Commodity prices also firmed immediately.
The abnormal rises in shares and goods caused grave
concern in official quarters to the extent that stens wire
at once taken to counteract a dangerous price inflaticn.
Warnings
* Prepared with the assistance of the Office of the Commerciel
Attaché.

=== Page 538 === (confidence 0.98)
-23 -
Warnings were issued to stock uxchanges, banks were in-
structed not to extend loans for speculation purposus, n,
stricter economic control mousures were formulated. To
cushion the impact of war dov lopnents the Cabinet on
September 15 ondorsed  n wonomic policy proviiin
radical revisions in the mat riale mobilizution und pre-
ductive capacity xpansion plans, trade programs and
financial mobilization, with dual aims of strenthening
the defense and economic struetures and of establis!ing
a self-surricient economie bioe er Japan, "Manehukue" and
China.
The surge of initial enthusiasm at the beginning of
the month soon fell off as it began to be realized that
Japan wes not in as good a position as it was first sup-
posed to take advantare of the opportunities offered by
the war for a business boom. In taking stock of its
position there were fcund adv rse fa:tors as well as
favorable ones to be considered.
The factors favorable for an increase in Jaran's foreign
trade as a result of the European hostilities were considered
to be (1) the orportunity for Japan tc increase its sales
abroad of agricultural, marine and manufactured products
by taking up the markets formerly held by the belligerent
Powers and by even increasing sales of those products to
the belligerent Powers themselves; (2) the opportunity
to realize much higher prices for Japanese exnorts through
the general increase in world prices; and (3) the oppor-
tunity to make large profits from its shipping industry
through the withdrawal of competition of much of the shipping
of the belligerent Powers and through the inreased demand
in world commerce for Japanese ships.
As

=== Page 539 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
As an offset, however: to these favoratle factors it
was reportedly realized that a rumber of rather fornidable
obstacles had presented themselves. In the first piace i
became aprarent thut higher basic prices of fereign indus-
trial raw materials, the export embargoes alrendy imnosed by
some producing countries on those raw materieis nd the
probability of export bans by others on such essential
materials as oil, copper, aluminum, nickel, iron ores,
special steels, and chemicals, would sericusly impair
Japan's chances of obtaining the necessary materials where-
from it could roduce the manufactured products to supply
the markets with their demands. Secondly, it was realized
that a number of foreign countries directly or indirectly
involved in the hostilities would plac: import embargces
on non-essentials, many of which Japan was best equipped
to supply. Tnirdly, the decline in foreign exchange rates,
higher basic prices abroad, increased freight rates and war
risk insurance premiums had already substantially raised
yen prices of imported materials which must be reflected in
higher prices of export goods. Fourthly, it was stated that
ducts, would have extensively to expand her productive
facilities and in order to do this she would require large
imports of raw materials, semi-manufactured heavy equip-
ment and much machinery, all of which would be very difficult
to obtain under war conditions, particularly so as many of
the countries which
sunplied the raw materials and the
equipment would no
longer be in a position to sell such
to Japan. Lastly, in regard to shipping, Japan's own needs,
especially in relation with the yen-bloc countries, would
preclude its being able to make available many ships to
take

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.96)
-25-
take advantage of the war-time possibilities and it was
doubtful whether she could expand her shipping fast enoug
in time to profit from the absence of competition fron
other countries. There wes one other consideratien thot
was also notd n ti n' rrtr r 1
1
the United states fer many prouuets whieh had pr visty

increase her purchases Prom An ricu to any greut xtent she
would have to do so on a credit or loan basis. These ccn-
ditions do not, however, apply at the present and there
may be unsurmountable difficulties in realizing them.
Many of the measures proposed by the Governnent to
meet the war-time cenditions were implemented during the
month under review. Besides the promulgation of Articles
6, 1l and 19 cf th? National Mobilization Law mentioned in
the foregoing section of this report, another ordinance was
enforced which regulates exports to the yen-bloc ar as of
a wide range of articles including machinery, lumber,
textiles and such food items as starch, vegetables, fruit,
meat, fish, oils and tea. Restrictions have also been en-
forced on exports of machinery and metals to all markets.
Beginning October lst the allotment of coal to industries
consuming large quantities of that fuel, particularly peace-
time industries such as spinning and textiles, were to be
cut by from 10 to 20 per cent. A general petroleum sales
company was to be established to control distribution of
petroleum products throughout the country. It was reported
that the Government was considering another check to commodit
prices in the form of proposed export duties on such products
as

=== Page 541 === (confidence 0.99)
- 26 -
as raw silk and canned foods. It was announced that the
Ministry of Commerce had decided to operate a wartime
marine insurance compensation system through a wartime
re-insurance pool to b organized by marine insurance
companies. It was also announced that cocoons were to
be exempted from
maximum price rates provided for under
the provisions of articles 6, 11 and 19 of the
National
Mobilization Law in order to permit the silk industry to
gain the greatest benefits from the increase in the nrice
of silk abroad.
According to authoritetive reports wholesale commodity
prices in Japan rose by approximately 5 per cent and retail
prices by approximately 4.5 per cent in September over those
prevailing in August.
At the same time the price of 18
principal goods imported incroased
by 5.3 per cent and the
price of 11 principal goods exported by 12.8 per cent.
There was recorded an exceptionally high jumn in the price
of raw silk from the end of August to the end of September.
During that period the verage rise was cround Yen 4o0 from
the spot prices on August 30 of Yen 1275 per bale.
Money rates, despite the intensified business activity
during the month under ruview, showed little change. How-
ever, th: Bank of Japen's note issue markedly increased.
After the yen had travelled up and down with the fluctua-
tions in the exchange rate of the pound, going at one time
down to U.S. 21-3/16 on Septomber 29, its link with storling
was finally dissolved ane the yen pegged to the United
States dollar at the rate of Yen 1 = 23.44 cents.
Equinoxial storms in September alleviated to some
extent the water shortage in Jepan, particularly in the
central and northern parts but in western Japan insufficient
rain fell to break the drought there which had assumed
fairly
